Asymptotics of Symmetric Polynomials: A Dynamical Point of view
Alice Guionnet, Jiaoyang Huang

Abstract
In this paper we study the asymptotic behavior of the (skew) Macdonald and Jack symmetric polynomials as the number of variables grows to infinity. We characterize their limits in terms of certain variational problems. As an intermediate step, we establish a large deviation principle for the θ \theta analogue of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks. When θ = 1 \theta=1 , these walks are equivalent to random Lozenges tilings of strip domains, where the variational principle (with general domains and boundary conditions) has been proven in the seminal work [ 20 ] by Cohn, Kenyon, and Propp. Our result gives a new argument of this variational principle, and also extends it to non-intersecting θ \theta -Bernoulli random walks for any θ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) \theta\in(0,\infty) . Remarkably, the rate functions remain identical, differing only by a factor of 1 / θ 1/\theta .



1
Introduction

Macdonald symmetric functions, initially discovered by Macdonald in the late 1980s
[
46
]
, are central to a number of key developments in mathematics and mathematical physics. They are indexed by Young diagrams and implicitly depend on two parameters
q,t\in(0,1)
. Their degeneracy, by taking
t=q^{\theta}
and sending
q
to approach one, gives the Jack polynomials. This family of symmetric functions depends on the positive parameter
\theta>0
, and was first introduced in
[
40
,
39
]
. Notably, when
\theta=1
, the Jack polynomials coincide with the well-known Schur polynomials, up to some multiplicative constants.

These symmetric polynomials appear in algebraic combinatorics as generating functions, and provide a systematic way to enumerate combinatorial objects. In representation theory, these symmetric polynomials offer a crucial bridge between algebraic structures, combinatorial objects and group representations. Recently, symmetric polynomials have become central tools in the development of integrable stochastic models. This theory has found numerous applications in areas such as random partitions, random matrix theory, and directed polymers. In this article, we study the asymptotic behaviors of symmetric polynomials, namely (skew) Macdonald and Jack polynomials, as the number of parameters goes to infinity. In this introduction, we discuss the results for skew Jack polynomials. The results on skew Macdonald polynomials are more involved and are presented in
Section

1.4
. Recall that skew Jack polynomials
J_{{\bm{\lambda}}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}}({\bm{x}};\theta)
are symmetric functions in infinitely many variables
{\bm{x}}=(x_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\infty}
and parametrized by two Young diagrams
\bm{\mu}\subset{\bm{\lambda}}
, where
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{1}\geqslant\mu_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\mu_{\ell({\bm{\mu}})}),{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{\ell({\bm{\lambda}})})
are arrays of integer numbers with length
\ell({\bm{\mu}}),\ell({\bm{\lambda}})
respectively. We refer to
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.2
for more detailed discussion on (skew) Jack polynomials. We consider the limit of skew Jack polynomials along a sequence of Young diagrams
{\bm{\lambda}}^{(N)},{\bm{\mu}}^{(N)}
and parameters
{\bm{b}}^{(N)}
, where the column and row sizes of Young diagrams and the number of parameters grow linearly in
N
.
We show the limit
N^{-2}\ln J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{(N)}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}^{(N)}}({\bm{b}}^{(N)};\theta)
exists and give an explicit characterization of the limit in terms of a variational problem.

1.1
Nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli Walk Ensembles

Understanding the asymptotic behavior of symmetric polynomials is fundamental to modern combinatorics, statistical mechanics and representation theory. However, there are few results in this direction, as symmetric polynomials tend to have complicated structures and thus are not easily approachable by algebraic combinatorics techniques. In this article, we will study the asymptotic behaviors of symmetric polynomials via a dynamical approach.

Many symmetric polynomials arise naturally as the partition functions of interacting particle systems. Some notable examples are

1.

The partition function of nonintersecting Brownian bridges is given by the Harish-Chandra-Itzykson-Zuber integral formula
[
29
]
. It is closely related with Schur polynomials.

2.

Macdonald processes as introduced by Borodin and Corwin
[
8
]
, by definition have Macdonald polynomials as their partition functions. Degenerations of Macdonald processes include the Schur processes
[
54
,
52
]
, Jack processes
[
28
,
37
]
, Hall-Littlewood processes
[
7
]
and Whittaker processes
[
8
]
.

3.

Partition functions of vertex models give families of symmetric rational functions, which generalize Schur symmetric polynomials, as well as some of their variations
[
1
,
2
,
6
,
11
]
.

On one hand, the asymptotic behavior of symmetric polynomials provides a valuable tool for characterizing the dynamics of interacting particle systems, where these polynomials serve as partition functions. On the other hand, this also offers an alternative approach to investigating the asymptotic behaviors of symmetric polynomials by exploring the asymptotic behaviors of the associated interacting particle systems using dynamical approaches.

Fix large
N
, and take Young diagrams
\bm{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}),{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{1}\geqslant\mu_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\mu_{N})
, and parameters
{\bm{b}}=(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})
.
The skew Jack symmetric polynomials
J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}}({\bm{b}};\theta^{-1})
(where
{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime},{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}
are the transposes of
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
), can be interpreted as the partition functions of
N
-particle nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks. For any Young diagrams
{\bm{\nu}}=(\nu_{1}\geqslant\nu_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\nu_{N})
with at most
N
rows, we encode it by a particle configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\bm{\nu}})=({\mathsf{x}}_{1},{\mathsf{x}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathsf{x}}_{N})
, where

\displaystyle{\mathsf{x}}_{i}=\nu_{i}-(i-1)\theta,\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N.

(1.1)

From the construction, this particle configuration lives on the following
\theta
-dependent lattice

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigl\{({\mathsf{x}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{x}}_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\mid{\mathsf{x}}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z},\quad{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}\in\theta+\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},\quad i=1,2,\dots,N-1\bigr\}.

(1.2)

Definition 1.1

(non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk ensembles)
.

An
n
-particle non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk from time
0
to time
{\mathsf{S}}
is a sequence of particle configurations
\mathsf{p}=\big(\bm{\mathsf{x}}(0),\bm{\mathsf{x}}(1),\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{{\mathsf{x}}}}({\mathsf{S}})\big)\in(\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta})^{{\mathsf{S}}}
such that
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})=({\mathsf{x}}_{1}({\mathsf{t}}),{\mathsf{x}}_{2}({\mathsf{t}}),\cdots,{\mathsf{x}}_{n}({\mathsf{t}}))
, and
\mathsf{x}_{i}({\mathsf{t}}+1)-\mathsf{x}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\in\{0,1\}
for each
{\mathsf{t}}\in[0,{\mathsf{S}}]
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
; viewing
\{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{S}}}
as the space-time trajectory for the
i
-th particle, which may either jump to the right or not move at each step. From the construction of the lattice (
1.2
), the paths are non-intersecting, since we have
\mathsf{x}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})>\mathsf{x}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})
whenever
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
and
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{S}}
.

Assume
{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\lambda}}
are Young diagrams with at most
N
rows. We identify them as particle configurations
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}=({\mathsf{y}}_{1}>{\mathsf{y}}_{2}>\cdots>{\mathsf{y}}_{N}),\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}=({\mathsf{z}}_{1}>{\mathsf{z}}_{2}>\cdots>{\mathsf{z}}_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}

{\mathsf{y}}_{i}=\mu_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad{\mathsf{z}}_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N.

(1.3)

We denote the set of non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}=({\mathsf{y}}_{1}>{\mathsf{y}}_{2}>\cdots>{\mathsf{y}}_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}=({\mathsf{z}}_{1}>{\mathsf{z}}_{2}>\cdots>{\mathsf{z}}_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}};{\mathsf{T}})=\{{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in(\mathbb{W}^{N}_{\theta})^{{\mathsf{T}}}:\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}},\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{T}})=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}\}.

(1.4)

The set
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}};{\mathsf{T}})
is nonempty if there exists a non-intersecting Bernoulli walk
\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
from
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{T}})=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}
, which is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\mathsf{y}}_{i}\leqslant{\mathsf{z}}_{i}\leqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{i}+{\mathsf{T}},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N.

(1.5)

Indeed, if (
1.5
) holds we can take

\displaystyle{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})=\max\{{\mathsf{y}}_{i},{\mathsf{z}}_{i}-({\mathsf{T}}-{\mathsf{t}})\},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N.

Then we can rewrite the skew Jack polynomial
J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}/{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}}(\cdot;\theta^{-1})
evaluated at
(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})
, in terms of the weights (
1.7
) as follows

\displaystyle J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}/{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}}(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1};\theta^{-1})=\frac{J_{{\bm{\mu}}}(1^{N};\theta)}{J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1^{N};\theta)}\sum_{{\mathsf{p}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}};\bm{{\mathsf{z}}};{\mathsf{T}})}{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}}),

(1.6)

where
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}
are the transposes of
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
, and for non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk
{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
and
{\bm{b}}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})
, we define their weights as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}})=\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})},

(1.7)

where
{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}})=(e_{1}({\mathsf{t}}),e_{2}({\mathsf{t}}),\cdots,e_{N}({\mathsf{t}}))
is the increment at time
{\mathsf{t}}
,

\displaystyle e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})={\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}}+1)-{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\in\{0,1\},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N.

The formula (
1.6
) is derived from the construction of the Jack process, as explained in more detail in
Section

6
. On the left-hand side of (
1.6
), there are explicit formulas for the Jack symmetric polynomials evaluated at
1^{N}
, i.e.
J_{\bm{\mu}}(1^{N};\theta),J_{\bm{\lambda}}(1^{N};\theta)
, making them amenable for asymptotic analysis. The primary challenge lies in analyzing the sum of the weights
{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}})
. In the special case where
b_{i}=1
, this sum of weights can be interpreted as the partition function of non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}
, with the transition probability given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}|\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+\theta e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}},

(1.8)

for
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
, where
V
is the Vandermonde determinant, and
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, see
Figure

1
.
We remark that the above transition probability is given by pairwise interactions, and the interactions between adjacent particles are singular to prevent colliding. More precisely, from the above transition probability, if
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}
and
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}\geqslant\theta+1
, then
({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}+e_{i+1})\in\theta+\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. If
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}=\theta
, then (
1.8
) is nonzero only if
(e_{i},e_{i+1})\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,1)\}
. In these cases,
({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}+e_{i+1})\in\theta+\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. Therefore the Markov process (
1.8
) stays in the lattice
\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}
.

Figure 1:
Shown above is the transition from the Young diagram
\bm{\mu}=(5,3,2,1,1,1)
to the Young diagram
\bm{\lambda}=(6,4,2,2,1,1)
, which are encoded by the particle systems through the relation (
1.1
).

Our first main result establishes a large deviation principle for the non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks (
1.8
). Notably, when
\theta=1
, these walks reduce to non-intersecting Bernoulli random walks, which is equivalent to random Lozenge tiling of strip domains. Cohn, Kenyon, and Propp
[
20
]
previously proved a variational principle for random tiling models (domino tiling and lozenge tiling) with general boundary conditions. Our result extends this variational principle to non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks for any
\theta\in(0,\infty)
. Remarkably, the rate functions remain identical, differing only by a factor of
1/\theta
. To state the large deviation principle, we need to introduce the notations of height function and surface tension.

1.2
Height Function and Surface Tension

Given any
N
-particle nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk ensemble
{\mathsf{p}}=\big(\bm{\mathsf{x}}(0),\bm{\mathsf{x}}(1),\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{{\mathsf{x}}}}({\mathsf{T}})\big)\in(\mathbb{W}^{N}_{\theta})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
from time
0
to time
{\mathsf{T}}
, we denote the increment at time
{\mathsf{t}}
as
{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}})=(e_{1}({\mathsf{t}}),e_{2}({\mathsf{t}}),\cdots,e_{N}({\mathsf{t}}))
,

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)-\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\in\{0,1\}^{N}.

We denote the rescaled particle configuration (by rescaling space and time by
1/N
),

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\cdots,x_{N}(t))=\frac{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})}{N},\quad t=\frac{{\mathsf{t}}}{N},\quad 0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}.

(1.9)

In the rest of the paper, we will use the scaling above (we will denote
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})
for particle configurations before scaling, and
{\bm{x}}(t)
for after scaling).
For
{\mathsf{t}}/N\leqslant t\leqslant({\mathsf{t}}+1)/N
, we linearly interpolate the particle configurations

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}(t)

\displaystyle=\left({\mathsf{t}}+1-Nt\right){\bm{x}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{t}}}{N}\right)+\left(Nt-{\mathsf{t}}\right){\bm{x}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{t}}+1}{N}\right)={\bm{x}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{t}}}{N}\right)+\left(Nt-{\mathsf{t}}\right)\frac{{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}})}{N}.

We encode the particle densities of
{\bm{x}}(t)
as

\displaystyle\rho(x;{\bm{x}}(t))=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bm{1}(x\in[x_{i}(t),x_{i}(t)+\theta/N]),\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}/N,

(1.10)

and define the associated height function

\displaystyle H(x,t)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\rho(y;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}y,\quad(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,{\mathsf{T}}/N].

(1.11)

We notice that the total mass of
\rho
in (
1.10
) is
\theta
for any
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}/N
, and
H(x,t)
is a non-decreasing function of
x
from
0
to
\theta
. The walk ensemble
{\mathsf{p}}=\big(\bm{\mathsf{x}}(0),\bm{\mathsf{x}}(1),\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{{\mathsf{x}}}}({\mathsf{T}})\big)
, the rescaled particle configuration
{\bm{x}}(t)
can be recovered easily by either the empirical particle density
\varrho(x;{\bm{x}}(t))
, or the height function
H(x,t)
. In the rest of the paper, we will not distinguish them.

Given an
N
-particle nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk ensemble
{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
, we recall its weight
{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}})
from (
1.7
). For
{\bm{b}}=1^{\mathsf{T}}
, we simply denote the weight as
{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}})
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}})={\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};1^{\mathsf{T}})=\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}.

(1.12)

Under the transition probability (
1.8
), the probability of the walk
{\mathsf{p}}
is given by
2^{-N{\mathsf{T}}}{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}})
.
We also define the weight of the height function
H
associated with
{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
as

{\mathcal{W}}(H)={\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}}).

From the construction of the height function (
1.11
),
H(x,t)
is
2
-Lipschitz, and almost surely,

\displaystyle\nabla H(x,t)=(\partial_{x}H(x,t),\partial_{t}H(x,t))\in\{(0,0),(1,0),(1,-1)\}.

To analyze the limits of height functions of these interacting particle systems, it will be useful to introduce continuum analogs of the height functions considered in (
1.11
). So, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}=\big\{(s,t)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}_{<0}:s+t>0\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},

(1.13)

and its closure
\overline{\mathcal{T}}=\big\{(s,t)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}_{\leqslant 0}:s+t\geqslant 0\big\}
. We interpret
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
as the set of possible gradients, also called
slopes
, for a continuum height function. The height functions
H(x,t)
associated with particle configurations (from (
1.11
)) is
2
-Lipschitz and satisfies
\nabla H(x,t)\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
for
(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,{\mathsf{T}}/N]
.

Definition 1.2
.

Fix time
T>0
, and denote
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathbb{R}}\times[0,T]
. We say that a function
H:{\mathfrak{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
admissible
if
H
is
2
-Lipschitz and
\nabla H(u)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
for almost all
u\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. We further say that the boundary height function
h=(h(x,0),h(x,T)):\partial{\mathfrak{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

admits an admissible extension to
{\mathfrak{R}}
if
\Adm(\mathfrak{R};h)
, the set of admissible functions
H:{\mathfrak{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
H|_{{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0,T\}}=h
, is not empty.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
and
(s,t)\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
we denote the
Lobachevsky function

L:\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and the
surface tension

\sigma:\overline{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle L(x)=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\log|2\sin z|\mathrm{d}z;\qquad\sigma(s,t)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\Big(L\big(\pi(1-s)\big)+L(-\pi t)+L\big(\pi(s+t)\big)\Big).

(1.14)

For any admissible height function
H
on
{\mathfrak{R}}
, we further denote the
entropy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(H)=\displaystyle\iint_{\mathfrak{R}}\sigma\big(\nabla H(x,t)\big)\mathrm{d}x{\rm d}t.

(1.15)

The entropy functional (
1.15
) also governs the logarithm of the number of lozenge tilings for a given domain and boundary height function
[
20
]
. The surface tension
\sigma
is strictly concave within the interior of
{\mathcal{T}}
and linear along the boundary of
\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
. As a consequence, the maximizer of the energy functional (
1.15
) possesses what are known as “liquid regions”, where the solution is real analytic, and “frozen regions”, where the solution is piecewise linear. Variational problems associated with (
1.15
), and in more general setting, have been explored in previous studies
[
42
,
60
,
4
]
.

1.3
Large Deviation Principle for non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks

We prove a large deviation for non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks (
1.8
) with given boundary condition at time
0
and time
{\mathsf{T}}
. We make the following assumption on the height profile of the limiting boundary condition.

Definition 1.3
.

Fix any
\theta>0
. We denote by
\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
the set of boundary height functions
h=(h(x,0),h(x,T)):\partial{\mathfrak{R}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
such that

1.

\partial_{x}h(x,0),\partial_{x}h(x,T)
are two compactly supported positive measures with density bounded by
1
and total mass
\theta
.

2.

Moreover,
h=(h(x,0),h(x,T))
admits an admissible extension to
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

We equip
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
with the uniform topology.
First, we remark that since we assumed
H
is Lipschitz, the uniform topology in the spatial variable is equivalent to the weak topology on its derivative, as can be easily checked by integration by parts. Moreover, it is evident that
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
is a compact space. Moreover, if
H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, then for every
t\in[0,T]
,
\partial_{x}H(.,t)
is compactly supported, bounded by one and , because
\nabla H\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
,
\partial_{x}H(.,t)
is a non-negative measure for all time
t
.

Remark 1.4
.

We
remark that
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
depends on
\theta
due to the fact that
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
. We want to stress this dependency because it has important consequences, in particular
H(x,t)

is a non-decreasing function of
x
from
0
to
\theta
for all times
0\leqslant t\leqslant T
. Indeed, since
\partial_{t}H(x,t)
is non-positive for every
x
,
H(x,T)\leqslant H(x,t)\leqslant H(x,0)
. On the other hand,
H(x,T)=h(x,T)
and
H(x,0)=h(x,0)
are equal
to
0
for
x
sufficiently small, and
\theta
for
x
sufficiently large since
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
. Therefore, for every time
t\in[0,T]
,
H(x,t)
is equal
to
0
for
x
sufficiently small, and
\theta
for
x
sufficiently large.

To state our main result, let us precise our running assumption concerning the particle configurations.

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\theta,T>0
and
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
.
Given two sequences of particle configurations
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)})
,

\displaystyle\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}=({\mathsf{y}}_{1}^{(N)}\geqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{2}^{(N)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{N}^{(N)})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N},\quad\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}=({\mathsf{z}}_{1}^{(N)}\geqslant{\mathsf{z}}_{2}^{(N)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant{\mathsf{z}}_{N}^{(N)})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N},\quad N\geqslant 1\,.

we say that
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)},{\mathsf{T}})
are
(h,\theta,T)
-admissible iff

1.

There exists a constant
C>0
,
|{\mathsf{y}}_{i}^{(N)}|,|{\mathsf{z}}_{i}^{(N)}|\leqslant CN
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
.

2.

The empirical density (recall from (
1.10
)) of
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
converges, namely when
N\rightarrow\infty

\displaystyle\varrho(x;\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}/N)\rightarrow\partial_{x}h(x,0),\quad\varrho(x;\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}/N)\rightarrow\partial_{x}h(x,T),

(1.16)

in distribution.

3.

As
N
goes to infinity,
{\mathsf{T}}/N
goes to
T
.

We further assume that for
N
large enough the set of non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks of length
{\mathsf{T}}
from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
is nonempty, namely
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})\neq\emptyset
(see (
1.4
)
).

We remark that a height function
G\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})
is defined on
{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,{\mathsf{T}}/N]
. If
{\mathsf{T}}/N<T
, we can extend
G
to
{\mathfrak{R}}
by setting
G(x,t)=G(x,T)
for
t\geqslant T
. For any
H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, the distance
\|G-H\|_{\infty}=\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{R}}}|G(x,t)-H(x,t)|=\sup_{(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,{\mathsf{T}}/N]}|G(x,t)-H(x,t)|+\OO(|{\mathsf{T}}/N-T|)
, as
H
and
G
are Lipschitz. We can now state the main result of this article concerning the large deviations for the distribution of the height function under the law
\mathbb{P}
defined in (
1.8
).

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\theta,T>0
and
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
. Consider two sequences of particle configurations and time
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)},{\mathsf{T}})
which are
(h,\theta,T)
-admissible, and non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks (
1.8
) starting from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
at time
0
.

1.

Then,
for any
H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, the following holds

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\{G\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}}):\|G-H\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon\})=-\frac{1}{\theta}{\mathcal{J}}_{h}(H){-}T\ln 2,

(1.17)

where

{\mathcal{J}}_{h}
is the functional defined for
H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
by

{\mathcal{J}}_{h}(H)=-\iint_{{\mathfrak{R}}}\sigma(\nabla H(x,t)){\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\frac{1}{2}\left.\iint_{{\mathfrak{R}}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x,t){\rm d}h(x,t)\right|_{0}^{T}.

(1.18)

Moreover, (
1.17
) holds if we replace the
\limsup
by a
\liminf
.

2.

{\mathcal{J}}_{h}
has compact level sets in
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
.

3.

The law of the height functions conditioned to remain in
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})
satisfies a large deviation principle with speed
N^{2}
and good rate function
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}(H)
which is infinite outside
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
and otherwise given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{h}(H)=\frac{1}{\theta}({\mathcal{J}}_{h}(H)-\inf_{G\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)}{\mathcal{J}}_{h}(G))\,.

(1.19)

Moreover,
{\mathcal{I}}_{h}
is minimized at a unique minimizer
H^{h}\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
.

The surface tension
\sigma
is the same as that of lozenge tiling. It has been proven in
[
23
, Theorem 7.5]
, that
{\mathcal{J}}_{h}
is lower semicontinuous over
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
with the uniform topology. Since
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
is compact, it follows that
{\mathcal{J}}_{h}
has compact level sets in
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
. The uniqueness of the minimizer for the variational principle (
1.19
) has been proven in
[
60
, Proposition 4.5]
. We remark that even though the rate function
{\mathcal{J}}_{h}(H)
does not depend on
\theta
, the space
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
over which we minimize it depends on
\theta
, as
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
.

Our second main result derives the large deviation asymptotics of skew Jack symmetric polynomials as the number of variables goes to infinity. As discussed after (
1.6
), the main challenge lies in analyzing the sum of the weights
{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}})
from (
1.7
). It can be interpreted as the partition function of non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}
, with time dependent drift. Explicitly, the transition probability is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}}}(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}|\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})=\frac{1}{(1+{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}})^{N}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+\theta e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})},

(1.20)

for
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
and
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
.
The large deviation of the aforementioned non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks with time-dependent drift can be deduced from
Theorem

1.6
through the application of Varadhan’s lemma. An immediate consequence of this is the following result concerning the asymptotics of skew Jack polynomials following from the formula (
1.6
). The proof for this result is provided in
Section

6.4
. We next state our large deviation results under the law
\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}}}
and deduce the asymptotics of skew Jack polynomials in the related scaling.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\theta,T>0
and
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
.
We consider a sequence of Young diagrams

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}^{(N)}=(\lambda_{1}^{(N)}\geqslant\lambda_{2}^{(N)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}^{(N)}),\quad\bm{\mu}^{(N)}=(\mu_{1}^{(N)}\geqslant\mu_{2}^{(N)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\mu_{N}^{(N)}),\quad N\geqslant 1,

such that
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)})
are given by (
1.3
). Assume
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)},{\mathsf{T}})
are
(h,\theta,T)
-admissible and for
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
take
{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}=e^{f({\mathsf{t}}/N)}
for a continuously differentiable function
f
.

1.

The asymptotics of the skew Jack polynomials are given by

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln J_{({\bm{\lambda}}^{(N)})^{\prime}\setminus({\bm{\mu}}^{(N)})^{\prime}}(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1};\theta^{-1})=\frac{1}{\theta}{\mathcal{J}}^{f}_{h},

where

{\mathcal{J}}^{f}_{h}:=\sup_{H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)}\left\{\iint_{{\mathfrak{R}}}\sigma(\nabla H(x,t)){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+{\mathcal{F}}^{f}(H)\right\},

(1.21)

and the linear functional
{\mathcal{F}}^{f}(H)
is given by

\mathcal{F}^{f}(H):=-\iint_{\mathfrak{R}}f(s)\partial_{s}H(y,s){\rm d}y{\rm d}s.

(1.22)

2.

The law of height functions under
\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}}}
defined in (
1.20
) conditioned to remain in
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})
satisfies a large deviation principle with speed
N^{2}
and good rate function
{\mathcal{I}}^{f}_{h}(H)/\theta
which is infinite outside
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
and otherwise given by

{\mathcal{I}}^{f}_{h}(H)=-\left(\iint_{\mathfrak{R}}\sigma(\nabla H(x,t)){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+{\mathcal{F}}^{f}(H)\right)+{\mathcal{J}}^{f}_{h}\,.

(1.23)

Moreover,
{\mathcal{I}}^{f}_{h}
achieves its minimal value at a unique height function
H^{f}_{h}
.

1.4
Asymptotics for Skew Macdonald Polynomials

The goal of this section is to study the asymptotics of the skew Macdonald polynomials
P_{{\bm{\lambda}}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}}({\bm{x}};q,t)
, which are symmetric functions in infinitely many variables
{\bm{x}}=(x_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\infty}
and parametrized by two Young diagrams
\bm{\mu}\subset{\bm{\lambda}}
. We refer to
Appendix

C
for more detailed discussion on (skew) Macdonald polynomials.

Fix
\theta>0
, and take
t=q^{\theta}
. Assume
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
are Young diagrams with at most
N
rows, and take any
{\bm{b}}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})
. Then the same as in (
1.3
), we identify
{\bm{\mu}},{\bm{\lambda}}
as particle configurations
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}
.
For non-intersecting Bernoulli walks
{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
from
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{T}})=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}
, we define their weights as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}})=\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}{q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}.

(1.24)

Then we can rewrite the skew Macdonald polynomial evaluated at
(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})
as follows

\displaystyle P_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}/{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}}(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1};t,q)=\frac{P_{{\bm{\mu}}}(1,t,t^{2},\cdots,t^{N-1};q,t)}{P_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1,t,t^{2},\cdots,t^{N-1};q,t)}\sum_{{\mathsf{p}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}};\bm{{\mathsf{z}}};{\mathsf{T}})}\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}}),

where
\bm{\lambda}^{\prime},{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}
are the transposes of
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
.

Similarly to the skew Jack polynomials, there are explicit formulas for the Macdonald symmetric polynomials evaluated at the principal specialization, i.e.
P_{{\bm{\mu}}}(1,t,t^{2},\cdots,t^{N-1};q,t),P_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1,t,t^{2},\cdots,t^{N-1};q,t)
, making them amenable for asymptotic analysis. The sum of the weights
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}};{\bm{b}})
can be interpreted as the partition function of non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}
, with the transition probability given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}},q}(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}|\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\frac{q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}+\theta e_{i}}-q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta e_{j}}}{q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-q^{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N}b_{\mathsf{t}}^{e_{i}}.

(1.25)

for
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
, and
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
. The above Markov process (
1.25
) is a special case of ascending Macdonald process as constructed in
[
8
]
.

The Macdonald processes (
1.25
) are different from non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks (
1.8
), and also have singular interactions among adjacent particles. However, by taking their ratios, we observe the cancellation of singularities. This observation allows us to establish the large deviation principle for the Macdonald process through the application of Varadhan’s lemma. The following result on the asymptotics of skew Macdonald polynomials is a consequence of the large deviation principle of the Macdonald process (
1.25
). The proof is given in
Section

6.4
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\theta,T>0
and
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
.
We consider a sequence of Young diagrams
(\bm{\lambda}^{(N)},\bm{\mu}^{(N)})
such that
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)})
are given by (
1.3
). Assume
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)},{\mathsf{T}})
are
(h,\theta,T)
-admissible. For
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
, we take
{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}=e^{f({\mathsf{t}}/N)}
for a continuously differentiable function
f
. Moreover,
we take
t=q^{\theta},q=e^{\kappa/N}
for some

\kappa<0
.

1.

The asymptotics of the skew Macdonald polynomials are given by

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln P_{({\bm{\lambda}}^{(N)})^{\prime}\setminus({\bm{\mu}}^{(N)})^{\prime}}(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1};q,t)=\frac{1}{\theta}{\mathcal{J}}^{f}_{h},

where

{\mathcal{J}}^{f}_{h}
is defined in (
1.21
).

2.

The law of height functions under
\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}},q}
defined in (
1.25
) conditioned to remain in
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})
satisfies a large deviation principle with speed
N^{2}
and good rate function
{\mathcal{I}}^{f}_{h}(H)/\theta
which is infinite outside
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
and otherwise given by (
1.23
).
Moreover,
{\mathcal{I}}^{f}_{h}
achieves its minimal value at a unique height function
H^{f}_{h}
.

We remark that the rate function surprisingly does not depend on
\kappa
. This indicates that
q
is converging toward one too rapidly to induce changes at the large
N
limit. However, other scalings seem difficult to deduce by Varadhan’s lemma and require other kinds of large deviations estimates.

1.5
Related works on the Asymptotics of Symmetric Polynomials

The asymptotic behavior of symmetric polynomials can be studied under other scaling regimes. One intensively studied regime is to take limit along a sequence of Vershik-Kerov partitions, where the size and rows of the partitions all grow linearly in
N
. In this way, the normalized Schur functions approximate a character of the infinite unitary group. The asymptotics of normalized Schur functions in this scaling regime were originally derived by Vershik and Kerov in
[
63
]
. Generalizations of these results to the asymptotics of Jack and Macdonald polynomials under the same scaling can be found in
[
53
,
21
,
22
]
.

While the Vershik-Kerov partitions involve thin partitions, this paper explores a different scaling regime. Specifically, we consider the limit along a sequence of partitions where the number of rows and columns grows linearly in
N
, but the size grows quadratically in
N^{2}
. Gorin and Panova’s work in
[
26
]
presents an example within this regime. They obtained the asymptotics of Schur polynomials with all but finitely many parameters set to one, see also
[
33
,
32
]
. These asymptotic results played an important role in the study of extreme characters of the infinite-dimensional unitary group
[
26
,
16
]
, as well as applications in random lozenge tilings
[
17
,
18
]
and the six-vertex model
[
26
,
25
]
.

The structure constants of symmetric polynomials, such as Kostka number, Kronecker coefficients and Littlewood-Richardson coefficients are fundamental quantities in algebraic combinatorics. Despite the absence of explicit formulas, the asymptotics of specific extreme Kronecker and Littlewood-Richardson coefficients have been derived in
[
57
,
62
,
55
,
56
]
. Using the asymptotics of Schur symmetric polynomials, the large deviation asymptotics of Kostka numbers and the large deviation upper bound for the Littlewood-Richardson coefficients have been obtained in
[
5
]
. These results were generalized in
[
38
]
to the asymptotic behavior
of weight multiplicities of irreducible representations of compact or complex simple Lie algebras in the limit of large rank. The asymptotics of Jack polynomials and Macdonald polynomials should allow to address similar questions for the large deviations of their structure constants.

1.6
Ideas of the proofs

In the continuous setting, the asymptotics of the Harish-Chandra-Itzykson-Zuber integral has been discovered through studying the large deviations of Dyson’s Brownian motion by the first named author and Zeitouni
[
34
,
35
,
30
]
. Subsequently, analogous findings were extended to the rectangular spherical integral and Generalized Bessel Functions in
[
38
,
31
]
. Large deviations are established thanks to the standard tilting argument by martingales.
However, these martingales are constructed thanks to stochastic calculus and the specific structures of Dyson’s Brownian motion.

In this paper, we focus on the discrete setting, where tools from stochastic calculus are not available. We
summarize the main ideas of the proof of our main Theorems,
Theorem

1.6
.

Theorem

1.7
and
Theorem

1.8
, can then be deduced by Varadhan’s lemma.
In the special case when
\theta=1
, the non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks (
1.8
) are equivalent to random Lozenge tiling of strip domains, where the variational principle of the height function has been proven in
[
20
]
. In fact their results apply to random tilings of more general domains. The proof of the variational principle for tilings in
[
20
]
relies on the exact computation of the partition function for tilings on a large torus, utilizing Kasteleyn’s matrix. Unfortunately, this method is not applicable in our context when
\theta\neq 1
.
Instead, our proof relies on the recently introduced dynamical loop equations by the second author and Gorin
[
24
]
, based on Nekrasov’s equations
[
10
,
51
,
50
]
. These equations have proven effective for analyzing the fluctuations of large families of two-dimensional interacting particle systems in both discrete and continuous settings. Examples include nonintersecting Bernoulli/Poisson random walks
[
27
,
45
,
36
]
,
\beta
-corner processes
[
28
,
9
]
, measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns
[
19
,
59
,
58
]
, and Macdonald processes
[
8
]
. In particular, a version of dynamical loop equations has been derived for the non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks.

We establish the large deviation principle using Cramér’s method, by tilting the measure of nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk using exponential martingales. A key point is that these martingales can be identified as smooth functions of the height functions thanks to dynamical loop equations.
Our proof is divided into an upper bound and a lower bound. The normalization constant for these exponential martingales can be determined using dynamical loop equations. Subsequently, the large deviation upper bound can be obtained straightforwardly by applying Markov’s inequality. The measure tilted by the exponential martingale coincides with the drifted non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walk. Utilizing the dynamical loop equation once again, we establish a limit shape theorem for the drifted non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walk. As a consequence, for any targeting measure process, we can construct an exponential martingale such that the tilted measure concentrates around it. This provides the large deviation lower bound.

In the above outlined proof, we utilize dynamical loop equations to investigate nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks with drift. However, two challenges hinder their direct application. Firstly, dynamical loop equations require that the drift is analytic. To overcome this, we convolve the targeting measure process with a small Cauchy distribution (which is reminiscent of the strategy followed in
[
34
]
). This convolution enables the analytical extension of the density to a strip region around the real axis, allowing for a similar extension of the drift terms. As a trade-off, we are required to analyze measure-valued processes supported on the entire real axis. This challenge is addressed through precise truncations and the utilization of the explicit form of the rate function.

Secondly and more critically, dynamical loop equations require certain non-criticality conditions. Specifically, the particle system cannot be too dense, meaning the density cannot be too close to one. To tackle this challenge, rather than studying the nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks as a whole, we partition space-time into small regions of parallelogram shape. Within each region, we ensure that both the density and velocity remain nearly constant. In each region, the system resembles nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks but with a reduced number of particles and possibly left and right boundaries. If the density and velocity are non-extremal, we can establish a large deviation principle using the dynamical loop equation as outlined above. However, if the density or velocity are extremal, we prove a large deviation upper bound by directly analyzing the walk. Importantly, it is observed that the contribution from these regions is negligible. The original nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks have long-range pairwise interactions. After partitioning, particles from different regions also interact with each other. Fortunately, these interactions occur between particles which are far from each other and are not singular, which can be approximated as smooth weights. In this manner, we can integrate all regions together and derive the variational principle for the original nonintersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks.

Loop equations and Nekrasov’s equations have been key to establish central limit theorems
[
41
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
. To our knowledge, it is the first time it is used to derive large deviations.

1.7
Organization of the paper

In
Section

2
, we collect various facts about height functions, Vandermonde determinants and the free entropy. They will be used repeatedly in the rest of this paper.
Section

3
provides an overview of the proof for
Theorem

1.6
, which relies on both a large deviation upper bound (
Proposition

3.2
) and a corresponding lower bound (
Proposition

3.5
) for non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks with a constant slope.
The proof for
Proposition

3.2
is detailed in
Section

4
, while the proof for
Proposition

3.5
is presented in
Section

5
. Moving forward,
Section

6
contains the proof for
Theorem

1.7
, on the asymptotics of skew Jack polynomials. Similarly, the proof for
Theorem

1.8
, which explores the asymptotics of skew Macdonald polynomials, is outlined in
Appendix

C
.
Finally in
Appendix

A
, we collect the results on dynamical loop equations from
[
24
]
.

1.8
Notations

We use
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
to represent Young diagrams.
For macro particle locations we use mathsf letters:
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}}),\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}),\bm{{\mathsf{y}}},\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}
, where time
{\mathsf{t}}
ranges from
0
to
{\mathsf{T}}
.
For micro particle locations we use standard letters
x_{i}(t),{\bm{x}}(t),{\bm{y}},{\bm{z}}
, where the time
t
ranges from
0
to
T
. We use
H
and
H^{*}
for arbitrary limiting Height functions, , that is an element of
\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
that arises as the limit of height functions.
\widetilde{H}
stands for the smoothed version of
H
. And we use

{\mathcal{H}}
for the associated height functions of non-intersecting random walks.

For two quantities
X
and
Y
depending on
N
,
we write that
X=\OO(Y)
or
X\lesssim Y
if there exists some universal constant
C>0
such
that
|X|\leqslant CY
. We write
X=\oo(Y)
, or
X\ll Y
if the ratio
|X|/Y\rightarrow\infty
as
N
goes to infinity. We write

X\asymp Y
if there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that
Y/C\leqslant|X|\leqslant CY
. We denote
[\![{a,b}]\!]=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
and
[\![{n}]\!]=[\![{1,n}]\!]
.

2
Setup and Preliminary Results

In this section, we collect various facts about height functions, the Vandermonde determinant, and free entropy. These facts will be used repeatedly throughout the rest of this paper. In the rest of this paper, for simplicity of notations, we will assume that
T={\mathsf{T}}/N
. The more general case that
{\mathsf{T}}/N
converges to
T
as in
Definition

1.5
can be proven in the same way, by noticing that the rate functions in
Theorem

1.6
,
Theorem

1.7
,
Theorem

1.8
are all continuous in
T
.

2.1
Approximating Height Function

For any height function
H^{*}\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}};h)
,
the following lemma states that if
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})\neq\emptyset
, then there exists a non-interesecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
, and its height function is close to
H^{*}
. The proof is a consequence of
a careful discretization of
H^{*}
, and we postpone it to
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\theta,T>0
and
h\in\Adm^{\partial}_{\theta}({\mathfrak{R}})
. Consider two sequences of particle configurations and time
{\mathsf{T}}
:
(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)},{\mathsf{T}})
which are
(h,\theta,T)
-admissible as defined in
Definition

1.5
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
,
and any
H^{*}\in\Adm(\mathfrak{R};h)
, there exists a non-intersecting Bernoulli paths
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
from
{\bm{x}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}/N
to
{\bm{x}}(T)=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}/N
with height function
{\mathcal{H}}
, such that on
{\mathfrak{R}}

\displaystyle\|H^{*}-{\mathcal{H}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon,

(2.1)

provided
N
is large enough.

We introduce the following
\ell
-mesh, and
Lemma

2.3
states that on most of the
\ell
-mesh,
H^{*}
has an approximate linear approximation.

Definition 2.2

(
\ell
-mesh)
.

Adopt
Definition

1.3
, there exists a large constant
A>0
, such that for any
H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}};h)
,

\displaystyle H(x,t)=0,\quad x\leqslant-A,\quad H(x,t)=\theta,\quad x\geqslant A.

Figure 2
:
Shown above is the
\ell
-mesh.

Take a small
\ell>0
such that
A/\ell,T/\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
. We cover the region
[-A,A]\times[0,T]
by rectangles of size
6\ell\times\ell
(see
Figure

2
):

\displaystyle[-A,A]\times[0,T]=\cup{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta),\quad{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)=[(\alpha-3)\ell,(\alpha+3)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell],

where
\alpha,\beta
are integer numbers so that

{-A/\ell\leqslant\alpha\leqslant A/\ell,\quad 0\leqslant\beta\leqslant T/\ell}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
H
be an asymptotic height function, namely a given element of
Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, and let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. If
\ell
is sufficiently small then on at least a
1-\varepsilon_{0}
fraction of the rectangles
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
from the
\ell
-mesh (in
Definition

2.2
) we have the following two properties

1.

On
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H
has a linear approximation within an error bounded by
\ell\varepsilon_{0}
, namely there exists
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
such that for
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle|H(x,t)-(H(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)+\varrho(x-\alpha\ell)-\varrho v(t-\beta\ell))|\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}\ell.

2.

For at least a
1-\varepsilon_{0}
fraction (in
measure) of the points
x
of
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the gradient
\nabla H(x)
exists and is within
\varepsilon_{0}
of
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
[
20
, Lemma 2.2]
, so we omit.
∎

Lemma

2.3
states that any height function
H\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
locally can be approximated by height functions with constant slope. We introduce the following height functions with constant slope.

Definition 2.4
.

Given a slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
, we construct the constant slope height function
{\mathcal{A}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,\ell]
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=(x-tv)\varrho,\quad(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{P}};\quad{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=0,\quad x\leqslant tv;\quad{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=\ell\varrho,\quad x\geqslant\ell+tv.

(2.2)

where
{\mathfrak{P}}
is the parallelogram region:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}:=\{x,t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,\quad tv\leqslant x\leqslant\ell+tv\}.

(2.3)

We say
{\mathcal{A}}
is the height function with slope given by
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
.

2.2
Vandermonde Determinant and Free Entropy

Lemma

2.5
and
Lemma

2.6
concern certain useful identity and estimates of Vandermonde determinants.

Lemma 2.5
.

For any particle configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}\in\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta}
, and
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
, the following holds

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{n}=k}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}={n\choose k}.

(2.4)

Proof.

Since Vandermonde determinant is anti-symmetric, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{n}=k}V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}}),

(2.5)

as a function of
{\mathsf{x}}_{1},{\mathsf{x}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathsf{x}}_{n}
, is anti-symmetric. Therefore, it has the Vandermonde determinant
V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})
as a factor. We notice that for every
\theta
, the coefficient of the monomial
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{n-1}{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{n-2}\cdots{\mathsf{x}}_{n-1}
of degree
n(n-1)/2
in
V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})
is
1
. The claim (
2.4
) follows from that the coefficient of
{\mathsf{x}}_{1}^{n-1}{\mathsf{x}}_{2}^{n-2}\cdots{\mathsf{x}}_{n-1}
in (
2.5
) is
{n\choose k}
, and in
V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})
is
1
.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

There exists a constant
C>0
. For any particle configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}\in\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta}
, and
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}\in\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta}
(this is equivalent to that
V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})\neq 0
),
the following hold

\displaystyle C^{-n}\leqslant\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}\leqslant 2^{n}.

(2.6)

Proof.

The upper bound in (
2.6
) follows from
Lemma

2.5
which implies

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{e}}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}=2^{n},

(2.7)

and the fact that all terms in the left hand side are non-negative.
For the lower bound, we first show that there exists a constant
C=C(\theta)
such that for every configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}
,

\displaystyle\left|\ln\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+\theta e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}-\theta\ln\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}\right|\leqslant C\frac{(e_{i}-e_{j})^{2}}{({{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}})^{2}}.

(2.8)

If
e_{i}-e_{j}=0
, there is nothing to prove, the lefthand side is
0
. If
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}=(j-i)\theta
, i.e. the particle configuration on
\{{\mathsf{x}}_{i},{\mathsf{x}}_{i+1},\cdots,{\mathsf{x}}_{j}\}
is tightly packed. The quantity
V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})
is nonzero only if
e_{i}-e_{j}\in\{0,1\}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{e_{i}-e_{j}}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}\in\left\{0,\frac{1}{\theta(j-i)}\right\},\quad\mbox{ so that }0\leqslant\frac{e_{i}-e_{j}}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\theta}.

Otherwise,
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}\geqslant 1+\theta
, and we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{e_{i}-e_{j}}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{1+\theta}.

The statement (
2.8
) follows from the inequality below

\displaystyle|\ln(1+\theta x)-\theta\ln(1+x)|\leqslant Cx^{2},\quad x\in[-(1+\theta)^{-1},\theta^{-1}],

which follows from the fact that for
x\in[-(1+\theta)^{-1},\theta^{-1}]

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{2}(\ln(1+\theta x)-\theta\ln(1+x))|=\theta(1-\theta)\frac{|1-\theta x^{2}|}{(1+x)^{2}(1+\theta x)^{2}}\leqslant\frac{(1+\theta)^{5}(1-\theta)}{\theta\min\{\theta,1\}}.

Summing over
i,j
in (
2.8
), we deduce

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}&\geqslant\theta\ln\left(\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}\right)-C\sum_{i<j}\frac{4}{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j})^{2}}\\
&\geqslant\theta\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}-C\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{4}{(\theta(i-j))^{2}}\geqslant\theta\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}-C^{\prime}n.\end{split}

(2.9)

By the same argument as for (
2.9
) (replacing
{\bm{e}}
by
-{\bm{e}}
), we also have that
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}-{\bm{e}}
satisfies

\displaystyle n\ln 2\geqslant\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}-\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}

\displaystyle\geqslant\theta\ln\left(\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}-{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}\right)-C^{\prime}n=\theta\ln\left(\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}\right)-C^{\prime}n,

(2.10)

where the upper bound by
n\ln 2
comes from (
2.6
).
Combining (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), we obtain the lower bound in (
2.6
)

\displaystyle\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}\geqslant\theta\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}-C^{\prime}n\geqslant-n\ln 2-2C^{\prime}n=-(\ln 2+2C^{\prime})n.

∎

In the following lemma we collect certain estimates on the limit of the Vandermonde determinants, and on the free entropy.

Lemma 2.7
.

Fix
\kappa<0
. There exists a constant
C=C(\kappa)>0
, the following holds.
For any particle configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N}
, let
{\bm{x}}\in\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}/N
. Assume
{\bm{x}}
is supported on
[-A,A]
, and we denote its empirical density as
\varrho(x;{\bm{x}})
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\theta^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i<j}\ln(x_{i}-x_{j})=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|\varrho(x;{\bm{x}})\varrho(y;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}x{\rm d}y+\OO\left(\frac{\ln N}{N}\right),\\
&\frac{\theta^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i<j}\ln(1-e^{\kappa(x_{i}-x_{j})})=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln(1-e^{\kappa|x-y|})\varrho(x;{\bm{x}})\varrho(y;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}x{\rm d}y+\OO\left(\frac{\ln N}{N}\right).\end{split}

(2.11)

For any two height functions
h_{1},h_{2}
such that
\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
with
0\leqslant\partial_{x}h_{1},\partial_{x}h_{2}\leqslant 1
and their supports
\supp(\partial_{x}h_{1}),\supp(\partial_{x}h_{2})\in[-A,A]
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left|\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h_{1}(x){\rm d}h_{1}(y)-\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h_{2}(x){\rm d}h_{2}(y)\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon,\\
&\left|\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln(1-e^{\kappa|x-y|}){\rm d}h_{1}(x){\rm d}h_{1}(y)-\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln(1-e^{\kappa|x-y|}){\rm d}h_{2}(x){\rm d}h_{2}(y)\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon.\end{split}

(2.12)

Proof.

We will only prove the first statements in (
2.11
) and (
2.12
), and the second statements follow from the same arguments. For any
i<j-1
, denote
x_{i}-x_{j}=r\geqslant 2\theta/N
, we have

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\int_{x_{i}}^{x_{i}+\theta/N}\int_{x_{j}}^{x_{j}+\theta/N}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}x{\rm d}y=\int_{0}^{\theta/N}\int_{0}^{\theta/N}\ln|r+x-y|{\rm d}x{\rm d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\theta/N}(\ln(r-\tau)+\ln(r+\tau))\left(\frac{\theta}{N}-\tau\right){\rm d}\tau=\frac{\theta^{2}}{N^{2}}\ln r+\int_{0}^{\theta/N}\ln\left(1-\frac{\tau^{2}}{r^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{\theta}{N}-\tau\right){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta^{2}}{N^{2}}\ln r+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N^{4}r^{2}}\right).

By summing over all the pairs
i<j-1
, and bound
\ln(x_{i}-x_{i+1})=\OO(\ln N)
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\theta^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i<j}\ln(x_{i}-x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{2}\int_{x_{i}}^{x_{i}+\theta/N}\int_{x_{j}}^{x_{j}+\theta/N}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}x{\rm d}y+\OO\left(\frac{\ln N}{N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|\varrho(x;{\bm{x}})\varrho(y;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}x{\rm d}y+\OO\left(\frac{\ln N}{N}\right).

This gives (
2.11
).
The first statement in (
2.12
) follows from

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}:=\left|\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}(h_{1}(x)-h_{2}(x)){\rm d}h_{1}(y)\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon.

Denoting
\varrho_{1}(y)=\partial_{y}h_{1}(y)
, we deduce by an integration by part

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\left|\int\PV\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varrho_{1}(y)}{x-y}{\rm d}y(h_{1}(x)-h_{2}(x)){\rm d}x\right|\lesssim\varepsilon\int_{-A}^{A}|\Hib(\varrho_{1})(y)|{\rm d}y

\displaystyle\leqslant\varepsilon\left(2A\int_{-A}^{A}|\Hib(\varrho_{1})(y)|^{2}{\rm d}y\right)^{1/2}\leqslant\varepsilon\left(2A\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\varrho_{1}^{2}{\rm d}y\right)^{1/2}=\varepsilon\sqrt{2A},

where we used that the Hilbert transform preserves
L_{2}
norm, and
\varrho_{1}\leqslant 1
.
∎

3
Outline of the proof of Theorem
1.6

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.6
assuming it holds in the case where
H
has constant slope. The proof of the later is derived in the next two sections.
We recall the boundary height function
h
from
Definition

1.3
, and the sequences of particle configurations
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
from
Theorem

1.6
.
We recall the set of non-interesecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks from
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
to
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
from (
1.4
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})=\{{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}:\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{0}=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}\}.

Fix an asymptotic height function
H^{*}\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, and sufficiently small
\varepsilon_{0},\ell>0
such that
Lemma

2.3
holds and
{\mathsf{L}}=\ell N\in\mathbb{N}
. We introduce some parameters
0<\varepsilon,\delta,\xi\ll\ell
and
0<\zeta\ll 1
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}\ell,\quad\delta\ll\zeta\ell,\quad\varepsilon\ll\zeta\ell,\quad\xi\ll\zeta\ell\quad\varepsilon/\delta\ll\zeta^{2},\quad\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)\ll\zeta\xi.

(3.1)

We denote and
{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*})
the set of non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks with height function close to
H^{*}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*})=\{{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)};{\mathsf{T}})\text{ with height function ${\mathcal{H}}$}:\|{\mathcal{H}}-H^{*}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon\}.

3.1
Upper Bound

To prove the large deviation upper bound for
\mathbb{P}({\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}))
, we partition
{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*})
according to the possible particle configurations at times
\mathbb{N}{\mathsf{L}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}}):=\{{\mathsf{p}}=\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}):\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\text{ for }{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}\},

where
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
and
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{T}})=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
.
By our assumption that
|{\mathsf{y}}_{i}^{(N)}|,|{\mathsf{z}}_{i}^{(N)}|\leqslant CN
, then there exists
A>0
(say
A=C+{\mathsf{T}}
) such that the particle configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})
is supported on
[-AN,AN]
for all
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
. The number of total choices of
\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}}
is given by

\displaystyle{2AN\choose N}^{T/\ell}\leqslant 2^{(2TA/\ell)N}=e^{\OO(N)},

which is negligible. In the rest of this section, we will fix the particle configurations
\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}}
.

We fix integer numbers
\alpha,\beta
, and recall the
\ell
-mesh and rectangle
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)=[(\alpha-3)\ell,(\alpha+3)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]
from
Definition

2.2
. We consider the restriction of the particle configuration
{\bm{y}}(\beta\ell)
on the interval
[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell)

\displaystyle y_{i-1}(\beta\ell)<\alpha\ell\leqslant y_{i}(\beta\ell)<\cdots<y_{j}(\beta\ell)<(\alpha+1)\ell\leqslant y_{j+1}(\beta\ell).

We denote the index set of the above restricted particle configuration as
I(\alpha,\beta)=\{i,i+1,i+2,\cdots,j\}
and denote
n:=|I(\alpha,\beta)|=j-i+1
.
For any
{\mathsf{p}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})
, its restriction on the index set
I(\alpha,\beta)
and time interval
[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]
,
\{{\sf{\bm{x}}}_{k}(t)\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}
forms an
n
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walk inside the rectangle
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
. The next lemma states that its height function is close to a height function with constant slope.

Lemma 3.1
.

There exists a large finite constant
C>1
so that the following holds. Assume that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the height function
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}
and error
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon/\ell
in the sense of
Lemma

2.3
.
For any
\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})
, we denote the shifted and rescaled particle configuration
\{z_{k}(t)\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n,0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}

\displaystyle z_{k}(t)=\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{k+i-1}((t+\beta\ell)N)}{N}-\alpha\ell,\quad 1\leqslant k\leqslant n.

(3.2)

Then
\{z_{k}(t)\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n,0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
form an
n
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walk, where

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\theta n}{N}-\ell\varrho\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon,

(3.3)

and its height function
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}
(defined as in (
1.11
)) satisfies

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon,\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,\quad x\in{\mathfrak{R}}.

(3.4)

where
{\mathcal{A}}
is the height function with slope given by
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
from
Definition

2.4
.

Proof.

We denote the height function of the rescaled particle configuration
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
as
{\mathcal{H}}
, then from the construction (
3.2
),
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}
is a shifted version of
{\mathcal{H}}
. More precisely, for any
0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)={\mathcal{H}}(x+\alpha\ell,t+\beta\ell)-\frac{(i-1)\theta}{N},\quad z_{1}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant z_{n}(t)+\frac{\theta}{N}.

(3.5)

And for
x\leqslant z_{1}(t)
we have
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)=0
; for
x\geqslant z_{n}(t)
we have
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)=n\theta/N
.
Next, we show (
3.3
). Since
{\mathcal{H}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{n\theta}{N}+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)={\mathcal{H}}((\alpha+1)\ell,\beta\ell)-{\mathcal{H}}(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)=H^{*}((\alpha+1)\ell,\beta\ell)-H^{*}(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)+\OO(\varepsilon)=\ell\varrho+\OO(\varepsilon).

(3.6)

The claim (
3.3
) follows by rearranging.
We will prove (
3.4
), by dividing it into different regions.

1.

For
x\leqslant\min\{z_{1}(t),tv\}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)={\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=0
.

2.

For
x\geqslant\min\{z_{n}(t)+\theta/N,tv+\ell\}
,
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)=\theta n/N
and
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=\ell\varrho
, and the claim (
3.4
) follows from (
3.3
) .

3.

For
z_{1}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant z_{n}(t)+\theta/N
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)={\mathcal{H}}(x+\alpha\ell,t+\beta\ell)-\frac{(i-1)\theta}{N}={\mathcal{H}}(x+\alpha\ell,t+\beta\ell)-{\mathcal{H}}(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)\\
&=H^{*}(x+\alpha\ell,t+\beta\ell)-H^{*}(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)+\OO(\varepsilon)\\
&=x\varrho-t\varrho v+\OO(\varepsilon).\end{split}

(3.7)

It follows that for
\max\{z_{1}(t),tv\}\leqslant x\leqslant\min\{z_{n}(t)+\theta/N,tv+\ell\}
, we have
|{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)|=\OO(\varepsilon)
.

4.

If
tv\leqslant z_{1}(t)
, then for
tv\leqslant x\leqslant z_{1}(t)
we have

\displaystyle 0=H(x,t)\geqslant{\mathcal{H}}(x+\alpha\ell,t+\beta\ell)-{\mathcal{H}}(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)=x\varrho-t\varrho v+\OO(\varepsilon)={\mathcal{A}}(x,t)+\OO(\varepsilon),

where in the second statement we used the construction of (
3.7
); in the third statement we used that
H^{*}
and
{\mathcal{H}}
are close, and
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
.
The claim (
3.4
) follows.

5.

If
z_{1}(t)\leqslant tv
, then for
z_{1}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant tv
we have
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=0
, and

\displaystyle 0\leqslant{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)=x\varrho-t\varrho v+\OO(\varepsilon)\leqslant\OO(\varepsilon),

where in the second statement we used (
3.7
); in the last statement we used
x\leqslant tv
.

6.

If
z_{n}(t)+\theta/N\leqslant tv+\ell
, then for
z_{n}(t)+\theta/N\leqslant x\leqslant tv+\ell
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\theta n}{N}={\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)\leqslant{\mathcal{H}}(x+\alpha\ell,t+\beta\ell)-{\mathcal{H}}(\alpha\ell,\beta\ell)=x\varrho-t\varrho v+\OO(\varepsilon)={\mathcal{A}}(x,t)+\OO(\varepsilon),

where in the second statement we used the construction of (
3.7
); in the third statement we used that
H^{*}
and
{\mathcal{H}}
are close, and
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
.
The claim (
3.4
) follows from the above estimate together with (
3.6
).

7.

If
tv+\ell\leqslant z_{n}(t)+\theta/N
, then for
tv+\ell\leqslant x\leqslant z_{n}(t)+\theta/N
we have
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=\varrho\ell
, and

\displaystyle\frac{\theta n}{N}\geqslant{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)=x\varrho-t\varrho v+\OO(\varepsilon)\geqslant\ell\varrho+\OO(\varepsilon).

(3.8)

where in the second statement we used (
3.7
); in the last statement we used
x\geqslant\ell+tv
. The claim (
3.4
) follows from combining (
3.8
) with (
3.6
).

∎

With the above decomposition, we can rewrite the probability
\mathbb{P}({\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}}))
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{2^{N{\mathsf{T}}}}\sum_{{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})}\prod_{\beta}\prod_{\alpha}\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i<j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\\
\times\prod_{\beta}\prod_{\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}}\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right),\end{split}

(3.9)

where the summation is over paths
\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})
.

The large deviation upper bound in
Theorem

1.6
is a consequence of the following two statements. We postpone their proofs in
Section

4
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Adopt the notations in
Theorem

1.6
. Fix any
H^{*}\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, and a particle configuration
\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*})
, with
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
and
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{T}})=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
.
There exists a constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\sum\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i<j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\leqslant C,

(3.10)

where the summation is over
\{{\sf{\bm{x}}}_{k}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta{\mathsf{L}},(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}]}
with
{\sf{\bm{x}}}_{k}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})={\sf{\bm{y}}}_{k}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})
.
If we assume that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the height function
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}
and error
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon/\ell
in the sense of
Lemma

2.3
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\sum\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i<j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\\
&\qquad=\frac{1}{\theta}\iint_{[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO(\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\log(1/\varepsilon)),\end{split}

(3.11)

where the summation is over
\{{\sf{\bm{x}}}_{k}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta{\mathsf{L}},(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}]}
with
{\sf{\bm{x}}}_{k}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})={\sf{\bm{y}}}_{k}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})
and (
3.4
) holds.

Lemma 3.3
.

Adopt notations in
Theorem

1.6
. Fix any
H^{*}\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, and a particle configuration
\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*})
, with
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
and
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{T}})=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
.
For any
\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})
, the following holds

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{\beta}\sum_{\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\ln\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{T}})-{\mathsf{y}}_{j}({\mathsf{T}})}{{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{y}}_{j}(0)}\right)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0}N^{2}).

Proof of Large Deviation Upper bound in
Theorem

1.6
.

Thanks to
Lemma

3.3
, we can bound (
3.9
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\eqref{e:ldpup}

\displaystyle\leqslant-T\ln(2)+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\sum_{{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})}\prod_{\alpha,\beta}\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i<j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{T}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{T}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)}\right)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0})

\displaystyle=-T\ln(2)+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\iint_{[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO(\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\log(1/\varepsilon))

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{T}})-{\mathsf{y}}_{j}({\mathsf{T}})}{{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{y}}_{j}(0)}\right)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\iint_{\mathfrak{R}}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\frac{1}{2\theta}\left.\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x,t){\rm d}h(x,t)\right|_{0}^{T}-T\ln(2)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\ln(1/(\varepsilon))),

where in the second equality, we used Proposition
3.2

and that by assumption, the number of
(\alpha,\beta)
such that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H^{*}
does not have a linear approximation, is bounded by
\varepsilon_{0}
. In the last line we used
Lemma

2.7
. The large deviation upper bound in
Theorem

1.6
follows by sending
N
to infinity, and
\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon
to zero.
∎

3.2
Lower Bound

To prove the large deviation lower bound, we recall the height function
{\mathcal{H}}
as constructed in (
2.1
)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{H}}-H^{*}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon.

(3.12)

We denote
\{{\bm{y}}(t)\}_{t\in\{0,\ell,2\ell,\cdots,T\}}
the particle configurations corresponding to
{\mathcal{H}}
at times
0,\ell,2\ell,\cdots,T
.

We recall the
\ell
-mesh from
Definition

2.2
. Take any
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the same as in the upper bound, we consider the restriction of the particle configuration
{\bm{y}}(\beta\ell)
on the interval
[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell)

\displaystyle y_{i-1}(\beta\ell)<\alpha\ell\leqslant y_{i}(\beta\ell)<\cdots<y_{j}(\beta\ell)<(\alpha+1)\ell\leqslant y_{j+1}(\beta\ell).

(3.13)

Denote the index set
I(\alpha,\beta)=\{i,i+1,i+2,\cdots,j\}
. For any
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
in
{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})
, its restriction on the index set
I(\alpha,\beta):=n
and time interval
[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]
,
\{x_{k}(t)\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}
form an
n
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walk inside
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
.
The shifted particle configuration

\displaystyle y_{k+i-1}(t+\beta\ell)-\alpha\ell,\quad 1\leqslant k\leqslant n,

(3.14)

form an
n
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walk on the parallelogram shaped region

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}=\{x,t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,y_{i}(t+\beta\ell)-\alpha\ell\leqslant x\leqslant y_{j+1}(t+\beta\ell)-\theta-\alpha\ell\}.

(3.15)

Fix a small parameter
0<\zeta\ll 1
satisfying (
3.1
), we define the sub-region for slopes
{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}=\{(u,v)\in{\mathcal{T}}:\zeta<u,-v,u+v\leqslant 1-\zeta\}.

(3.16)

To obtain a large deviation lower bound, we will restrict to the following set of
N
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walks
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
: for any
\alpha,\beta
,

1.

If on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the height function
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
(recall from (
3.16
)) and error
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon/\ell
in the sense of
Lemma

2.3
. We denote the shifted particle configuration
\{z_{k}(t)\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n,0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}

\displaystyle z_{k}(t)=y_{k+i-1}(t+\beta\ell)-\alpha\ell,\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,\quad 1\leqslant k\leqslant n.

(3.17)

Then
z_{k}(t)
form an
n
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli walk inside
\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}
from (
3.15
). Recall that
{\mathcal{A}}
is the height function with slope given by
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
from
Definition

2.4
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}
is a linear approximation of the height function of
\{z_{k}(t)\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n,0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
, with
L_{\infty}
distance bounded by
C\varepsilon
.

We restrict to
{\bm{x}}(t)
such that the height function
{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}
of
\{x_{k+i-1}(t+\beta\ell)-\alpha\ell\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n,0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
satisfies

\displaystyle(x_{k+i-1}(t+\beta\ell)-\alpha\ell,t)\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}},\quad\left|{\mathcal{H}}^{\rm s}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon,\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell.

(3.18)

2.

Otherwise, we take

\displaystyle\{x_{k}(t)\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}=\{y_{k}(t)\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}.

(3.19)

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
N
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walks
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
satisfying (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), its height function
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{H}}-H^{*}\|\leqslant C\varepsilon.

(3.20)

Proof.

For any
\alpha,\beta
, we recall the index set
I(\alpha,\beta)=\{i,i+1,i+2,\cdots,j\}
from (
3.13
).
There are two cases. If on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, then for
\beta\ell\leqslant t\leqslant(\beta+1)\ell
and
y_{i}(t)\leqslant x\leqslant y_{j}(t)
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\varrho(x-y_{i}(t))-\varrho v(t-\beta\ell)=H^{*}(x,t)-H^{*}(y_{i}(t),\beta\ell)+\OO(\varepsilon)\\
&\quad=H^{*}(x,t)-{\mathcal{H}}(y_{i}(t),\beta\ell)+\OO(\varepsilon)=H^{*}(x,t)-i\theta/N+\OO(\varepsilon)\,,\end{split}

(3.21)

where we used (
3.12
).
And using (
3.18
), the same argument as in (
3.7
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-i\theta/N=\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(y_{i}(t),\beta\ell)=\varrho(x-y_{i}(t))-\varrho v(t-\beta\ell)+\OO(\varepsilon).\end{split}

(3.22)

The claim (
3.20
) follows from combining (
3.21
) and (
3.22
).

In the second case that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, either
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, or
H^{*}
does not have a linear approximation. Then our construction gives

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)={\mathcal{H}}(x,t)=H^{*}(x,t)+\OO(\varepsilon),

and (
3.20
) holds trivially.

∎

The large deviation lower bound in
Theorem

1.6
is a consequence of the following statement. We postpone its proof in
Section

5
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Adopt notations from
Theorem

1.6
. Fix any
H^{*}\in\Adm({\mathfrak{R}},h)
, and denote
\{{\bm{y}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
the particle configuration constructed in
Lemma

2.1
. There exists a constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\sum\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i\neq j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\geqslant-C,

(3.23)

where the summation is over
\{{x}_{k}(t)={\mathsf{x}}_{k}(Nt)/N\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}
such that (
3.18
) holds.
