If we assume that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the height function
H^{*}
has a linear approximation with slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
(recall from (
3.16
)) and error
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon/\ell
in the sense of
Lemma

2.3
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\sum\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i\neq j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\\
&\geqslant\frac{1}{\theta}\iint_{[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO\left(\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)}{\ell}\right),\end{split}

(3.24)

where the summation is over
\{x_{k}(t)={\mathsf{x}}_{k}(Nt)/N\}_{k\in I(\alpha,\beta),t\in[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}
such that (
3.18
) holds.

Proof of Large Deviation Lower bound in
Theorem

1.6
.

Thanks to
Lemma

3.3
and
Proposition

3.5
, we can lower bound (
3.9
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\eqref{e:ldpup}

\displaystyle\geqslant-T\ln(2)+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\sum_{{\mathbb{B}}_{\varepsilon}(H^{*}:\{\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{{\mathsf{t}}\in\{0,{\mathsf{L}},2{\mathsf{L}},\cdots,{\mathsf{T}}\}})}\prod_{\alpha,\beta}\prod_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{i,j\in I(\alpha,\beta):i<j}\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{T}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{T}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)}\right)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0})

\displaystyle=-T\ln(2)+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\int_{[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{T}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{T}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)}\right)

\displaystyle+\OO\left(\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)}{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\iint_{\mathfrak{R}}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\frac{1}{2\theta}\left.\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x,t){\rm d}h(x,t)\right|_{0}^{T}-T\ln(2)

\displaystyle+\OO\left(\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)}{\ell}\right),

where in the second equality, we used that the number of
(\alpha,\beta)
such that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H^{*}
does not have a linear approximation is bounded by
\varepsilon_{0}
; in the last line we used
Lemma

2.7
. The large deviation lower bound in
Theorem

1.6
follows by using the choice of parameters from (
3.1
).

∎

4
Large Deviation Upper Bound: Constant Slope Case

In this section we study the following
n
-particle non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk from time
0
to
{\mathsf{L}}=\ell N

\big(\bm{\mathsf{x}}(0),\bm{\mathsf{x}}(1),\ldots,\bm{\mathsf{{\mathsf{x}}}}({\mathsf{L}})\big)\in(\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta})^{{\mathsf{L}}}
(see
Definition

1.1
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})},\quad 0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}.

(4.1)

Then we will prove
Proposition

3.2
, the large deviation upper bound for the non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks with height function approximately linear.

We recall the height function
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)
with constant slope
\nabla{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
from
Definition

2.4
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=(x-tv)\varrho,\quad(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{P}};\quad{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=0,\quad x\leqslant tv;\quad{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=\ell\varrho,\quad x\geqslant\ell+tv.

(4.2)

where
{\mathfrak{P}}
is the parallelogram region

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}:=\{x,t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,\quad tv\leqslant x\leqslant\ell+tv\},

(4.3)

We assume that the density satisfies (
3.3
)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\theta n}{N}-\ell\varrho\right|\leqslant C\varepsilon.

(4.4)

Fix a small parameter
0<\zeta\ll 1
satisfying (
3.1
), we recall
{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
from (
3.16
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}=\{(u,v)\in{\mathcal{T}}:\zeta<u,-v,u+v\leqslant 1-\zeta\}.

There are two cases,

1.

Extreme slope case where
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
.

2.

Interior slope case where
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
.

The following proposition gives the large deviation upper bound for
n
-particle non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walks with height function having approximately constant slope. Its proofs are given in
Section

4.2
and
Section

4.4
.

Proposition 4.1
.

We recall the rate function
\sigma
from (
1.14
), and parameters
\zeta,\delta,\varepsilon,\ell
from (
3.1
).
Fix
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
such that (
4.4
) holds. Take the height function
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)
with constant slope
\nabla{\mathcal{A}}=(\varrho,-\varrho v)
on the parallelogram region
{\mathfrak{P}}
, as in (
4.2
). The height function
{\mathcal{H}}
of the
n
-particle non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk (
4.1
) satisfies

1.

If
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)=-\frac{n}{\ell N}\ln(2)+\OO(\zeta^{1/2}\ln(1/\zeta)).

(4.5)

2.

If
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\leqslant\frac{\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)}{\theta}-\frac{n}{\ell N}\ln(2)+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+(\delta/\ell)\log^{2}(\ell/\delta)\right).

Proof of
Proposition

3.2
.

The first statement (
3.10
) in
Proposition

3.2
follows from
Lemma

2.6
.

For the second statement (
3.11
), we recall from
Lemma

2.3
that at least
1-\varepsilon_{0}
fraction of the points
x
of
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the gradient
\nabla H^{*}
exists and is within
\varepsilon_{0}
of
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
. As a consequence,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}\iint_{[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0}).

(4.6)

If
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, then the claim (
3.11
) follows from (
4.6
) and the second statement of
Proposition

4.1
.
If
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\notin{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, without loss of generality we assume that
\varrho\leqslant\zeta
. The Lobachevsky function (recall from (
1.14
)) is
\pi
-periodic, namely
L(x+\pi)=L(x)
and
-L(\pi-x)=L(x)
. Moreover, because of the logarithmic singularity, the Lobachevsky function satisfies

\displaystyle|L(x)-L(y)|\lesssim|x-y|\log(1/|x-y|).

(4.7)

and

L(\zeta)=\OO(\zeta\log(1/\zeta))
for
\zeta\rightarrow 0+
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)=-L(\varrho)+L(\varrho v)+L((1-v)\varrho)=\OO(\zeta\ln(1/\zeta)).

(4.8)

The claim (
3.11
) follows from (
4.5
)
and (
4.8
).
∎

4.1
Outline of the proof of
Proposition

4.1

For the extreme slope case, to estimate
\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)
, we will directly upper bound the number of height profiles satisfying
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
by a combinatorics argument, and the probability
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{H}})
for each of these height profile by a delicate analysis of Vandermonde determinants. It turns out both parts are negligible. The proof is given in
Section

4.2
.

For the interior slope case, we recall the constant slope height function
{\mathcal{A}}
from (
4.2
). We convolve it with a Cauchy distribution. Take small
\delta>0
, denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(x,t)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int\frac{\delta{\mathcal{A}}(y,t){\rm d}y}{(y-x)^{2}+\delta^{2}}.

(4.9)

Since for
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{P}}
,
\nabla{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, namely,

\zeta\leqslant\varrho,\varrho v,\varrho(1-v)\leqslant 1-\zeta,

it follows that
\nabla\widetilde{H}(x,t)\in{\mathcal{T}}
for
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. Properties of the smoothed height function
\widetilde{H}(x,t)
are collected in
Section

4.3
.

We define the associated
complex slope

\widetilde{f}:{\mathfrak{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{-}
by setting, for any
(x,t)\in{\mathfrak{R}}
,
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=\widetilde{f}(x,t)\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
to be the unique complex number satisfying

\displaystyle\arg^{*}\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=-\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t);\qquad\arg^{*}\big(\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)+1\big)=\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t),

(4.10)

where for any
z\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
we have set
\arg^{*}z=\theta\in(-\pi,0)
to be the unique number in
(-\pi,0)
satisfying
e^{-\mathrm{i}\theta}z\in\mathbb{R}
; see
Figure

3
for a depiction. By the law of sines (
4.10
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{|\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)|}{\sin(-\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))}=\frac{|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)|}{\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t))}=\frac{1}{\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))}.

(4.11)

From (
4.11
), we can solve the complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}\exp(\ln\sin(-\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))),\\
&1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}\exp(\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))).\end{split}

(4.12)

Figure 3:
Shown above is the complex slope
\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{f}(x,t)
.

We take a drift function
g_{t}(x)
given by

\displaystyle g_{t}(x)=\ln\sin(-\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\Hib(\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)).

(4.13)

As we will show in
Section

4.3
, the complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
, and the drift function
g_{t}(x)
can both be extended analytically to a strip neighborhood of the real axis, and on this strip region they are related by

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)=e^{\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)},\quad\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t){\rm d}x}{z-x}.

To prove the large deviation upper bound for the interior case in
Proposition

3.2
, we
tilt the law of the Markov process (
4.1
) by an exponential martingale.
For any
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}-1
, let

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{g}:=\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t))-\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t))\right|{\bm{x}}(t)\right]\right),\quad t={\mathsf{t}}/N,

and the following is a Martingale

\displaystyle\prod_{t\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]}\frac{e^{N\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{g}}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{N\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{g}}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}.

(4.14)

In particular it gives the following identity

\displaystyle 1=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]/N}\frac{e^{N\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{g}}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{N\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}^{g}}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]/N}\frac{e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}\right].

(4.15)

The large deviation upper bound follows from restricting the above identity to the event
\{\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\}
.

\displaystyle 1\geqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]/N}\frac{e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}\bm{1}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\right].

(4.16)

In
Section

4.4
, we will show that on the event,
\{\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\}
, up to a small error, the numerator in the exponential martingale (
4.14
) can be written in terms of
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)
. For the denominator, we will use the dynamical loop equation
Theorem

A.5
to compute its expectation. They together lead to the following large deviation upper bound.

Proposition 4.2
.

Adopt notations and assumptions from
Proposition

4.1
. Take the height function
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)
with constant slope
\nabla{\mathcal{A}}=(\varrho,-\varrho v)
on the parallelogram region
{\mathfrak{P}}
, as in (
4.2
).
If
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
, then the height function
{\mathcal{H}}
of the
n
-particle non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk (
4.1
) satisfies

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\leqslant-\frac{S({\mathcal{A}};g)}{\theta\ell^{2}}+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta\zeta^{2}}\right),

where
g_{t}(x)
is the drift function from (
4.13
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}S({\mathcal{A}};g)&=-\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}t,\end{split}

(4.17)

where the contour
\omega
encloses
[-3\ell,3\ell]
and
m^{*}_{t}
is the Stieltjes transform of
\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)
,

\displaystyle m^{*}_{t}(z)=\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t){\rm d}x}{x-z}.

(4.18)

We will then analyze the rate function
S({\mathcal{A}};g)
in (
4.17
) in
Section

4.5
. Miraculously, after simplifying the rate function, we recover these
Lobachevsky functions (see (
1.14
)).

Proposition 4.3
.

Adopt the notations and assumptions in
Proposition

4.2
.
The rate function satisfies

\displaystyle S({\mathcal{A}};g)=-\ell^{2}(\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)-\varrho\ln(2))+\OO(\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\ell).

(4.19)

4.2
Proof of
Proposition

4.1
: Extreme Slope Case

There are three cases for the extreme slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
(recall from (
3.16
)): i)
-\varrho v\leqslant\zeta
ii)
\varrho+\varrho v\geqslant 1-\zeta
iii)
\varrho\geqslant 1-\zeta
. We recall from (
3.1
) that
\varepsilon\ll\zeta\ell
.

We start with the first two cases. Their proofs are the same, so we will only give the proof of the first case.

Proof of the First and Second Cases.

In the first case
-\zeta\leqslant\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}\leqslant 0
, and most of
e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})
are zero.
In fact, we have by integration by parts

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\theta}{N^{2}}\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\sum_{1\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})&=\frac{\theta}{N^{2}}\left(\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\varrho_{\ell}(x;{\bm{x}}(\ell)){\rm d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}x\varrho_{0}(x;{\bm{x}}(0)){\rm d}x\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{H}}(x,0)-{\mathcal{H}}(x,\ell)){\rm d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{A}}(x,0)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,\ell)){\rm d}x+\OO(\varepsilon\ell)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\ell}-\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t){\rm d}t{\rm d}x+\OO(\varepsilon\ell)=\OO(\zeta\ell^{2}+\varepsilon\ell)=\OO(\zeta\ell^{2})\,,\end{split}

(4.20)

where we used that
\varepsilon\ll\zeta\ell
from (
3.1
).
We denote the time set

\displaystyle I=\{1\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}:\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\leqslant\zeta^{1/2}\ell N\},\quad I^{\complement}=\{1\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}:\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})>\zeta^{1/2}\ell N\}.

(4.21)

Then (
4.20
) gives that
|I^{\complement}|=\OO(\zeta^{1/2}\ell N)
. There are
{\ell N\choose\OO(\zeta^{1/2}\ell N)}=e^{\OO(-\zeta^{1/2}\ln(\zeta)(\ell N))}
ways to choose the set
I^{\complement}
. Recall the definition (
1.12
) of the weight
{\mathcal{W}}
. Once we fix the sets
I,I^{\complement}
, the total weights of non-intersecting Bernoulli walks satisfying (
4.21
) is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{{\mathsf{p}}\text{ satisfies }\eqref{e:defI}}{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}})&\leqslant\prod_{{\mathsf{t}}\in I}\sum_{\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\leqslant\zeta^{1/2}\ell N}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V({\bm{x}}({\mathsf{t}}))}\prod_{{\mathsf{t}}\in I^{\complement}}\sum_{{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})+{\theta}{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V({\bm{x}}({\mathsf{t}}))}\\
&\leqslant\left(\sum_{k\leqslant\zeta^{1/2}\ell N}{n\choose k}\right)^{|I|}2^{n|I^{\complement}|}=e^{\OO(-\zeta^{1/2}\ln(\zeta)(\ell N)^{2})},\end{split}

(4.22)

where we used that

\displaystyle\sum_{\sum_{i}e_{i}=k}\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\theta}{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}={n\choose k}\leqslant n^{k},

from
Lemma

2.5
.
It follows from (
4.22
) and the fact that there at most
e^{\OO(-\zeta^{1/2}\ln(\zeta)(\ell N))}
ways to choose the set
I^{\complement}
, we conclude (
4.5
) for
-\varrho v\leqslant\zeta
.

In the second case that
1-\zeta\leqslant\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}+\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}\leqslant 1
, we have that most of
e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})
are one. By the same argument as the first case, we will have that (
4.5
) holds.
∎

For the last case, we have
1-\zeta\leqslant\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}\leqslant 1
, and
(1-\zeta)\ell N\leqslant n\leqslant\ell N
, we need to bound the total weight of the paths
{\mathsf{p}}
corresponding to
{\mathcal{H}}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathsf{p}}:\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon}{\mathcal{W}}({\mathsf{p}})=\sum_{{\mathsf{p}}:\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon}\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}.

(4.23)

We remark that the normalization constant
2^{-n{\mathsf{L}}}
gives the term
-(n/\ell N)\ln 2
in (
4.5
).
We denote the index sets

\displaystyle I_{0}=[\![{1,\zeta n}]\!],\quad I_{1}=[\![{\zeta n,n-\zeta n}]\!],\quad I_{2}=[\![{n-\zeta n,n}]\!].

(4.24)

For
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant\zeta\ell
, we have that for any index
i\in I_{1}
,
x_{i}(t)\in[tv,\ell+tv]
.
Fix any time
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
. For
i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2}
, there are
2^{|I_{0}|+|I_{2}|}=2^{2\zeta\ell N}
possible choices for
e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})
. For the particle configuration
\{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1}}
, using again that
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant\zeta\ell
, the number of empty sites is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1}}(({\mathsf{x}}_{i+1}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{i+1}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-\theta)=\OO(\zeta\ell N+\varepsilon N)=\OO(\zeta\ell N).

Therefore, the number of configurations
{\mathsf{p}}
satisfying
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
is at most

\displaystyle 2^{2\zeta N}{\ell N\choose\OO(\zeta\ell N)}=e^{\OO(-\zeta\ln(\zeta)\ell N)}.

(4.25)

The following lemma bounds each summand in (
4.23
)

Lemma 4.4
.

For non-intersecting Bernoulli walks
{\mathsf{p}}=\{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
satisfying that
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
, the following holds

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}=e^{\OO(\ell N\zeta\ln\frac{1}{\zeta})}\left(\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}\right)^{\theta}.

(4.26)

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we will omit the time
{\mathsf{t}}
dependence. We recall from (
2.8
)

\displaystyle\left|\ln\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+\theta e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}-\theta\ln\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}\right|\leqslant C\frac{(e_{i}-e_{j})^{2}}{({{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}})^{2}}.

(4.27)

Next, we show that for
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{(e_{i}-e_{j})^{2}}{({{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}})^{2}}=\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}\frac{1}{({{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}})^{2}}=\OO(\zeta\ln(\ell/\zeta)\ell N).

Let
I=\{i\in[\![{1,n}]\!]:e_{i}=1\}
and
J=\{j\in[\![{1,n}]\!]:e_{j}=0\}
.
The particle configuration
\{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
consists of at most
\OO(\zeta\ell N)
tightly packed pieces. Namely, there exists
1=i_{0}<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{K}=n+1
with
K=\OO(\zeta\ell N)
, such that for any
i_{k}\leqslant i<j<i_{k+1}
,
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}={\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta(j-i)
. Denote the interval
T_{k}=[\![{i_{k},i_{k+1}-1}]\!]
. Then either
I\cap T_{k}=\emptyset
or
I\cap T_{k}=\{i_{k},i_{k}+1,\cdots,j_{k}\}
for some
i_{k}\leqslant j_{k}<i_{k+1}
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i\in I\cap T_{k},j\in J}\frac{1}{|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}|^{2}}&\leqslant\sum_{i\in I\cap T_{k}}\sum_{d\geqslant 1+\min\{|i-j_{k}|,|i-i_{k}|\}}\frac{1}{(d\theta)^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{i\in I\cap T_{k}}\frac{1}{\theta^{2}(1+\min\{|i-j_{k}|,|i-i_{k}|\})}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}(1+\ln({1+i_{k}-j_{k}})).\end{split}

(4.28)

We can then sum over all the intervals
T_{k}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}\frac{1}{|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\sum_{i\in I\cap T_{k},j\in J}\frac{1}{|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}|^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}(1+\ln({1+i_{k}-j_{k}}))

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{K}{\theta^{2}}\left(1+\ln\left(\frac{\sum_{k}{1+i_{k}-j_{k}}}{K}\right)\right)\lesssim\frac{K\ln(\ell N/K)}{\theta^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\zeta\ell N\ln(1/\zeta)}{\theta^{2}},

where for the second statement we used (
4.28
); the third statement we used Jensen’s inequality; the last two statements we used that
\sum_{k}(1+i_{k}-j_{k})\leqslant K+\ell N
.
∎

In the following we prove the third case, when
\varrho\geqslant 1-\zeta
.

Proof of the Third Case.

Plugging (
4.26
) into (
4.23
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}\\
&=e^{\OO(-\zeta\ln(\zeta){\mathsf{L}}\ell N)}\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}\left(\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{i}({\mathsf{t}}))-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})+e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}}\right)^{\theta}\\
&=e^{\OO(-\zeta\ln(\zeta)(\ell N)^{2})}\left(\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}}))}{V({\mathsf{x}}(0))}\right)^{\theta}.\end{split}

Finally we prove the following bound on the ratio of the Vandermonde determinant

\displaystyle\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}}))}{V({\mathsf{x}}(0))}\leqslant e^{\OO(\zeta(\ell N)^{2})}.

(4.29)

The claim (
4.5
) follows from plugging (
1.12
), (
4.29
) into (
4.23
), and using (
4.25
).
To prove (
4.29
), we recall the three index sets
I_{0},I_{1},I_{2}
from (
4.24
).
It follows from showing that

\displaystyle\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}}))}{V({\mathsf{x}}(0))}\leqslant e^{\OO(\zeta(\ell N)^{2})}\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}})|_{I_{1}})}{V({\mathsf{x}}(0)|_{I_{1}})},

(4.30)

the relation
V(\theta,2\theta,\cdots,|I_{1}|\theta)\leqslant V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}})|_{I_{1}})
, and

\displaystyle\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}})|_{I_{1}})}{V(\theta,2\theta,\cdots,|I_{1}|\theta)}=e^{\OO(\zeta(\ell N)^{2})}.

(4.31)

The claim (
4.30
) follows from the following one time estimate

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}}))}{V({\mathsf{x}}(0))}\frac{V({\mathsf{x}}(0)|_{I_{1}})}{V({\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}})|_{I_{1}})}=\prod_{(i,j)\notin I_{1}\times I_{1}}\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{L}})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)}}}\leqslant\phantom{{}={}}\prod_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in[\![{1,n}]\!]\atop i<j}\left(1+\frac{2{\mathsf{L}}}{|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)|}}\right)

(4.32)

\displaystyle\leqslant\prod_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in[\![{1,n}]\!]\atop i<j}\left(1+\frac{2{\mathsf{L}}}{|i-j|\theta}\right)\leqslant\prod_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2}}\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant\ell N}\left(\frac{3\ell N}{k\theta}\right)^{2}=e^{\OO((|I_{0}|+|I_{2}|)\ell N)}=e^{\OO(\zeta(\ell N)^{2})},

(4.33)

where we used that
|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)|\geqslant|i-j|\theta
and
n\leqslant\ell N
.
Next we prove (
4.31
). Since
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
and
1-\zeta\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}\leqslant 1
, we have that up to some shift
{\mathsf{x}}({\mathsf{L}})|_{I_{1}}
is obtained from the particle configuration
\theta,2\theta,\cdots,|I_{1}|\theta
by adding at most
\OO(\zeta\ell N)
empty sites. Next we show that adding an empty site increases the Vandermonde determinant by at most a factor
e^{\OO(\ell N)}
, then (
4.31
) follows. Say from the configuration
x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{|I_{1}|}
, we add an empty site between
x_{k}
and
x_{k+1}
, and get the particle configuration
x_{1}+1>x_{2}+1>\cdots>x_{k}+1>x_{k+1}>\cdots x_{|I_{1}|}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{V(x_{1}+1,\cdots,x_{k}+1,x_{k+1},\cdots x_{|I_{1}|})}{V(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{|I_{1}|})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\leqslant k,j\geqslant k+1}\left(1+\frac{1}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\right)\leqslant\prod_{i\leqslant k,j\geqslant k+1}\left(1+\frac{1}{|i-j|\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\sum_{i\leqslant k,j\geqslant k+1}\frac{1}{|i-j|\theta}}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{\theta}\left(\frac{1}{1}+\frac{2}{2}+\cdots+\frac{\ell N}{\ell N}\right)}=e^{\ell N/\theta},

where we used that the multiset
\{|i-j|:i\leqslant k,j\geqslant k+1\}
contains at most
m
copies of
m
for any
1\leqslant m\leqslant\ell N
.
∎

4.3
Complex Slope Estimates

In this section, we prove that the complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
and the drift function
g_{t}(x)
(as constructed in (
4.10
) and (
4.13
)) can be extended analytically to a strip neighborhood of the real axis in
Lemma

4.7
. This step is crucial to use the dynamical loop equations, since for instance
Theorem

A.3
requires the functions
\phi^{\pm}
to be holomorphic.

In
Lemma

4.5
and
Lemma

4.6
, we collect some estimates of the smoothed height function
\widetilde{H}(x,t)
(as constructed in (
4.9
)) . Their proofs follow from explicit computations, so we postpone them to
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix any
0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
,
\delta\ll\ell
, and denote

\displaystyle\kappa_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{\delta{\rm d}y}{(y-x)^{2}+\delta^{2}},

(4.34)

then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)=\varrho\kappa_{t}(x),\quad\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t)=-\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x).\end{split}

(4.35)

The function
\kappa_{t}(x)\in[0,1]
and for
x\in[tv,\ell+tv]
, it holds

\displaystyle\kappa_{t}(x)\asymp 1,\quad 1-\kappa_{t}(x)\asymp\frac{C\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})},

for
x\not\in[tv,\ell+tv]
, it holds

\displaystyle 1-\kappa_{t}(x)\asymp 1,\quad\kappa_{t}(x)\asymp\frac{C\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})^{2}/\ell}.

(4.36)

We recall that the Hilbert transform
\Hib(u)
of a function
u:{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
is given by
\Hib(u)(t)=\PV\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x)/(t-x){\rm d}x
where
\PV
denotes the principal value.

Lemma 4.6
.

There exists a large constant
C>0
, for any small
0<\delta\ll\ell
, recall
\kappa_{t}(x)
from (
4.34
), the following holds

1.

\kappa_{t}(x)
can be extended analytically to any
z=x+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\eta]\leqslant\delta/2
, and

\displaystyle|\kappa_{t}(z)-\kappa_{t}(x)|\leqslant\frac{C\eta}{\delta}\min\{\kappa_{t}(x),1-\kappa_{t}(x)\}.

(4.37)

2.

The Hilbert transform
\Hib(\kappa_{t})
extends analytically to any
z=x+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\eta]\leqslant\delta/2
,

\displaystyle\left|\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z)\right|\leqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{C\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}&\text{if $\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\ell$}\\
\ln(\ell/\delta)+C&\text{for all $z$}\end{array}\right.,\quad\left|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z)\right|\leqslant\frac{C\eta}{\delta}.

If
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\gtrsim\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\kappa_{t}(z)\lesssim\frac{\delta\ell}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})^{3}},\quad\Hib(\kappa_{t}(z)\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}.

(4.40)

We recall the complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
and drift function
g_{t}(x)
from
(
4.12
) and (
4.13
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}\exp(\ln\sin(-\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))),\\
&g_{t}(x)=\ln\sin(-\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\Hib(\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)).\end{split}

(4.41)

The following lemma states that the complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
and the drift function
g_{t}(x)
can be extended analytically to a strip neighborhood of the real axis. Its proof follows from explicit computations, so we postpone it to
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 4.7
.

There exists a large constant
C>0
, for any
0<\delta\ll\ell
,
\zeta\ll 1
, extreme slope
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\not\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
(recall from (
3.16
)),
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
and
g_{t}(x)
as in (
4.41
) can be extended analytically to the strip region

{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{D}}(C)=\{x+\mathrm{i}\eta:|\eta|\leqslant C^{-1}\delta\}
, and for any
z\in{\mathcal{D}}
the following holds

1.

The norm of
\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)
satisfies:
|\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|\leqslant C/\zeta
.

2.

The norm of
g_{t}(z)
satisfies:
|g_{t}(z)|\leqslant\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta)+C
. If
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}=\ln\frac{\sin(v)}{\sin(1-v)}+\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right).

(4.42)

The imaginary part of
g_{t}(z)
satisfies:
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[g_{t}(z)]|\leqslant C\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]/\delta
.

3.

The derivative of
g_{t}(x)
satisfies: for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
|\partial_{x}g_{t}(x)|,|\delta_{t}g_{t}(x)|\leqslant C/\delta
.

4.4
Proof of
Proposition

4.2
: Interior Slope Case

In this section we prove
Proposition

4.2
, which implies
Proposition

3.2
for the interior slope case. It is a consequence of the following two lemmas.

Lemma 4.8
.

Adopt the notations and assumptions in
Proposition

4.2
.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be a height function with
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
, and denote
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
the particle configuration associated with
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}({\mathsf{t}}/N))}=-\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO(N(\|g\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{t}g\|_{\infty}))\\
&=-\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO((\varepsilon/\delta)(\ell N)^{2}+\varepsilon(\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta))\ell N^{2}).\end{split}

(4.43)

Lemma 4.9
.

Adopt the notations and assumptions in
Proposition

4.2
.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
be a height function with
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
, and denote
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
the particle configuration associated with
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
, then for any
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}=tN\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}-1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t)/N)}|{\bm{x}}(t)]&=\frac{N}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau+\OO\left(1\right)\\
&=\frac{N}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m_{t}^{*}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon\ell N}{\delta\zeta^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(4.44)

where
\omega
is a contour enclosing the interval
[-3\ell,3\ell]
,
m^{*}_{t}(z)
is defined in (
4.18
) and
m_{t}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of the empirical measure of
{\bm{x}}(t)
,

\displaystyle m_{t}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\rho(x;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}x}{x-z}.

Proof of
Proposition

4.2
.

Recall from (
4.16
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]/N}\frac{e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}\bm{1}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\right]\leqslant 1.

(4.45)

Proposition

4.2
follows from plugging
Lemma

4.8
and
Lemma

4.9
into (
4.45
), the error term simplifies using the relation (
3.1
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.8
.

Let
G_{t}(z)=\int_{0}^{z}g_{t}(x)dx
. We first notice that, for
t={\mathsf{t}}/N
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\rho(x;{\bm{x}}(t+1/N))-\rho(x;{\bm{x}}(t/N)))G_{t}(x){\rm d}x\\
&=\sum_{i}\frac{Ne_{i}({\mathsf{t}})}{\theta}\int_{x_{i}(t)}^{x_{i}(t)+\theta/N}(G_{t}(x+1/N)-G_{t}(x)){\rm d}x=\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t))+\OO\left(\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{t}g\|_{\infty}\right).\end{split}

Thus for the lefthand side of (
4.43
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}({\mathsf{t}})/N)}\\
&=\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t+1/N)-\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t))G_{t}(x){\rm d}x+\OO\left(N\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}\right)\\
&=-\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{H}}(x,t+1/N)-{\mathcal{H}}(x,t))g_{t}(x){\rm d}x+\OO\left(N\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}\right)\\
&=-\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{t}^{t+1/N}\partial_{s}{\mathcal{H}}(x,s){\rm d}sg_{t}(x){\rm d}x+\OO\left(N\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}\right)\\
&=-\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO(N(\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{t}g\|_{\infty})).\end{split}

(4.46)

This gives the first statement in (
4.43
).
Next we replace
{\mathcal{H}}
by
{\mathcal{A}}
, we can do an integration by part, use
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
, and that integration holds in the parallelogram
[0,\ell]\times[tv,tv+\ell]
, to deduce that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial_{t}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t))g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|\\
&\leqslant\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\left|\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t))g_{t}(x){\rm d}x\right|_{0}^{\ell}\right|+\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\left|\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t))\partial_{t}g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon\|g\|_{\infty}\ell N^{2}+\varepsilon\|\partial_{t}g\|_{\infty}(\ell N)^{2}.\end{split}

(4.47)

Thanks to
Lemma

4.7
, we have
\|g\|_{\infty}\leqslant\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta)+C
and
|\partial_{t}g_{t}(x)|\leqslant C/\delta
, and the second statement of (
4.43
) follows from (
4.47
).

∎

In the following, we prove
Lemma

4.9
. For simplicity of notations, we will omit the dependence on time
t
.
We can rewrite the lefthand side of (
4.44
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)}|{\bm{x}}]&=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\tau}\ln\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}\tau g(x_{i}/N)}|{\bm{x}}]{\rm d}\tau\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}\tau g(x_{i}/N)}|{\bm{x}}]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}\tau g(x_{i}/N)}|{\bm{x}}]}{\rm d}\tau,\end{split}

(4.48)

which is the expectation of
\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)
under the following deformed measure

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{\tau}}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+\theta{\bm{e}}/N)}{V({\bm{x}})}e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}\tau g(x_{i}/N)},

(4.49)

where
Z_{\tau}
is the normalization factor. We denote
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}[\cdot]
the expectation with respect to the measure (
4.49
).

We will use the dynamical loop equation
Theorem

A.3
and
Theorem

A.5
to compute the expectation

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}\tau g(x_{i}/N)}|{\bm{x}}]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}\tau g(x_{i}/N)}|{\bm{x}}]}=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\left[\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)\right].

Take a large constant
8C/(\delta\zeta)\leqslant K\leqslant\zeta/(2\varepsilon)
, where the constant
C>0
is from
Lemma

4.7
. This is possible thanks to our choice of parameters from (
3.1
).
We denote the annulus region

\displaystyle\Lambda=\Lambda_{K}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:(1/K)\leqslant\dist(z,[-3\ell,3\ell])\leqslant 2/K\}.

(4.50)

We need to verify the conditon that on
\Lambda
, the following are well defined, and lower and upper bounded

\displaystyle\ln(1+f^{(\tau)}(z)),\quad f^{(\tau)}(z):=e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}.

Lemma 4.10
.

Take
8C/(\delta\zeta)\leqslant K\leqslant\zeta/(2\varepsilon)
, then on
\Lambda
from (
4.50
), we have

\displaystyle\sin\left(\frac{\zeta\pi}{4}\right)\leqslant|1+f^{(\tau)}(z)|\lesssim\frac{\ell^{2}K}{\delta\zeta},\quad\frac{|f^{(\tau)}(z)|}{|1+f^{(\tau)}(z)|}\lesssim\frac{1}{\zeta},

(4.51)

and
\ln(1+f^{(\tau)}(z))
is well defined.

Proof.

We first show that for any
z\in\Lambda

\displaystyle-\pi(1-\zeta/2)\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]\leqslant\pi(1-\zeta/2).

(4.52)

Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)]=-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(\overline{z})]
, we will only prove (
4.52
) for
z\in\Lambda\cap\overline{\mathbb{H}}
. Thanks to
Lemma

4.5
,
{\mathcal{H}}(\pm 3\ell,t)={\mathcal{A}}(\pm 3\ell,t)
. Therefore, for any
z\in\Lambda
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}|m(z)-m^{*}(z)|&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{(\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)){\rm d}x}{x-z}\right|\leqslant\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}\frac{|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)|{\rm d}x}{|x-z|^{2}}\\
&\leqslant\varepsilon\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}x}{x^{2}+(1/K)^{2}}=\pi K\varepsilon\leqslant\zeta\pi/2,\end{split}

(4.53)

where in the last inequality we used that
K\leqslant\zeta/(2\varepsilon)
.
We can also compute
m^{*}(z)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m^{*}(z)]
explicitly

\displaystyle m^{*}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}{\rm d}x}{z-x}=\int_{tv\leqslant x\leqslant\ell+tv}\frac{\varrho{\rm d}x}{z-x}=-\varrho\ln\left(1-\frac{\ell}{z-tv}\right).

(4.54)

and

\displaystyle-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m^{*}(z)]=\eta\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}{\rm d}x}{|z-x|^{2}}=\eta\int_{tv\leqslant x\leqslant 1+tv}\frac{\varrho{\rm d}x}{|z-x|^{2}}\in[0,\pi\varrho]\subset[0,(1-\zeta)\pi].

(4.55)

The claim (
4.52
) follows from combining (
4.53
) and (
4.55
).
From
Lemma

4.7
,
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}g(z)|\leqslant C\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]/\delta\leqslant 2C/(\delta K)
.
Using (
4.52
), for any
z\in\Lambda
, we have
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}g(z)|\leqslant 2C/(\delta K)\leqslant\zeta\pi/4
, it follows that

\displaystyle-(1-\zeta/4)\pi\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[m(z)+\tau g(z)]\leqslant(1-\zeta/4)\pi.

It follows that

\arg e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}\in(-(1-\zeta/4)\pi,(1-\zeta/4)\pi).

Thus
1+e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}
takes value in the sector region given by
\{1+re^{\mathrm{i}u}:u\in(-(1-\zeta/4)\pi,(1-\zeta/4)\pi)\}
, which does not include the negative axis. Therefore, we can take
\ln(\cdot)
the branch defined on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}_{\leqslant 0}
and
\ln(1+e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)})
is well defined. Moreover

\displaystyle\min_{u\in(-(1-\zeta/4)\pi,(1-\zeta/4)\pi)}|1+re^{\mathrm{i}u}|\geqslant\sin\left(\frac{\zeta\pi}{4}\right),

we conclude that for
z\in\Lambda
, it holds
|1+e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}|\geqslant\sin(\zeta\pi/4)\gtrsim\zeta
. The last statement of (
4.51
) also follows.
On
\Lambda
, since
\dist(z,[-3\ell,3\ell])\geqslant 1/K
, we have

\displaystyle|e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}|\leqslant e^{\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}m^{*}(z)+|m^{*}(z)-m(z)|+\|g\|_{\infty}}\leqslant\left|1-\frac{\ell}{z-tv}\right|e^{\pi K\varepsilon+\|g\|_{\infty}}\leqslant(1+\ell K)e^{\pi K\varepsilon+\|g\|_{\infty}},

where we used (
4.54
) to bound
|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}m^{*}(z)|
and (
4.53
) to bound
|m(z)-m^{*}(z)|
. Using (
4.52
), for any
z\in\Lambda
, we have
|g(z)|\leqslant\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta)+C

thus
|1+e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}|\lesssim\ell^{2}K/(\zeta\delta)
. This finishes the proof of (
4.51
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.9
.

Lemma

4.10
with
K=8C/\delta\zeta
verifies that the measure (
4.49
) satisfies the assumption in
Theorem

A.5
(by taking
\varepsilon,n
there to be
1/N,n
here). Let
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}/N
. We conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{N}{\theta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{z-s}{\rm d}s=\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln(1+e^{m(w)+\tau g(w)}){\rm d}w}{(w-z)^{2}}+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),

(4.56)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[-3\ell,3\ell]
, but not
z
, the implicit constant in the error term depends on
K,\delta,\ell
. Moreover,
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
is a random variable with mean zero under
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}
.
By a contour
integral with respect to
G(z):=\int_{0}^{z}g(w){\rm d}w
on both sides and taking expectation we get

\displaystyle\frac{N}{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))G(s){\rm d}s=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m(w)+\tau g(w)})g(w){\rm d}w+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),

(4.57)

where
\omega
is a contour inside
\Lambda
. For the lefthand side of (
4.57
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{N}{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))G(s){\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\sum_{i}\frac{e_{i}}{\theta}\int_{x_{i}/N}^{(x_{i}+\theta)/N}(G(s+1/N)-G(s)){\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\left[\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)\right]+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right).

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\left[\sum_{i}e_{i}g(x_{i}/N)\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{\theta m(z)+\tau g(z)})g(z){\rm d}z+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right).

(4.58)

This gives the first statement in (
4.44
) by integrating from
\tau=0
to
\tau=1
and using (
4.48
).

Next we replace
m(z)
in the integral (
4.58
) by
m^{*}(z)
.
Thanks to (
4.53
), on the contour we have
|m(z)-m^{*}(z)|\leqslant\varepsilon K\pi\leqslant 1/2
, thus

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}|\ln(1+e^{m^{*}(z)+\tau g(z)})-\ln(1+e^{m(z)+\tau g(z)}))|\leqslant\left|\ln\left(1-\frac{(e^{m^{*}(z)-m(z)}-1)f^{(\tau)}(z)}{1+e^{m^{*}(z)+\tau g(z)}}\right)\right|\\
&\leqslant\left|\frac{(e^{m^{*}(z)-m(z)}-1)f^{(\tau)}(z)}{1+e^{m^{*}(z)+\tau g(z)}}\right|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon K}{\zeta}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta\zeta^{2}},\end{split}

(4.59)

where we used
Lemma

4.10
and take
K=8C/\delta\zeta
in the last inequality. The second statement in (
4.44
) follows by plugging (
4.59
) into (
4.58
), and noticing that the contour integral over
\omega
gives a factor
\OO(\ell)
.

∎

4.5
Rate Function and Proof of
Proposition

4.3

We recall the rate function
S({\mathcal{A}};g)
from (
4.17
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}S({\mathcal{A}};g)&=-\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}t,\end{split}

where the contour
\omega\in\Lambda
(from (
4.50
)) encloses
[-3\ell,3\ell]
.
In this section, we simplify
S({\mathcal{A}};g)
and prove
Proposition

4.3
.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\gtrsim\delta
, then

\displaystyle|\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z)|\lesssim\frac{\delta\varrho}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})},

Lemma 4.12
.

We introduce a new complex slope as
f^{*}_{t}(z)=e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
. Then for
x\in[tv,tv+\ell]
,

\displaystyle\arg f^{*}_{t}(x)=-\varrho\pi,\quad|\arg(1+f^{*}_{t}(x))+\varrho v\pi|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}.

(4.60)

Proof of
Lemma

4.11
.

The expressions of
m_{t}^{*}(x)
and
\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)
are explicit,

\displaystyle|\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z)|

\displaystyle=\varrho\left|\ln\left(1+\frac{\ell}{tv-z}\right)-\ln\left(1+\frac{\ell}{tv-z-\mathrm{i}\delta}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\varrho\left|\ln\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{i}\delta}{tv-z}\right)-\ln\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{i}\delta}{\ell+tv-z}\right)\right|,

(4.61)

from which the result follows from
|\ln(1+x)|\lesssim|x|
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.12
.

We recall
\kappa_{t}(x)
from (
4.34
),
g_{t}(x)
from (
4.13
), and
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=e^{\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)+g_{t}(x)}
from (
4.12
).
The three vertices
\{0,-1,\widetilde{f}_{t}\}
form a triangle, with three angles give by
\varrho\kappa_{t}(x)\pi,\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x)\pi,\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x)\pi
. From
Lemma

4.5
, for
x\in[tv,\ell+tv]
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{t}(x)\asymp 1,\quad 1-\kappa_{t}(x)\asymp\frac{C\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})},

(4.62)

It follows that
|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)|\gtrsim\varrho
.
We can rewrite
f^{*}_{t}(x)
in terms of
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle f^{*}_{t}(x)=\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)e^{m_{t}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)}.

m_{t}^{*}(x)
and
\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)
are explicit, satisfy (
4.61
) and
Lemma

4.11
. For
\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\gtrsim\delta
we have

\displaystyle\arg(1+f^{*}_{t}(x))

\displaystyle=\arg(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)e^{m_{t}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)})

\displaystyle=\arg((1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x))e^{m_{t}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)}-(e^{m_{t}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)}-1))

\displaystyle=\arg(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x))+\arg\left(1-\frac{(e^{m_{t}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)}-1)}{(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x))}\right)

\displaystyle=-\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x)\pi+\OO\left(\frac{|m_{t}^{*}(x)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(x)|}{|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)|}\right)=-\varrho v\pi+\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right),

where in the last equality we used (
4.62
) and
Lemma

4.11
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.3
.

We examine the second term on the righthand side of (
4.17
). We recall the dilogarithm function (see
[
64
]
),

\displaystyle\Li_{2}(z)=-\int_{0}^{z}\ln(1-u)\frac{{\rm d}u}{u},\quad\Li_{2}^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{\ln(1-x)}{x}

which can be analytically extended to the cut plane
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[1,\infty]
.
By a change of variable,

\displaystyle\Li_{2}(-e^{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{-e^{w}}\ln(1-u)\frac{{\rm d}u}{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{w}\ln(1+e^{x}){\rm d}x,

which is analytic on the strip
\{w\in{\mathbb{C}}:-\pi<\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[w]<\pi\}
.
Then, we can rewrite the last term in (
4.17
) by noticing that

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{1}\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{m^{*}_{t}(z)}^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}-\ln(1+e^{x}){\rm d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Li_{2}(-e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)})-\Li_{2}(-e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)}).

For the last term, since
m^{*}_{t}(z)=\ell\varrho/z+\OO(1/z^{2})
as
z\rightarrow\infty
. Thus
-e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)}=-1-\ell\varrho/z+\OO(1/z^{2})
when
z\rightarrow\infty
. Thus the contour integral is given by

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint\Li_{2}(-e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)}){\rm d}z

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint\Li_{2}\left(-1-\frac{\ell\varrho}{z}+\OO\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)\right){\rm d}z

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint\Li_{2}\left(-1-\ell\varrho w+\OO\left(w^{2}\right)\right)\frac{1}{w^{2}}{\rm d}w=\ell\varrho\Li_{2}^{\prime}(-1)=\ell\varrho\ln(2).

For the first term in (
4.17
) we need to evaluate the integral

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\Li_{2}(-e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}){\rm d}z,

(4.63)

where the contour
\omega\in\Lambda
(from (
4.50
)) encloses
[tv,tv+\ell]
.

We recall from
[
64
]
, the dilogarithmic function function
\Li_{2}(z)
jumps by
2\pi\mathrm{i}\log|z|
, as
z
crosses the cut. And the function
\Li_{2}(z)+\mathrm{i}\arg(1-z)\log|z|
, where
\arg

denotes the branch of the argument lying between
-\pi
and
\pi
, is continuous. Its imaginary part gives the Bloch-Wigner function
D(z)

\displaystyle D(z)=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\Li_{2}(z)]+\arg(1-z)\log|z|.

(4.64)

Bloch-Wigner function
D(z)
can be expressed as a single real variable

\displaystyle D(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left[D\left(\frac{z}{\overline{z}}\right)+D\left(\frac{1-1/z}{1-1/\overline{z}}\right)+D\left(\frac{1-\overline{z}}{1-{z}}\right)\right],

and

\displaystyle D(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\Li_{2}(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta})]=2L(\theta/2),

where
L
is the Lobachevsky function from (
1.14
). We recall the complex slope
f^{*}_{t}(z)=e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
from
Lemma

4.12
, and denote the three angle of the triangle
\{0,-1,f^{*}_{t}(x)\}
as
\pi p_{1},\pi p_{2},\pi p_{3}
. Then
Lemma

4.12
implies that

\displaystyle p_{1}=\varrho,\quad p_{2}=\varrho v+\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right),\quad p_{3}=\varrho(1-v)+\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right),

(4.65)

and it follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}D(-f)&=\frac{1}{2}\left[D\left(\frac{f}{\overline{f}}\right)+D\left(\frac{(f+1)/f}{(\overline{f}+1)/\overline{f}}\right)+D\left(\frac{1+\overline{f}}{1+{f}}\right)\right]\\
&=L(\pi p_{1})+L(\pi p_{1})+L(\pi p_{3})=\pi\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)+\OO\left(\frac{\delta\log(1/\zeta)}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right),\end{split}

(4.66)

where in the last equality, we also used that
\zeta\leqslant\varrho,\varrho v,\varrho(1-v)\leqslant 1-\zeta
, (
4.7
) and (
4.8
).

With the notation above, we can rewrite (
4.63
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\Li_{2}(-f^{*}_{t}(z)){\rm d}z,

(4.67)

where the contour
\omega\in\Lambda
(from (
4.50
)) encloses
[tv,\ell+tv]
. We can deform
\omega
to the real axis,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\Li_{2}(-f^{*}_{t}(z)){\rm d}z=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\Li_{2}(-f^{*}_{t}(x)){\rm d}x\\
&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(D(-f^{*}_{t}(x))-\arg(1+f^{*}_{t}(x))\log|f^{*}_{t}(x)|){\rm d}x,\end{split}

(4.68)

where we used (
4.64
),
f^{*}_{t}(\overline{z})=\overline{f^{*}_{t}(z)}
, and
\Li_{2}(\overline{w})=\overline{\Li_{2}(w)}
.

Thanks to (
4.68
), we can rewrite the rate
S({\mathcal{A}};g)
from (
4.17
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}S({\mathcal{A}};g)&=\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\pi^{-1}\arg(1+f_{t}^{*})\log|f_{t}^{*}|-\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}D(-f_{t}^{*}(x)){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\ell^{2}\varrho\ln(2).\end{split}

(4.69)

Recall that
\log|f_{t}^{*}|=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}m_{t}^{*}(x)+g_{t}(x)
,
\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}m_{t}^{*}(x){\rm d}x=0
, and
|\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}m_{t}^{*}(x)|\lesssim 1+\ln(\ell/\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\}))
.
We can rewrite the first term on the righthand side of (
4.69
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}(\pi^{-1}\arg(1+f_{t}^{*})-\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t))(\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}m_{t}^{*}(x)+g_{t}(x)){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&\lesssim\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}(\ln(\ell/\dist(x,\{tv,tv+\ell\}))+\ln(\ell/\delta)+\ln(1/\zeta)){\rm d}x{\rm d}t.\end{split}

(4.70)

where we used
Lemma

4.12
and
Lemma

4.7
.
We divide the integral above into two cases: i)
\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\delta
and ii)
\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\leqslant\delta
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\delta}\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}{\rm d}x\lesssim\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\ell,

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\leqslant\delta}\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\dist(x,\{tv,tv+\ell\}))}{\delta}{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\lesssim\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)\ell.

Combining the discussions above, we get that the rate function is given by

\displaystyle S({\mathcal{A}};g)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}D(-f_{t}^{*}(x)){\rm d}x+\ell^{2}\varrho\ln(2)+\OO(\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\ell)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\left(\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)+\OO\left(\frac{\delta\log(1/\zeta)}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right)\right){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\ell^{2}\varrho\ln(2)+\OO(\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\ell)

\displaystyle=-\ell^{2}\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)+\ell^{2}\varrho\ln(2)+\OO(\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\ell),

where in the second equality we used
(
4.66
). This finishes the proof of
Proposition

4.3
.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.3
.

For any
\beta,\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}
, denote
I=I(\alpha,\beta)
and
J=I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)
. Next we compute the interaction between different blocks. Thanks to (
4.26
), for
\alpha{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\alpha+1){\mathsf{L}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\ln\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)&=\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}}+1)-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}}+1)}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\\
&+\OO\left(\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\frac{1}{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(4.71)

We can bound the total error as

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\notin I(\alpha,\beta)}\frac{1}{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{\beta}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\notin I(\alpha,\beta)}\frac{1}{(i-j)^{2}},

(4.72)

where we used that
|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})|\geqslant\theta|i-j|
.

The upper bound on the righthand side of (
4.72
) is achieved when particles in
I(\alpha,\beta)
are tightly packed at
\{-|I(\alpha,\beta)|,\cdots,-1\}
, and the other particles are also tightly packed on
\{0,1,2,\cdots,N-|I(\alpha,\beta)|-1\}
. Therefore we can bound it as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\beta}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\notin I(\alpha,\beta)}\frac{1}{(i-j)^{2}}&\lesssim\sum_{\beta}\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant|I(\alpha,\beta)|}\left(\frac{1}{i^{2}}+\frac{1}{(i+1)^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\beta}\ln|I(\alpha,\beta)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell}\ln(\ell N),\end{split}

(4.73)

where in the last inequality, we used that
\sum_{\beta}|I(\alpha,\beta)|=N
and Jessen’s inequality.

Thanks to (
4.71
), (
4.72
) and (
4.73
), we can sum over
\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}
, and notice the cancellation of the first term on the righthand side of (
4.71
) when summing over
{\mathsf{t}}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\ln\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)\\
&=\sum_{\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})}\right)+\OO\left(N\ln(\ell N)\right)\\
&=\left(\sum_{i<j}-\sum_{\beta}\sum_{i<j\in I(\alpha,\beta)}\right)\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})}\right)+\OO\left(N\ln(\ell N)\right).\end{split}

(4.74)

To bound the summation over
i<j\in I(\alpha,\beta)
on the righthand side of (
4.74
), we recall from
Lemma

2.7

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{\theta^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i<j\in I(\alpha,\beta)}\ln\left({\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\iint\ln|x-y|\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{I(\alpha,\beta)}(\beta\ell))\rho(y;{\bm{x}}_{I(\alpha,\beta)}(\beta\ell)){\rm d}x{\rm d}y+\OO\left(\frac{\ln N}{\ell N}\right).\end{split}

(4.75)

There are two cases. If on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H^{*}
has a linear approximation, then the height functions corresponding to
\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{I(\alpha,\beta)}(\beta\ell))
and
\rho(x;{\bm{x}}_{I(\alpha,\beta+1)}((\beta+1)\ell)
differs by at most
\OO(\varepsilon)
. Thus
Lemma

2.7
and (
4.75
) together imply

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j\in I(\alpha,\beta)}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})}\right)=\OO(\varepsilon(\ell N)^{2}\ln(1/\ell))=\OO(\varepsilon_{0}(\ell N)^{2}),

(4.76)

if on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H^{*}
does not have a linear approximation, then we simply bound

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j\in I(\alpha,\beta)}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}((\beta+1){\mathsf{L}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(\beta{\mathsf{L}})}\right)=\OO((\ell N)^{2}).

(4.77)

Recall from
Lemma

2.3
, the total number of such pair
(\alpha,\beta)
such that on
{\mathfrak{R}}(\alpha,\beta)
,
H^{*}
does not have a linear approximation, is at most
\OO(\varepsilon_{0}/\ell^{2})
. By plugging (
4.76
), (
4.77
) into (
4.74
), and summing over
\alpha,\beta
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}\sum_{\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{\beta{\mathsf{L}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}<(\beta+1){\mathsf{L}}}\sum_{i\in I(\alpha,\beta),j\in I(\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}\ln\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})}\right)=\sum_{i<j}\theta\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{L}})}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(0)-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}(0)}\right)+\OO(\varepsilon_{0}N^{2}).

This finishes the proof of
Lemma

3.3
.
∎

5
Large Deviation Lower Bound: Constant Slope Case

We recall the parallelogram shaped region
\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}
from (
3.15
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}=\{x,t\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell,a(t)\leqslant x\leqslant b(t)\}.

(5.1)

where
\{a(t),b(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
are two Bernoulli walk paths such that
a(t)<b(t)
for all
0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
(more specifically

a(t)=y_{i}(t+\beta\ell)-\alpha\ell,b(t)=y_{j+1}(t+\beta\ell)-\theta-\alpha\ell
. From the construction in (
3.2
), the region
\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}
satisfies the following assumption

Assumption 5.1
.

Fix any
(\varrho,-\varrho v)\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\zeta}
. Denote the height function
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)
with constant
(\varrho,-\varrho v)
(see
Definition

2.4
). Take large integer
n\geqslant 1
such that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{n\theta}{N}-\varrho\right|\leqslant\varepsilon,

and an
n
-particle Bernoulli random walk
\{{\bm{y}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
such that
{\bm{y}}(t)\in[a(t),b(t)-\theta]
and its height function
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
satisfying

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon.

(5.2)

We denote
\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
the set of
n
-particle nonintersecting Bernoulli random walks
{\mathsf{p}}=\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
such that

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}(0)={\bm{y}}(0),\quad{\bm{x}}(\ell)={\bm{y}}(\ell),\quad a(t)\leqslant x_{n}(t)\leqslant\cdots\leqslant x_{1}\leqslant b(t)-\theta,\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell.

Proposition 5.2
.

Adopt
Assumption

5.1
. We recall the rate function
\sigma
from (
1.14
) and parameters
\zeta,\delta,\varepsilon,\ell
from (
3.1
). There exists an
n
-particle configuration
{\bm{x}}
, such that the Markov process (
4.1
) starting from
{\bm{x}}
with height function
{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\{{\mathcal{H}}:\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)\})\geqslant\frac{1}{\theta}\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)-\frac{n}{\ell N}\ln(2)+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}\right),

provided
N
is large enough.

The nonintersecting Bernoulli walk ensembles starting from
{\bm{x}}
in
Proposition

5.2
may not belong to
\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
, as needed to prove
Proposition

3.5
. The following proposition states that we can slightly modify them such that they belong to
\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Adopt
Assumption

5.1
. We recall the parameters
\zeta,\delta,\xi,\ell
from (
3.1
). Given a height function
{\mathcal{H}}
with
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)=:\varepsilon^{\prime}
corresponding to the nonintersecting Bernoulli walk
{\mathsf{p}}=\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
, and initial configuration
{\bm{x}}
as in
Proposition

5.2
. There exists a modified nonintersecting Bernoulli walk
\widehat{\mathsf{p}}=\{\widehat{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}\in\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
such that
its height function
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C^{\prime}\xi.

(5.3)

Moreover,
the map from
{\mathsf{p}}
to
\widehat{\mathsf{p}}
is at most
e^{\OO(\xi\ell N^{2})}
to one, and

\displaystyle\ln\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{p}})=\ln\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\mathsf{p}})+\OO(\xi\ell\ln(\ell/\xi)N^{2}).

Proof of
Proposition

3.5
.

The first statement (
3.23
) in
Proposition

3.5
follows
Lemma

2.6
.
For the second statement (
3.24
),we notice that the sum is taken over configurations satisfying (
3.18
), namely which are restricted to live in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{P}}
defined in
(
5.1
), or in other words which belong to
\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
.
Thanks to
Proposition

5.3
, we have

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\mathcal{H}}\in\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b):\|\widehat{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C^{\prime}\xi)=\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\sum_{\widehat{\mathsf{p}}:\widehat{\mathcal{H}}\in\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)\atop\|\widehat{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C^{\prime}\xi}\ln\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\mathsf{p}})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\sum_{{\mathsf{p}}:\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\prime}}\ln\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{p}})+\OO((\xi/\ell)\ln(\ell/\xi))=\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\prime})+\OO((\xi/\ell)\ln(\ell/\xi))

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{\theta}\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)-\frac{n}{\ell N}\ln(2)+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)}{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\iint_{[\alpha\ell,(\alpha+1)\ell]\times[\beta\ell,(\beta+1)\ell]}\sigma(\nabla H^{*}){\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\frac{n}{\ell N}\ln(2)+\OO\left(\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\varepsilon\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)}{\ell}\right),

where in the third line we used
Proposition

5.2

and in the last equality we used the second statement in
Lemma

2.3
.

∎

5.1
Proof of
Proposition

5.3

We recall from (
3.1
) that
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)\ll\xi\zeta
, and introduce the index sets

\displaystyle I_{0}=[\![{1,\xi N}]\!],\quad I_{1}=[\![{\xi N,n-\xi N}]\!],\quad I_{2}=[\![{n-\xi N,n}]\!].

(5.4)

We set

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{i}(t)=x_{i}(t),\quad\xi\leqslant t\leqslant\ell-\xi,\quad\xi N\leqslant i\leqslant n-\xi N.

(5.5)

To construct
\widehat{\mathsf{p}}
given (
5.5
), we need to construct the Bernoulli walk paths
\{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{0}},\{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{2}}
, and also
\{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N},\{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant(\ell-\xi)N}
.

For any
1\leqslant i\leqslant\varrho\ell N/\theta
, we denote the
i
-th level line of
{\mathcal{A}}
as

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(t)=\inf\{x:{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)>\theta(i-1)/N\}=\gamma_{i}(0)+tv=\frac{\theta(i-1)}{\varrho N}+tv,

(5.6)

which are straight lines.

By our assumption
\|{\mathcal{H}}-{\mathcal{A}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon^{\prime}
, we have for
\lceil\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\theta\rceil<i<n-\lceil\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\theta\rceil
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(\gamma_{i+\lceil\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\theta\rceil}(t),t)\leqslant\frac{\theta(i-1)}{N}-\varepsilon^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathcal{A}}(x_{i}(t),t)\leqslant\frac{\theta(i-1)}{N}+\varepsilon^{\prime}\leqslant{\mathcal{A}}(\gamma_{i-\lceil\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\theta\rceil}(t),t).

(5.7)

It follows from (
5.6
) and (
5.7
), and noticing
\xi\geqslant 2\varepsilon^{\prime}/\theta
, that for
i\in I_{1}
.

\displaystyle\gamma_{i-\lceil\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\theta\rceil}(t)\leqslant x_{i}(t)\leqslant\gamma_{i-\lceil\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\theta\rceil}(t),\quad\Rightarrow\quad|x_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t)|\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varrho.

(5.8)

By the same argument, our assumption (
5.2
) also implies that for any
i\in I_{1}
,

\displaystyle|y_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t)|\leqslant 2\varepsilon^{\prime}/\varrho,\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell.

(5.9)

Moreover, since
{\bm{y}}(t)\in[a(t),b(t)-\theta]
, (
5.2
) also implies that either
a(t)\leqslant tv
, or
a(t)\geqslant tv
and
{\mathcal{A}}(a(t),t)\leqslant{\mathcal{H}}(a(t),t)+\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{\prime}
. In both cases we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(t)-a(t)\geqslant\frac{1}{\varrho}\left(\frac{(i-1)\theta}{N}-\varepsilon^{\prime}\right).

(5.10)

Figure 4:
Shown above is construction of the Bernoulli walk paths
\widehat{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}}(t)
.

We will construct the Bernoulli walk paths
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{0}},\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N}
; and
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{2}},\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant(\ell-\xi)N}
in the following way; see
Figure

4
.
For
1\leqslant i\leqslant\xi N,0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})=\max\{Na({\mathsf{t}})+(i-1)\theta,{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0),{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})\}\\
&{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{t}})=\min\{Nb({\mathsf{t}})-i\theta,{\mathsf{y}}_{n-i+1}(0)+{\mathsf{t}},{\mathsf{y}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{L}})\}.\end{split}

(5.11)

And for
i\in I_{1},0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})=\max\{{\mathsf{x}}:{\mathsf{x}}-{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)\in\mathbb{Z},{\mathsf{x}}\leqslant(1-t/\xi){\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)+(t/\xi){\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)\},\\
&{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})=\max\{{\mathsf{x}}:{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{x}}\in\mathbb{Z},{\mathsf{x}}\leqslant(1-t/\xi){\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}}-\xi N)+(t/\xi){\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})\}.\end{split}

(5.12)

The maximum or minimum over Bernoulli paths is still a Bernoulli path. So
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
is a Bernoulli path. The map from
{\mathsf{p}}
to
\widehat{\mathsf{p}}
is at most
e^{\OO(\xi\ell N^{2})}
to one.

To show that
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n,0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}\in\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
, we need to check the following conditions

1.

\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\xi N,0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
are non-interesecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
to
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})
;
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\xi N,0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
are non-intersecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{x}}_{n-i+1}(0)
to
{\mathsf{y}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{L}})
.

2.

\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N}
are non-interesecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
to
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)
;
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
are non-intersecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})
to
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})
.

3.

{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{\xi N}({\mathsf{t}})<{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})
and
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{n-\xi N}({\mathsf{t}})<{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{n-\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})
.

From
Assumption

5.1
,
\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)\neq\emptyset
, so
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)\geqslant Na(0)+(i-1)\theta
,
{\mathsf{y}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{L}})\leqslant Nb({\mathsf{L}})-i\theta
and
{{\mathsf{y}}}_{i}(0)\geqslant{{\mathsf{y}}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-{\mathsf{L}}
. Thus at time
{\mathsf{t}}=0
,
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}(0)={\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
; at time
{\mathsf{t}}={\mathsf{L}}
,
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})={\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})
. In particular
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})
is a Bernoulli path from
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
to
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})
. Also from the construction (
5.12
), we have
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})<{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{j}({\mathsf{L}})
for
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant\xi N
. The same argument also implies that
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\xi N,0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
are non-intersecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{y}}_{n-i+1}(0)
to
{\mathsf{y}}_{n-i+1}({\mathsf{L}})
. This proves
Item

1
.

For
Item

2
, by our construction (
5.12
), we have that
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}(0)={\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
and
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}(\xi N)={\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)
. It follows from (
5.8
) and (
5.9
), that
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)-{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)=N(\gamma_{i}(\xi N)-\gamma_{i}(0))+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)=N\xi v+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)
, and

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{{\mathsf{t}}+1}{N\xi}\right){\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)+\left(\frac{{\mathsf{t}}+1}{N\xi}\right){\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)-\left(1-\frac{{\mathsf{t}}}{N\xi}\right){\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)-\left(\frac{{\mathsf{t}}}{N\xi}\right){\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)=v+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}/(\xi\varrho))\in(0,1),

provided
\varrho v\geqslant\zeta\gg\varepsilon^{\prime}/\xi
.

Thus the construction (
5.12
) implies that
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}}+1)-{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\in\{0,1\}
. We conclude that
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N}
are non-interesecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
to
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}(\xi N)
. The same argument also implies that
\{{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})\}_{i\in I_{1},0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}}
are non-intersecting Bernoulli paths form
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})
to
{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})
.

For
Item

3
, by symmetry, we only need to show that

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})<{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}}),\quad i\in I_{0},\quad 0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}-\xi N.

(5.13)

We will check the three relations:
\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})\geqslant Na({\mathsf{t}})+(\xi N+1)\theta
,
\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})\geqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
and
\widehat{\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})\geqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})
. For
\xi N\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}-\xi N
, the first relation follows from (
5.8
) and (
5.10
),
{\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(t)+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)\geqslant Na(t)+\theta(\xi N)/\varrho+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)>Na({\mathsf{t}})+\xi\theta N
. The second statement follows from
{\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(t)+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(0)+Ntv+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)={\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)+Ntv+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)>{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
, where we used that
\gamma_{\xi N}(t)
is linear in
t
, (
5.9
) and
t\geqslant\xi
. The last statement follows from similar argument
{\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(t)+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(\ell)+Nv(\ell-t)+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)={\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})+Nv(\ell-t)+\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)>{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})
, where we used that
\gamma_{\xi N}(t)
is linear in
t
, (
5.9
) and
t\leqslant\ell-\xi
. This finishes the proof of (
5.13
) for
\xi N\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{L}}-\xi N
.

For
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N
, using (
5.8
) the first statment follows from:

\displaystyle{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-t/\xi){\mathsf{y}}_{\xi N+1}(0)+(t/\xi){\mathsf{x}}_{\xi N+1}(\xi N)-1=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(t)-\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)

\displaystyle=Na(t)+\xi\theta N/\varrho-\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)>Na(t)+\xi\theta N,

where we used (
5.8
) and (
5.9
).
The second statement that
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})\geqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{i}(0)
holds trivially; and the third statement follows from (
5.9
) that
{\widehat{\mathsf{x}}}_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{t}})\geqslant N\gamma_{\xi N+1}(t)-\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)=N\gamma_{\xi N+1}({\mathsf{L}})-({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})v-\OO(\varepsilon^{\prime}N/\varrho)\geqslant{\mathsf{y}}_{i}({\mathsf{L}})-({\mathsf{L}}-{\mathsf{t}})
.
This finishes the proof of (
5.13
) for
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N
. And we conclude that
\widehat{\mathsf{p}}
constructed above belongs to
\Adm({\bm{y}}(0),{\bm{y}}(\ell),a,b)
.

Next we show for any
i\in I_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{x}_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{\prime}/\zeta\ll\xi.

(5.14)

and the claim (
5.3
) follows.

For
\xi\leqslant t\leqslant\ell-\xi
, (
5.14
) follows from (
5.8
). For
0\leqslant t\leqslant\xi
, from the construction (
5.12
) and (
5.8
) and (
5.9
)

\displaystyle|\widehat{x}_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t)|

\displaystyle\leqslant|(1-t/\xi)y_{i}(0)+(t/\xi)x_{i}(\xi N)-\gamma_{i}(t)|+1/N

\displaystyle\leqslant|(1-t/\xi)\gamma_{i}(0)+(t/\xi)\gamma_{i}(\xi N)-\gamma_{i}(t)|+(\varepsilon^{\prime}+1/N)=\varepsilon^{\prime}+1/N.

The case that
\ell-\xi\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
follows from the same argument.

We can decompose the weight
\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{p}})

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{p}})&=\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N\text{ or }{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant(\ell-\xi)N}\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}))}+\sum_{\xi N\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant(\ell-\xi)N}\left(\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{0}\cup I_{2}}({\mathsf{t}})+{\bm{e}}_{I_{0}\cup I_{2}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{0}\cup I_{2}}({\mathsf{t}}))}\right.\\
&\left.+\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{1}}({\mathsf{t}})+{\bm{e}}_{I_{1}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{1}}({\mathsf{t}}))}+\sum_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in I_{1}}\ln\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}\right)\right)-\ell Nn.\end{split}

(5.15)

Thanks to
Lemma

2.6
, we can bound terms in (
5.15
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\xi N\text{ or }{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant(\ell-\xi)N}\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})+\theta{\bm{e}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}))}=\OO(\xi nN)=\OO(\xi\ell N^{2}),\\
&\sum_{\xi N\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant(\ell-\xi)N}\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{0}\cup I_{2}}({\mathsf{t}})+{\bm{e}}_{I_{0}\cup I_{2}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{0}\cup I_{2}}({\mathsf{t}}))}=\OO((|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|)\ell N)=\OO(\xi\ell N^{2}).\end{split}

(5.16)

And for the last term in (
5.15
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in I_{1}}\ln\left(1+\frac{\theta(e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-e_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}}))}\right)\leqslant 2\theta\sum_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in I_{1}}\frac{1}{|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})|}\leqslant 2\sum_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in I_{1}}\frac{1}{|i-j|},\end{split}

(5.17)

where in the last inequality we used
|{\mathsf{x}}_{i}({\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}({\mathsf{t}})|\geqslant\theta|i-j|
. Recall the sets
I_{0},I_{1},I_{2}
from (
4.24
), we can further bound (
5.17
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{0}\cup I_{2},j\in I_{1}}\frac{1}{|i-j|}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\xi N}\sum_{j=i}^{\ell N}\frac{1}{j}\leqslant\xi N+\xi N\sum_{\xi N\leqslant j\leqslant\ell N}\frac{1}{j}\leqslant\xi N+\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)N=\OO(\xi\ln(\ell/\xi)N).

(5.18)

Plugging (
5.16
), (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) into (
5.15
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{p}})&=\sum_{\xi N\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant(\ell-\xi)N}\ln\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{1}}({\mathsf{t}})+{\bm{e}}_{I_{1}}({\mathsf{t}}))}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}_{I_{1}}({\mathsf{t}}))}-\ell Nn+\OO(\xi\ell\ln(\ell/\xi)N^{2}).\end{split}

(5.19)

The same statement (
5.19
) holds for
\widehat{\mathsf{p}}
. Moreover, from our construction (
5.5
), we conclude from (
5.19
)

\displaystyle\ln\mathbb{P}({\mathsf{p}})=\ln\mathbb{P}(\widehat{\mathsf{p}})+\OO(\xi\ell\ln(\ell/\xi)N^{2}).

5.2
Proof of
Proposition

5.2

We recall the smoothed height function
\widetilde{H}
from (
4.9
), the associated complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}
from (
4.10
), and the drift
g_{t}(z)
from (
4.13
).
We consider Markov process
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
with height function
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
, such that the initial data
{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)
is close to
\widetilde{H}(x,0)
. From the construction of
\widetilde{H}
in (
4.9
), thanks to
Lemma

4.5
, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)|\lesssim\delta+\int_{-3\ell}^{x}\frac{\delta\varrho{\rm d}y}{\delta+\dist(y,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\lesssim\delta\ln(\ell/\delta),

and it follows there exists some large
C>1

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant C\delta\ln(\ell/\delta))\geqslant\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon).

(5.20)

To lower bound (
5.20
), by the same argument as in proof of the large deviation upper bound, we tilt the Markov chain by the exponential Martingale (
4.14
