). The following lemma collects some estimates for the numerator and denominator of the exponential Martingale on the event
\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
,

Lemma 5.4
.

Adopt
Assumption

5.1
.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
be the height function associated with the particle configuration
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
, with
\supp({\bm{x}}(t))\in[-3\ell,3\ell]
and
\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon
then

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}({\mathsf{t}}/N))}=-\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}\right)(\ell N)^{2}\right).

(5.21)

Dynamical loop equation gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\ln\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t)/N)}|{\bm{x}}(t)]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m_{t}^{*}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon\ell\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\right),

(5.22)

where the contour
\omega\in\Lambda
(from (
4.50
)) encloses
[-3\ell,3\ell]
, and
m_{t}^{*}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of the empirical measure of
\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)

\displaystyle m_{t}^{*}(z)=\int_{tv}^{tv+\ell}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t){\rm d}x}{x-z}.

With
Lemma

5.4
, we can lower bound the righthand side of (
5.20
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{P}(\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\geqslant-\frac{S({\mathcal{A}};g)}{\theta\ell^{2}}+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}+\frac{\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{\varepsilon\ell\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{(\ell N)^{2}}\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]/N}\frac{e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}\bm{1}(\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\right],\end{split}

(5.23)

where
S({\mathcal{A}};g)
is from (
4.17
) and
Proposition

4.3
gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}S({\mathcal{A}};g)&=-\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{\ell}\oint\int_{0}^{1}\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau{\rm d}t\\
&=-\ell^{2}(\sigma(\varrho,-\varrho v)-\varrho\ln(2))+\OO(\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)^{2}\ell).\end{split}

(5.24)

To lower bound the second term on the righthand side of (
5.23
), we introduce the tilded measure

\mathbb{P}^{g}(\cdot)
as defined below,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{g}(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}-1}]\!]/N}\frac{e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(Nt)g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}|{\bm{x}}(t)]}(\cdot)\right].

(5.25)

Under
\mathbb{P}^{g}(\cdot)
,
the Markov process (
4.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{g}(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}|\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})=\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})\propto\frac{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{e_{i}g_{t}({\mathsf{x}}_{i}/N)},\quad t={\mathsf{t}}/N.

(5.26)

The large deviation lower bound follows from the following limit shape result

Proposition 5.5
.

Adopt
Assumption

5.1
. We recall the parameter
\varepsilon
from (
3.1
).
For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a small
c(\varepsilon)>0
, such that if the height function
{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)
of the initial data
{\bm{x}}(0)
satisfies
\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)-\widetilde{H}(x,0)\|_{\infty}\leqslant c(\varepsilon)
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{g}(\|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t)\|_{\infty}\leqslant\varepsilon)\geqslant 1-\varepsilon,

(5.27)

provided
N
is large enough.

Proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

Take
{\bm{x}}
with height function
{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)
as in
Proposition

5.5
. Proof of
Proposition

5.2
follows from plugging (
5.24
) and
Proposition

5.5
into (
5.23
).
∎

5.3
Proof of
Lemma

5.4

Proof.

From the construction of
\widetilde{H}
in (
4.9
), and thanks to (
4.36
) from
Lemma

4.5

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(-3\ell,t)=\int_{\infty}^{-3\ell}\kappa_{t}(y){\rm d}y\lesssim\delta,\quad\theta-\widetilde{H}(3\ell,t)\lesssim\delta,

(5.28)

it follows that

\displaystyle H(-3\ell,t)\lesssim\delta+\varepsilon\lesssim\delta,\quad\theta-\widetilde{H}(3\ell,t)\lesssim\delta+\varepsilon\lesssim\delta.

Using the above estimate we can rewrite the lefthand side of (
5.21
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}=\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}{\sum_{i:|x_{i}(t)|\leqslant 3\ell}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t))}+\OO(\delta\ell N^{2}\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}).\end{split}

(5.29)

By the same argument as in (
4.46
), (
5.29
) leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{{\mathsf{t}}=0}^{{\mathsf{L}}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}({\mathsf{t}})/N)}=-\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}\partial_{t}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t+\OO(\delta\ell N^{2}\|g_{t}\|_{\infty}).\end{split}

(5.30)

Next we replace
{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)
by
\widetilde{H}(x,t)
. Using the second and third statement of
Lemma

4.7
, and integrating by part

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}(\partial_{t}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|\\
&\leqslant\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\left|\left.\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}({\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t))g_{t}(z){\rm d}x\right|_{t=0}^{t=\ell}\right|+\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\left|\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}({\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\widetilde{H}(x,t))\partial_{t}g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon\|g\|_{\infty}\ell N^{2}+\varepsilon\|\partial_{t}g\|_{\infty}(\ell N)^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon\log(\ell/\delta)\ell N^{2}+(\varepsilon/\delta)(\ell N)^{2}\lesssim(\varepsilon/\delta)(\ell N)^{2}.\end{split}

(5.31)

Finally, we replace
\widetilde{H}(x,t)
by
{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\frac{N^{2}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}(\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)-\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))g_{t}(x){\rm d}x{\rm d}t\right|\\
&\leqslant\frac{N^{2}\|g\|_{\infty}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)-\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t)|{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&\lesssim\frac{N^{2}\|g\|_{\infty}}{\theta}\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-3\ell}^{3\ell}\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})^{2}/\ell}{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\\
&\lesssim\delta\ln(\ell/\delta)\ell\|g\|_{\infty}N^{2},\end{split}

(5.32)

where we used
Lemma

4.5
for the second inequality. The claim (
5.21
) follows from combining (
5.29
), (
5.30
), (
5.31
) and (
5.32
).

The same as in
Lemma

5.4
, by using the dynamical loop equation, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\mathbb{E}[e^{\sum_{i}e_{i}({\mathsf{t}})g_{t}(x_{i}(t)/N)}|{\bm{x}}(t)]&=\frac{N}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau+\OO\left(1\right),\end{split}

(5.33)

where
m_{t}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of the empirical measure of
{\bm{x}}(t)
, and the implicit constant in the error term
\OO(1)
depends on
\delta,\zeta
. We can deform the contour
\omega
such that it consists of two parallel lines
\{z:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]=\pm 1/K\}
, with
K\asymp 1/\delta\zeta
.

Recall from
Lemma

4.7
and use (
4.40
), if
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\left|g_{t}(z)-\ln\frac{\sin(v)}{\sin(1-v)}\right|=\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right)=\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{|z|}\right).

(5.34)

We can replace
g_{t}(z)
in (
5.33
) by
g_{t}(z)-\ln(\sin(v)/\sin(1-v))
and contour integral does not change. To replace
m_{t}(z)
in (
5.33
) by
m_{t}^{*}(z)
, the error is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau-\oint\int_{0}^{1}\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})g_{t}(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau\right|\\
&\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}|\ln(1+e^{m_{t}^{*}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})-\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})|\left|g_{t}(z)-\ln\frac{\sin(v)}{\sin(1-v)}\right|{\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau.\end{split}

(5.35)

For the difference
|\ln(1+e^{m_{t}^{*}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})-\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})|
, we need to upper bound
|m_{t}^{*}(z)-m_{t}(z)|
.
Recall from
Lemma

4.11
, for
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\delta

\displaystyle|m_{t}^{*}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}.

(5.36)

Next we bound
|m_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]\geqslant 1/K
, the same as in (
4.53
), we have
|m_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|\leqslant\varepsilon\pi K
. They together give a simple bound
|m_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z)|\leqslant\delta/\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})+\varepsilon K\pi\ll 1
for
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\gg\delta
. It follows that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}|\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)})-\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)}))|=\left|\ln\left(1+\frac{(e^{m_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z)}-1)e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)}}{1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)}}\right)\right|\\
&\leqslant\left|\frac{(e^{m_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z)}-1)e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)}}{1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g_{t}(z)}}\right|\lesssim\frac{|m_{t}(z)-m_{t}^{*}(z)|}{\zeta}\lesssim\frac{|m_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|+|\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)-m^{*}_{t}(z)|}{\zeta},\end{split}

(5.37)

where in the second to last inequality we used (
4.51
).

Next we will prove an improved estimate of the difference
|m_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|
when
z
is far away from
0
. For
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant 3\ell
,

\displaystyle|m_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{|z|}+\int_{|x|\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t){\rm d}x}{|z-x|}.

(5.38)

By symmetry, we will only prove (
5.38
) for
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]\leqslant-\ell
, then
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\asymp\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]\geqslant\ell
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}|m_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|&=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|\\
&\leqslant\left|\int_{x\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|+\left|\int_{x\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{x\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|+\OO\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{|z|}\right),\end{split}

(5.39)

where the last term is bounded by an integration by part and
|z-x|\gtrsim|z|
. For the first term on the righthand side of (
5.39
)
and we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{x\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|\leqslant\left|\int_{x\leqslant 2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]}\frac{\varrho\kappa_{t}(x)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|+\left|\int_{2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]\leqslant x\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\varrho\kappa_{t}(x)}{z-x}{\rm d}x\right|\lesssim\frac{\delta\ln(|z|K)}{|z|},

(5.40)

where we used
Lemma

4.5
to bound
\kappa_{t}
in the last term.
The claim (
5.38
) follows from combining (
5.39
) and (
5.40
).

We decompose the contour integral
\omega=\{z:\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]=\pm 1/K\}
on the righthand side of (
5.35
) into the following three parts

1.

For
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\leqslant\delta
, using
Lemma

4.10
and (
3.1
) that
|\ln(1+e^{m_{t}^{*}(z)+\tau g(z)})-\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g(z)})|\lesssim\ln(\ell^{2}K/\delta\zeta)\lesssim\ln(\ell/\zeta\delta)
, we can bound the integral as

\displaystyle\oint_{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\leqslant\delta}\ln(\ell/\zeta\delta)(\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta)+C)|{\rm d}z|\lesssim\delta\ln^{2}(\ell/\delta).

(5.41)

2.

For
\delta\leqslant\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\leqslant 10\ell
, using (
5.37
) and (
5.36
), we can bound the integral as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\oint_{\delta\leqslant\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\leqslant 10\ell}\frac{\varepsilon K+\delta/\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}{\zeta}(\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta)+C)|{\rm d}z|\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon\ell\ln(\ell/\delta)/(\delta\zeta^{2})+\delta\ln^{2}(\ell/\delta)/\zeta.\end{split}

(5.42)

3.

For
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant 10\ell
, using (
5.37
), (
5.36
), (
5.38
) and (
5.34
), we can bound the integral as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\oint_{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant 10\ell}\frac{1}{\zeta}\left(\frac{\delta\ln(|z|K)}{|z|}+\int_{|x|\geqslant|z|/2}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t){\rm d}x}{|z-x|}\right)\frac{\delta}{|z|}|{\rm d}z|\\
&\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2}\ln(\ell/\zeta\delta)}{\zeta\ell}+\int_{|x|\geqslant 3\ell}\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)\int_{6\ell\leqslant|z|\leqslant 2|x|}\frac{\delta}{\zeta|z-x||z|}|{\rm d}z|{\rm d}x,\end{split}

(5.43)

where we used that
K\asymp 1/\zeta\delta
, the inner integral is integrable

\displaystyle\int_{6\ell\leqslant|z|\leqslant 2|x|}\frac{\delta}{\zeta|z-x||z|}|{\rm d}z|\lesssim\frac{\delta\ln(\ell K)}{\ell\zeta},

(5.44)

and it follows from combining (
5.43
) and (
5.44
), and using (
5.28
)

\displaystyle\oint_{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant 3\ell}\frac{1}{\zeta}\left(\frac{\delta\ln(|z|K)}{|z|}+\int_{|x|\geqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}[z]/2}\frac{\partial_{x}{\mathcal{H}}(x,t){\rm d}x}{|z-x|}\right)\frac{\delta}{|z|}|{\rm d}z|\lesssim\frac{\delta^{2}\ln(\ell/\zeta\delta)}{\ell\zeta}.

(5.45)

By plugging (
5.41
), (
5.42
) and (
5.45
) into (
5.35
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\oint_{\omega}\ln(1+e^{m_{t}(z)+\tau g(z)})g(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau-\oint\int_{0}^{1}\ln(1+e^{m^{*}_{t}(z)+\tau g(z)})g(z){\rm d}z{\rm d}\tau\right|\\
&\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon\ell\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\frac{\delta^{2}\ln(\ell/\zeta\delta)}{\ell\zeta}+\delta\ln^{2}(\ell/\delta)\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon\ell\ln(\ell/\delta)}{\delta\zeta^{2}}+\delta\ln^{2}(\ell/\delta)\end{split}

(5.46)

The claim (
5.22
) follows from combining (
5.46
) and (
5.33
), and
\delta\ll\zeta\ell
from (
3.1
).

∎

5.4
Proof of
Proposition

5.5

In this section we prove
Proposition

5.5
. We recall the smoothed height function
\widetilde{H}
from (
4.9
), the associated complex slope
\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)
from (
4.10
), and the drift
g_{t}(z)
from (
4.13
). The following lemma collects some properties of
\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)
and
g_{t}(z)
from
Section

4.3

Lemma 5.6
.

The Markov process (
5.26
) satisfies

1.

There exists a constant
A=3\ell>0
, such that
\supp({\bm{x}}(t))\subset[-A,A]
.

2.

The height function
\widetilde{H}(x,t)\in\Adm(\mathfrak{R},h)
such that for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and a complex slope
f_{t}(x)\in\mathbb{H}^{-}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-\arg\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)=\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t),\\
&\arg(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(x))=\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t).\end{split}

3.

There exists a neighborhood
\Lambda
of
[-A,A]
, such that
\widetilde{f}_{t}(x)
extends continuously to
\Lambda\cap\overline{\mathbb{H}}
as

\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)=e^{\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}
, where
\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)=\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x){\rm d}x}{x-z},

(5.47)

and
g_{t}(z)
is analytic on
\Lambda
, and
g_{t}(\overline{z})=\overline{g_{t}(z)}
.

4.

We assume that
\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))
is well defined on
\Lambda\setminus[-A,A]
and is uniformly bounded on any compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[-A,A]
.

Proof.

The second statement is from the definition (
4.10
); the third and fourth statement follows from
Lemma

4.7
.
∎

From the second the third statement in
Lemma

5.6
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{m}_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x){\rm d}x}{w-x}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))}{(w-z)^{2}}{\rm d}z,

(5.48)

where the contour
\omega_{-}\in\Lambda
encloses
[-A,A]
, but not
w
. We can deform the contour
\omega_{-}
to enclose
w
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}\widetilde{m}_{t}(w)=\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(s){\rm d}s}{w-s}&=-\partial_{w}\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(w))+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))}{(w-z)^{2}}{\rm d}z\\
&=-\frac{(\partial_{w}\widetilde{m}_{t}(w)+\partial_{w}g_{t}(w))\widetilde{f}_{t}(w)}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(w)}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))}{(w-z)^{2}}{\rm d}z\\
&=-\frac{\partial_{w}\widetilde{m}_{t}(w)\widetilde{f}_{t}(w)}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(w)}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))}{(w-z)^{2}}{\rm d}z.\end{split}

(5.49)

where the contour
\omega\in\Lambda
encloses
[-A,A]
and
w
.

In the rest of this section we prove the uniqueness of the solutions of the equation (
5.48
). Assume that there is another solution
\widehat{f}_{t}(z)=e^{\widehat{m}_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}
satisfying (
5.48
)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{m}_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\widehat{\varrho}_{t}(s){\rm d}s}{w-s}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{\ln(1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z))}{(w-z)^{2}}{\rm d}z,

(5.50)

where
\widehat{m}_{t}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of
\widehat{\varrho}_{t}(s)
supported inside
[-A,A]
.

Proposition 5.7
.

If
\widehat{m}_{0}(z)=\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)
for any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[-A,A]
, then
\widehat{m}_{t}(z)=\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)
for any
t\geqslant 0
and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[-A,A]
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.7
.

Local uniqueness implies the global uniqueness. We only need to show that the statement of
Proposition

5.7
holds for
t\in[0,\delta]
for some small
\delta>0
. For the equation (
5.48
), we define the characteristic flow

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z_{t}=\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})+1}.

(5.51)

By plugging the characteristic flow (
5.51
) into (
5.50
), we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{m}_{t}(z_{t})=-\frac{\partial_{z}g_{t}(z_{t})\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(w))}{(z_{t}-w)^{2}}{\rm d}w.

(5.52)

We take a contour
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\in\Lambda
surrounding the interval
[-A,A]
, such that the characteristic flow (
5.51
) maps
z_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
to
z_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
for
t\geqslant 0
. For
t\leqslant\delta
, by the continuity of the characteristic flow, we have that
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
is inside an annulars region
S
, see
Figure

5

For
\delta>0
sufficiently small, there exists some constant
0<c<1
, the following holds uniformly for
z\in S
and
0\leqslant t\leqslant\delta
,

\displaystyle|\partial_{z}\widehat{m}_{t}(z)|\leqslant 1/c,\quad|\partial_{z}g_{t}(z)|\leqslant 1/c,\quad|\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|\leqslant 1/c,\quad|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|\geqslant c.

(5.53)

We denote the control parameter

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}:=\sup_{z\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}}|\widehat{m}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|.

Again for
\delta>0
sufficiently small, we can assume
\Gamma_{t}<c^{2}/4
.

Figure 5:
Shown above is the contour
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
inside the annulars region
S
.

We notice that
\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}|\widehat{m}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|=0
. By maximal principle,
|\widehat{m}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)|\leqslant\Gamma_{t}
for any
z
outside the contour
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
.

\displaystyle|\widehat{f}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|=|(e^{\widehat{m}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)}-1){\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}|\leqslant(e^{\Gamma_{t}}-1)|\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|\leqslant 2\Gamma_{t}/c\leqslant c/2,

where in the last two inequalities we used (
5.53
). It follows that
|1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z)|\geqslant|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|-|\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)-\widehat{f}_{t}(z)|\geqslant c/2
,
and

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}-\frac{\widehat{f}_{t}(z)}{1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z)}\right|=\frac{|\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)-\widehat{f}_{t}(z)|}{|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))(1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z))|}\leqslant\frac{2\Gamma_{t}/c}{(c/2)c}=\frac{4\Gamma_{t}}{c^{3}}.

(5.54)

And finally, we also have that

\displaystyle|\ln(1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z))-\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z))|=\left|1+\frac{\widehat{f}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)}\right|\leqslant\frac{|\widehat{f}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|}{|1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z)|}\leqslant\frac{2\Gamma_{t}/c}{c}=\frac{2\Gamma_{t}}{c^{2}}.

(5.55)

By plugging the characteristic flow (
5.51
) into (
5.50
), we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{m}_{t}(z_{t})=-\partial_{z}\widehat{m}_{t}(z_{t})\left(\frac{\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}-\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}\right)-\frac{\partial_{z}g_{t}(z_{t})\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widehat{f}_{t}(w))}{(z_{t}-w)^{2}}{\rm d}w,

(5.56)

where the the contour is bounded away from
z_{t}
, namely
|w-z_{t}|\geqslant c
. Moreover, the length of the contour is bounded by
10A
.

By taking the difference of (
5.52
) and (
5.56
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{t}(\widehat{m}_{t}(z_{t})-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z_{t}))&=-(\partial_{z}\widehat{m}_{t}(z_{t})+\partial_{z}g_{t}(z_{t}))\left(\frac{\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}-\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}\right)\\
&\phantom{{}={}}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widehat{f}_{t}(w))-\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(w))}{(z_{t}-w)^{2}}{\rm d}w.\end{split}

(5.57)

Thanks to (
5.53
), (
5.54
) and (
5.55
), the righthand side of (
5.57
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\left|(\partial_{z}\widehat{m}_{t}(z_{t})+\partial_{z}g_{t}(z_{t}))\left(\frac{\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widehat{f}_{t}(z_{t})}-\frac{\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}{1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(z_{t})}\right)\right|\leqslant\frac{2}{c}\frac{2\Gamma_{t}}{c^{2}}=\frac{4\Gamma_{t}}{c^{3}}.

and

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\ln(1+\widehat{f}_{t}(w))-\ln(1+\widetilde{f}_{t}(w))}{(z_{t}-w)^{2}}{\rm d}w\right|\leqslant\frac{2\Gamma_{t}}{c^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\omega}\frac{|{\rm d}w|}{|z_{t}-w|^{2}}\leqslant\frac{2\Gamma_{t}}{c^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{10A}{c^{2}}=\frac{10A\Gamma_{t}}{\pi c^{4}}.

It then follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Gamma_{t}\leqslant\frac{4\Gamma_{t}}{c^{3}}+\frac{10A\Gamma_{t}}{\pi c^{4}}=\left(\frac{4}{c^{3}}+\frac{10A}{\pi c^{4}}\right)\Gamma_{t}.

Since
\Gamma_{0}=0
, Grönwell inequality implies that
\Gamma_{t}=0
. Since
\widehat{m}_{t}(z)-\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)
is analytic outside
[-A,A]
, it follows that
\widehat{m}_{t}(z)=\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)
and the solution is unique.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.5
.

We denote the empirical particle density
\rho_{t}(x)=\rho(x;{\bm{x}}(t))
and its Stieltjes transform
m_{t}(z)
, and the complex slope

\displaystyle\rho_{t}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{1}([x_{i}(t),x_{i}(t)+\theta/N]){\rm d}x,\quad m_{t}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\rho_{t}(x)}{z-x}{\rm d}x,\quad f_{t}(z)=e^{m_{t}(z)+g_{t}(z)}\quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell.

(5.58)

Lemma

5.6
verifies that the transition probability (
5.26
) with parameters satisfying Assumptions
Assumption

A.1
and
Assumption

A.4
(with
({\bm{l}},{\bm{r}})=(-A,A)
) for all large enough
N
. Thanks to
Theorem

A.5
, the time difference of the Stieltjes transform for the empirical particle density (
5.58
) satisfy for for any
z\in\Lambda\setminus[-A,A]
, and
{\mathsf{t}}\in[0,{\mathsf{L}}]
, we have as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\displaystyle N\left(m_{t+1/N}(z)-m_{t}(z))\right)=\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln(1+f_{t}(z)){\rm d}w}{(w-z)^{2}}+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),\quad t={\mathsf{t}}/N.

(5.59)

Moreover,
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
are mean
0
random variables such that
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[-A,A]}
are asymptotically Gaussian with covariance given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}N\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z_{1}),\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z_{2})\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{f_{t}(z)}{1+f_{t}(z)}\frac{1}{(w-z_{1})^{2}}\frac{1}{(w-z_{2})^{2}}{\rm d}w+\oo(1),\end{split}

(5.60)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[-A,A]
, but not
z_{1},z_{2}
. We also have that the higher order joint moments of
\{\sqrt{N}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[-A,A]}
converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
to the Gaussian joint moments.

Let
C([0,\ell],\mathscr{M}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})])
denote the space of continuous functions from
[0,\ell]
to the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the weak topology of measures. Then
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(t))
represents a random element of this space. In fact, there is a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset C([0,\ell],\mathscr{M}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})])
, such that the distribution of
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(t))_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
is supported on
\mathcal{K}
— this is because measures
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(t))
are supported inside
[-A,A]
and the dependence on
t
is Lipschitz (each element of
{\bm{x}}(t)
jumps at most by
1/N
when
t
grows by
1/N
), cf.
[
3
, Lemma 4.3.13]
.

Since the space of probability measures on a compact set is compact, we conclude that the stochastic processes
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(t))_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
have subsequential limits in distribution as
N\to\infty
. We let
(\rho^{*}_{t})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell}
be one of the limiting points. Our task is to show that
\rho^{*}_{t}
are as described in
Proposition

5.5
(Implying, in particular, that all the limiting points are the same.) Note that for each
0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
,
\rho^{*}_{t}
is an absolutely continuous measures of density at most
1
, because so were the prelimit measures.

For any
t\in[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}}]\!]/N
, using (
5.59
), we have

\displaystyle m_{t}(z)=m_{0}(z)+\frac{1}{N}{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+\sum_{\tau\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}-1}]\!]}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta N}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln(1+f_{t}(z)){\rm d}w}{(w-z)^{2}}+\OO\left(\frac{1}{N}\right),

(5.61)

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
is a martingale given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{\tau\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{t}}-1}]\!]}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}(z).

(5.62)

We can estimate the
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
term by Doob/Kolmogorov’s inequality for martingales:

{\rm Prob}\left(\sup_{t\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{L}}}]\!]}|{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)/N|>\lambda\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}N^{2}}\sum_{\tau\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{T}}-1}]\!]}\mathbb{E}[\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau}(z)]^{2},\qquad\lambda>0.

Using (
5.60
), each term in the last sum is
\OO(1)
. There are
\OO(N)
terms and therefore the probability decays as
\OO(1/N\lambda^{2})
. We conclude that the martingale part in (
5.61
) goes to
0
in probability as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Hence,
N\rightarrow\infty
limit of (
5.61
) gives an integral equation for
\rho^{*}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}m^{*}_{t}(z)=\widetilde{m}_{0}(z)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln(1+f^{*}_{s}(z)){\rm d}w}{(w-z)^{2}}{\rm d}s,\end{split}

(5.63)

where
f^{*}_{s}(z)=e^{m^{*}_{s}(z)+g_{s}(z)}
is the limit of
f_{s}(z)
. We arrive at the same partial differential equation as (
5.48
), and
Proposition

5.7
implies that
m^{*}_{s}(z)=\widetilde{m}_{t}(z)
.
Proposition

5.5
follows.

∎

6
Asymptotics for Jack Polynomials

In this section we explain the correspondence between non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk ensembles and certain Jack ascending process (
6.13
). This correspondence will be used to derive large deviation asymptotics for (skew) Jack polynomials. We collect some basic properties of Jack symmetric functions in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.2
. We recall the Jack ascending process in
Section

6.3
. Our main references are
[
47
]
and
[
61
]
. In
Section

6.4
, we prove our main result
Theorem

1.7
.

6.1
Young Diagrams and Symmetric Functions

Given a Young diagram
{\bm{\lambda}}
, a box
\Box\in{\bm{\lambda}}
is a pair of integers,

\displaystyle\Box=(i,j)\in{\bm{\lambda}},\text{ if and only if }1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell(\bm{\lambda}),1\leqslant j\leqslant\lambda_{i}.

We denote
{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}
the transposed diagram of
\bm{\lambda}
, defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}^{\prime}=|\{i:1\leqslant j\leqslant\lambda_{i}\}|,\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant\lambda_{1}.

For a box
\Box=(i,j)\in{\bm{\lambda}}
, its arm
a_{\Box}
and leg
l_{\Box}
are

\displaystyle a_{\Box}=\lambda_{i}-j,\quad l_{\Box}=\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i.

Let
\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\cdots)
be an infinite set of indeterminates, and
\Sym
the graded algebra of symmetric functions in infinitely many variables over
{\mathbb{R}}
. We use the following notations for certain symmetric functions indexed by partitions
{\bm{\lambda}}
:

•

We denote the monomial symmetric functions
m_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}})
, which is defined as the sum of all monomials
\bm{x}^{{{\bm{\lambda}}_{\sigma}}}
where
{{\bm{\lambda}}_{\sigma}}
ranges over all distinct permutations of
{\bm{\lambda}}
.

•

We denote the power sum symmetric functions
p_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}})
, as

\displaystyle p_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}})=\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell({{\bm{\lambda}}})}p_{\lambda_{i}}({\bm{x}}),\quad p_{n}({\bm{x}})=x_{1}^{n}+x_{2}^{n}+x_{3}^{n}+\cdots.

Both the set of monomial symmetric functions
\{m_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}}):{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}\}
, and the set of power sum symmetric functions
\{p_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}}):{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}\}
form homogeneous bases of
\Sym
.

6.2
Jack Symmetric Polynomials

Let
\theta>0
be a parameter (indeterminante), and let
\mathbb{Q}(\theta)
denote the field of all rational functions of
\theta
with rational coefficients. Define a scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on the vector space
\Sym\otimes\mathbb{Q}(\theta)
of all symmetric functions over the field
\mathbb{Q}(\theta)
by the condition

\displaystyle\langle p_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}}),p_{{\bm{\mu}}}({\bm{x}})\rangle=\delta_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}z_{{\bm{\lambda}}}\theta^{-\ell({\bm{\lambda}})}.

(6.1)

We recall that a partial order on Young diagrams is obtained by declaring
\bm{\lambda}\preceq{\bm{\mu}}
if
|\bm{\lambda}|=|{\bm{\mu}}|
and
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{i}\leqslant\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\cdots+\mu_{i}
for all
i
. The following fundamental result is due to Macdonald, which characterizes Jack symmetric functions.

Let
\theta>0
be a parameter (indeterminante). Jack symmetric functions
J_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};\theta)
are elements of the algebra
\Sym
of the symmetric functions in infinitely many variables
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
uniquely determined by the following two properties:

1.

J_{\bm{\lambda}}
,
|\bm{\lambda}|=m
, can be expressed in terms of the monomial symmetric functions via a strictly upper unitriangular transition matrix:

J_{\bm{\lambda}}=m_{\bm{\lambda}}+\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}<{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}_{m}}R_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}m_{{\bm{\mu}}},

where
R_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}
are functions of
\theta
and
{\bm{\mu}}<{\bm{\lambda}}
is comparison in the dominance order on the set
\mathbb{Y}_{m}
of all partitions of
m
(equivalently, Young diagrams with
m
boxes).

2.

They are pairwise orthogonal with respect to the scalar product defined on the power sums via

\displaystyle\langle p_{\bm{\lambda}},p_{\bm{\mu}}\rangle_{\theta}=\delta_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}z_{\bm{\lambda}}\theta^{-\ell(\bm{\lambda})},\qquad z_{\bm{\lambda}}=\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lambda_{1}}i^{m_{i}({\bm{\lambda}})}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant\lambda_{1}}m_{i}({\bm{\lambda}})!,

(6.2)

where
{\bm{\lambda}}=1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\dots
, i.e.
m_{i}
is the multiplicity of
i
in
{\bm{\lambda}}
,
\ell({\bm{\lambda}})
is the number of rows, and
p_{k}=(x_{1})^{k}+(x_{2})^{k}+\dots
,
k\geqslant 1
.

We recall the scalar product on
\Sym
as defined in (
6.2
), and introduce the following notation,

\displaystyle j_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(\theta)=\langle J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}};\theta),J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}};\theta)\rangle.

(6.3)

The number
j_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(\theta)
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle j_{{\bm{\lambda}}}=\prod_{\Box\in{\bm{\lambda}}}\frac{a_{\Box}+\theta l_{\Box}+1}{a_{\Box}+\theta l_{\Box}+\theta}.

(6.4)

See
[
61
, Theorem 5.8]
for a proof.
We denote the dual polynomial
\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}}({\bm{x}};\theta)=J_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};\theta)/j_{\bm{\lambda}}(\theta)
.
Finally, the skew Jack polynomials
J_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}},\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
are defined through the expansions:

\displaystyle J_{\bm{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{1},y_{2},\dots;\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}}J_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(x_{1},x_{1},\dots;\theta)J_{\bm{\mu}}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots;\theta),

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{\bm{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{1},y_{2},\dots;\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}}\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(x_{1},x_{1},\dots;\theta)\widetilde{J}_{\bm{\mu}}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots;\theta).

Theorem 6.1
.

We have

\displaystyle J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1^{N};\theta)=\prod_{\Box\in{\bm{\lambda}}}\frac{N\theta+(j-1)-\theta(i-1)}{a_{\Box}+\theta l_{\Box}+\theta}.

(6.5)

Proof.

See
[
61
, Proposition 2.3]
.
∎

The following lemma gives the asymptotics of the Jack symmetric polynomials at the principal specialization

Lemma 6.2
.

Given a Young diagram
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}_{N}
, we identify it as a particle configuration

\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}=({\mathsf{x}}_{1},{\mathsf{x}}_{2},\dots,{\mathsf{x}}_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N},\quad{\mathsf{x}}_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\theta(i-1)\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N,

(6.6)

then

\displaystyle J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1^{N};\theta)=\prod_{i<j}\frac{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(\theta)}{\Gamma(i\theta)}.

(6.7)

Given a sequence of Young diagrams

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}^{(N)}=(\lambda_{1}^{(N)}\geqslant\lambda_{2}^{(N)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N}^{(N)})\in\mathbb{Y}_{N},\quad N\geqslant 1

such that

1.

There exists a constant
C>0
,
\lambda_{1}^{(N)}\leqslant CN

2.

There exists a
1
-Lipschitz nondecreasing function
h:{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto[0,\theta]
, and when
N\rightarrow\infty

\displaystyle\frac{\theta}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}^{(N)}-(i-1)\theta}{N}\right)\rightarrow\partial_{x}h(x),

in distribution. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{(N)}}(1^{N};\theta)=\frac{1}{2\theta}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x){\rm d}h(y)-\frac{\theta\ln(\theta)}{2}+\frac{3\theta}{4}.

(6.8)

Proof.

We can reorganize the numerator and denominator in (
6.5
), and rewrite it in terms of the particle configuration
\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}
from (
6.6
).

\displaystyle\prod_{\Box\in{\bm{\lambda}}}N\theta+(j-1)-\theta(i-1)=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(N\theta+{\mathsf{x}}_{i})}{\Gamma((N-i+1)\theta)}=\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(N\theta+{\mathsf{x}}_{i})}{\Gamma(i\theta)},

and

\displaystyle\prod_{\Box\in{\bm{\lambda}}}\frac{1}{a_{\Box}+\theta l_{\Box}+\theta}=\prod_{i\leqslant j}\frac{\Gamma(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}+\theta(j-i)+\theta)}{\Gamma(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j+1}+\theta(j-i)+\theta)}=\Gamma(\theta)^{N}\prod_{i}\frac{1}{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+N\theta)}\prod_{i<j}\frac{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j})}.

The claim (
6.7
) follows from combining the above two expressions.

For the asymptotics (
6.8
), simply write

\lambda_{i}^{(N)}-(i-1)\theta={\mathsf{x}}_{i}
.
We notice that there exists
C=C(\theta)>0
, Stirling’s formula implies that the log Gamma function satisfies
(z-1/2)\ln z-z-C\leqslant\ln\Gamma(z)\leqslant(z-1/2)\ln z-z+C
for
z\geqslant\theta
, and
|\partial_{z}\ln\Gamma(z)-\ln z|\leqslant C/z
for
z\geqslant\theta
.

So we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\ln\Gamma(\theta)-\ln\Gamma(i\theta))&=-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(i\theta\ln(i\theta/e)+\OO(N)\right)\\
&=-\frac{\theta\ln N}{2}-\theta\int_{0}^{1}x\ln x{\rm d}x-\frac{\theta}{2}\ln(\theta/e)+\OO(\ln N/N)\\
&=-\frac{\theta\ln N}{2}-\frac{\theta\ln(\theta)}{2}+\frac{3\theta}{4}+\OO(\ln N/N),\end{split}

(6.9)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\sum_{i<j}\ln\frac{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j})}=\sum_{i<j}\theta\ln({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j})+\OO\left(\sum_{i<j}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}\right)\\
&=\sum_{i<j}\theta\ln({\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j})+\OO\left(N\ln N\right)=N^{2}\left(\frac{\theta\ln N}{2}+\frac{1}{2\theta}\iint_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x){\rm d}h(y)\right)+\OO(N\ln N).\end{split}

(6.10)

where we used that
{\mathsf{x}}_{i}-{\mathsf{x}}_{j}\geqslant\theta(i-j)
for the second inequality and in the last equality we used
Lemma

2.7
. The claim (
6.8
) follows from plugging (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) into (
6.7
).
∎

6.3
Jack Process

Computations in the algebra of symmetric functions
\Sym
can be converted into numeric identities by means of
specializations
, which are algebra homomorphism from
\Sym
to the set of complex numbers. A specialization
\rho
is uniquely determined by its values on any set of algebraic generators of
\Sym
and we use
(p_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
as such generators. The value of
\rho
on a symmetric function
f
is denoted
f(\rho)
.
Given two specializations
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, we define their union
(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
through the formula:

p_{k}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=p_{k}(\rho)+p_{k}(\rho^{\prime}),\quad k\geqslant 1.

A specialization
\rho
is an algebra homomorphism from
\Sym
to the set of complex numbers. A specialization
\rho
is called
Jack-positive
if its values on all (skew) Jack symmetric functions with a fixed
\theta
are real and non-negative, i.e.,

\displaystyle J_{\bm{\lambda}}(\rho;\theta)=\rho(J_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};\theta))\geqslant 0,

for all
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}
. The set of Jack positive specializations are classified by
Kerov, Okounkov and Olshanski in

[
43
, Theorem A]
(We slightly modify the statement (our
\beta_{i}
is
\theta\beta_{i}
in
[
43
, Theorem A]
) such that it matches with
Theorem

C.1
).

Theorem 6.3
.

For any fixed
\theta>0
, Jack positive specializations can be parameterized by triplets
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\gamma)
, where
\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta}
are sequences of real numbers with

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant 0,\quad\beta_{1}\geqslant\beta_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant 0,\quad\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}+\frac{\beta_{i}}{\theta}<\infty,

and
\gamma
is a non-negative real number. The specialization corresponding to a triplet
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\gamma)
is given by its values on Newton power sums
p_{k}
,

p_{1}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}+\frac{1}{\theta}\left(\gamma+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\beta_{i}\right),\qquad p_{k}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\alpha_{i})^{k}+\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\beta_{i})^{k}.

Following
[
28
, Section 2.3]
, we create Markov chains out of the Jack-positive specializations:

Definition 6.4
.

Given two specializations
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
we define the ascending transition through

\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}\mid{\bm{\mu}})=\frac{1}{H_{\theta}(\rho;\rho^{\prime})}\frac{J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(\rho;\theta)}{J_{{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho;\theta)}\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho^{\prime};\theta),

(6.11)

where
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
are partitions with
{\bm{\mu}}\subset{\bm{\lambda}}
and the normalization constant
H_{\theta}(\rho;\rho^{\prime})
is given by

H_{\theta}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\exp\left\{\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\frac{\theta}{k}p_{k}(\rho)p_{k}(\rho^{\prime})\right\}.

We further use the
w_{\beta}
automorphism of the algebra of symmetric functions
\Sym
defined on the power sums by:

w_{\beta}(p_{k})=(-1)^{k-1}\beta p_{k}.

As shown in
[
46
, Chapter VI, Section 10, (10.6)]
,

w_{\theta^{-1}}\bigl(J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}/{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}}(\cdot;\theta)\bigr)=\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\cdot;\theta^{-1}),

where
{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}
and
{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}
are transposed Young diagrams
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
, respectively. Hence, with the specialization
\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})
, as in (
6.11
), we have

\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1});\theta)=J_{{\bm{\lambda}}^{\prime}/{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}}(\bm{\alpha}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1});\theta^{-1}).

To obtain the asymptotics of skew Jack polynomials, we study the following special Jack ascending process. We fix
N,{\mathsf{T}}\geqslant 1
, and a sequence of positive numbers
b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1}
. The transition probability at time
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
is given in the notations of Theorem
6.3
as

\rho:\,\alpha_{i}=1,\,1\leqslant i\leqslant N;\qquad\rho^{\prime}:\beta_{1}=b_{\mathsf{t}},

(6.12)

with all other parameters set to
0
. The transition probability at time
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)={\bm{\lambda}}|{\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{t}})={\bm{\mu}})=\frac{1}{H_{\theta}(1^{N},\beta_{1}=b_{\mathsf{t}})}\frac{J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1^{N};\theta)}{J_{{\bm{\mu}}}(1^{N};\theta)}\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\beta_{1}=b_{{\mathsf{t}}};\theta).

(6.13)

Then the transition probability gives the skew Jack polynomials

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{T}})={\bm{\lambda}}|{\bm{\lambda}}(0)={\bm{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1}\frac{1}{(1+b_{\mathsf{t}})^{N}}\frac{J_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(1^{N};\theta)}{J_{{\bm{\mu}}}(1^{N};\theta)}\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1});\theta).

(6.14)

The following claim states that we can encode the Jack ascending process
6.13
of Young diagrams as an
N
-particle non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walk.

Claim 6.5
.

The transition probability of the Jack process
6.13
is non-degenerate only for partitions
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
with at most
N
parts, i.e.
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{N})
and
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{1}\geqslant\mu_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\mu_{N})
. Further, if we identify

\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}=({\mathsf{x}}_{1},{\mathsf{x}}_{2},\dots,{\mathsf{x}}_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{N},\quad{\mathsf{x}}_{i}=\mu_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad{\mathsf{x}}_{i}+e_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant N,

(6.15)

then
e_{i}\in\{0,1\}
and the transition probability is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}}}(\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}+{\bm{e}}|\bm{{\mathsf{x}}})=\frac{1}{(1+b_{\mathsf{t}})^{N}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\frac{({\mathsf{x}}_{i}+\theta e_{i})-({\mathsf{x}}_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{{{\mathsf{x}}_{i}}-{{\mathsf{x}}_{j}}}b_{\mathsf{t}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}}.

(6.16)

6.4
Proof of
Theorem

1.7

Thanks to the relation (
6.14
), the large deviation asymptotics of the skew Jack polynomials can be obtained from the large deviation principle of the dynamics (
6.16
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\widetilde{J}_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1});\theta)=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln\left(\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}}}({\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{T}})={\bm{\lambda}}|{\bm{\lambda}}(0)={\bm{\mu}})H(1^{N},\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1}))\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{2\theta}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x,0){\rm d}h(y,0)-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\ln|x-y|{\rm d}h(x,T){\rm d}h(y,T)\right)+\oo(1),\end{split}

(6.17)

where we used
Equation

6.6
.
The dynamics (
6.16
) and the non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli random walks (
1.8
) differ by the following drift

\displaystyle{D(\{{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}})}:=\prod_{0\leq{\mathsf{t}}\leq\mathsf{T-1}}2^{N}b_{\mathsf{t}}^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}e_{i}(\mathsf{t})},\quad H(1^{N},\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1})){\rm d}\mathbb{P}^{{\mathsf{b}}}=D(\{\sf{\bm{x}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}){\rm d}\mathbb{P}.

Theorem

1.7
follows from plugging the following lemma to (
6.17
).

Lemma 6.6
.

Adopt the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

1.7
, and denote the height function of
\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
as
H(x,t)
, then the following holds

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln{D(\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}})}=\frac{1}{\theta}\mathcal{F}^{f}(H)+T\ln 2+\OO(1/N),

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{f}(H):=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(s)\partial_{s}H(y,s){\rm d}y{\rm d}s.

(6.18)

Moreover,
{\mathcal{F}}^{f}
is continuous on the space of Lipschitz functions with uniform norm.

Proof.

We notice that since
e_{i}(\mathsf{t})=\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t}+1)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t})
and
b_{\mathsf{t}}=e^{f({\mathsf{t}}/N)}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln{D(\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}})}&=N{\mathsf{T}}\ln 2+\sum_{0\leq{\mathsf{t}}\leq\mathsf{T}-1}f\left({\frac{\mathsf{t}}{N}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t}+1)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t}))\\
&=N{\mathsf{T}}\ln 2+\sum_{1\leq{\mathsf{t}}\leq\mathsf{T}}f\left({\frac{\mathsf{t-1}}{N}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t})-\sum_{0\leq{\mathsf{t}}\leq{\mathsf{T}}-1}f\left({\frac{\mathsf{t}}{N}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t})\\
&=N{\mathsf{T}}\ln 2+\sum_{1\leq{\mathsf{t}}\leq\mathsf{T}-1}\left(f\left(\frac{\mathsf{t-1}}{N}\right)-f\left({\frac{\mathsf{t}}{N}}\right)\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{t})+f\left({\frac{\mathsf{T}}{N}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{T})-f(0)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(0)\\
&=N{\mathsf{T}}\ln 2+\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{s}f(s)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\lfloor\mathsf{N}s\rfloor){\rm d}s+f(T)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{T})-f(0)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(0).\end{split}

On the other hand, for
t\in[{\mathsf{t}}/N,({\mathsf{t}}+1)/N]
, we have set
x(t)=\mathsf{x}(\mathsf{t})/N+(Nt-\mathsf{t})e(\mathsf{t})/N
, so that for all integer number
\mathsf{t}
,

\sup_{t\in[{\mathsf{t}}/N,({\mathsf{t}}+1)/N]}|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\lfloor\mathsf{N}t\rfloor)-N\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}(t)|\leqslant N.

Therefore, we deduce that

\displaystyle\ln{D(\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}})}=N{\mathsf{T}}\ln 2-N\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{s}f(s)\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}(s){\rm d}s+f(T)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(\mathsf{T})-f(0)\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathsf{x}_{i}(0)+\OO(N),

where the
\OO(N)
error is given as
N\theta^{-1}\int_{0}^{T}|\partial_{s}f(s)|{\rm d}s
.
Now it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}y\rho(y;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}y\sum_{i=1}^{N}{1}_{[x_{i}(t),x_{i}(t)+\theta/N]}(y){\rm d}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\left(x_{i}(t)+\frac{\theta}{N}\right)^{2}-x_{i}(t)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\theta}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}(t)+\OO(1/N).

By integration by parts we also have since
H(x;{\bm{x}}(t))=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\rho(y;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}y
,

\frac{\theta}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}(t)+\OO(1/N)=\int y\rho(y,{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}y=[yH(y,t)]_{-K}^{K}-\int_{-K}^{K}H(y,t){\rm d}y,

where we notice that we can restrict the integral on the left to a compact set where the particles lives, say
[-K,K]
that can be chosen independent of the time (provided the particles at time
0
are bounded and
T
is also bounded). Then

[yH(y;{\bm{x}}(t))]_{-K}^{K}=\theta K
is independent of
t
.

As a conclusion, we get:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\ln{D(\{\bm{{\mathsf{x}}}({\mathsf{t}})\}_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{s}f(s)\int_{-K}^{K}H(y,s){\rm d}y{\rm d}s-f(T)\int_{-K}^{K}H(y,T){\rm d}y+f(0)\int_{-K}^{K}H(y,0){\rm d}y\right)

\displaystyle+T\ln 2+\OO(1/N).

Noticing that
H(y,s)
is Lipschitz, thus differentiable.
The claim (
6.18
) follows from an integration by part. Finally, it is clear that if
\partial_{s}f
is bounded,
\mathcal{F}^{f}
is continuous for the uniform norm.
∎

Appendix A
Dynamical Loop Equation

We define the particle configuration:

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigl\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid x_{i}>x_{i+1},\quad i=1,2,\dots,n-1\bigr\}.

(A.1)

Given a particle configuration
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}^{n}
, an interaction function
b(z)
, and weight functions
\phi^{+}(z),\phi^{-}(z)
. Our central object is the following transition probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{x}})}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta e_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}},

(A.2)

where
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
Z({\bm{x}})
is a normalization constant.

Assumption A.1
.

Fix constants
{\mathsf{N}}>0
and
{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}
.
We introduce a small parameter
\varepsilon={\mathsf{N}}/n\ll 1
, and assume the particle configuration
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\widetilde{\mathbb{W}}_{\theta}^{n}
satisfies
{\bm{l}}\leqslant\varepsilon x_{n}\leqslant\varepsilon x_{1}\leqslant{\bm{r}}-\varepsilon
. We further assume

1.

There exists an open complex neighborhood
\Lambda
of
[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]
, such that for
z=\varepsilon\xi
,

\displaystyle b(\xi)=z\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi^{\pm}(\xi)={\varphi}^{\pm}(z),\quad z\in\Lambda.

(A.3)

where
z
and
\varphi^{\pm}(z)
are holomorphic functions on
\Lambda
. In addition, we require
z
to be conformal (injective and biholomorphic) and
{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{z})=\overline{z}
.

2.

The functions
z
,
[\partial_{z}z]^{-1}
, and
\varphi^{\pm}(z)
are uniformly bounded, namely there exists a universal
1
1

1

By “universal” we mean not depending on
n
,
\varepsilon
, or
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle|z|\leqslant C,\quad|\partial_{z}z|\geqslant 1/C,\ \quad|\varphi^{\pm}(z)|\leqslant C,\quad z\in\Lambda.

(A.4)

Remark A.2
.

In
[
24
]
, the particle configurations are assumed to be inside the lattice:

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigl\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid x_{1}\in\mathbb{Z},\quad x_{i}-x_{i+1}\in\theta+\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},\quad i=1,2,\dots,n-1\bigr\}.

Here we relaxed the particle configuration to (
A.1
).

The dynamical loop equation states that the following observable is a holomorphic function.

Theorem A.3

(Dynamical Loop Equation)
.

Choose an open set
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, a particle configuration
{{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
such that the interval
[x_{n},x_{1}]\subset U
, a parameter
\theta>0
, two holomorphic functions
\phi^{+}(z)
,
\phi^{-}(z)
on
U
and a conformal (i.e., holomorphic and injective) function
b(z)
on
U
. Assume that the random
n
–tuple
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is distributed according to the transition probability (
A.2
). Then the following observable is a holomorphic function of
z\in U
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{b(z+\theta)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(z)-b(x_{j})}+\phi^{-}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{b(z)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(z)-b(x_{j})}\right].

(A.5)

Proof.

See
[
24
, Proof of Theorem 1.1]

∎

Our last assumption for the asymptotic analysis involves the following functions defined in terms of the empirical density (
1.10
) and functions
\phi^{\pm}(z)
of Assumption
A.1
:

{\mathcal{B}}(z)={\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(z)+\varphi^{-}(z),\quad\quad{\mathcal{G}}(z)=\exp\left[\theta\int_{{\bm{l}}}^{{\bm{r}}}\frac{\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{z-s}{\rm d}s\right].

(A.6)

Note that
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
is a holomorphic function for
z\in\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]
.

Assumption A.4
.

There exists annular set
S\subset\bigl(\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]\bigr)
(containing a contour surrounding
[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]
), a small universal constant
c>0
such that for
z\in S
we have
c<|{\mathcal{B}}(z)|<c^{-1}
. Moreover, for any closed contour
\omega\subset\bigl(\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]\bigr)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{B}}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}(z)}{\rm d}z=0,

(A.7)

which implies that there exists a well-defined single-valued branch of the function
\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)
in
S
.

Theorem A.5
.

Consider transition probability (
A.2
) with parameters satisfying Assumptions
A.1
and
A.4
for all small enough
\varepsilon
. Then for any
z\in\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]
we have as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{{\bm{l}}}^{{\bm{r}}}\frac{(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{z-s}{\rm d}s=\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\rm d}w}{(w-z)^{2}}+\OO\left(\varepsilon\right),

Moreover,
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
are mean
0
random variables such that
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]}
are asymptotically Gaussian with covariance given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z_{1}),\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z_{2})\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(w)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z_{1})}{(w-{\mathsf{b}}(z_{1}))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z_{2})}{(w-{\mathsf{b}}(z_{2}))^{2}}{\rm d}w,\end{split}

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]
, but not
z_{1},z_{2}
. The higher order joint moments of
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]}
converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
to the Gaussian joint moments.

The implicit constants in the
\OO(\varepsilon)
are uniform in all the involved parameters, as long as the constants
C
and
c
of Assumptions
A.1
and
A.4
are fixed and
z
belongs to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[{\bm{l}},{\bm{r}}]
.

Appendix B
Proofs for Results from
Section

2.1
and
Section

4.3

Proof of
Lemma

2.1
.

In this proof, for simplicity of notations, we omit the dependence on
N
and simply write
\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}^{(N)}
and
\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}^{(N)}
.
By our assumption
{\mathcal{P}}(\bm{{\mathsf{y}}},\bm{{\mathsf{z}}};{\mathsf{T}})\neq\emptyset
, there exists a non-intersecting Bernoulli paths
\{{\bm{y}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
from
{\bm{y}}(0)=\bm{{\mathsf{y}}}/N
to
{\bm{y}}(T)=\bm{{\mathsf{z}}}/N
.

Next we construct a non-intersecting Bernoulli paths
\{{\bm{z}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
, such that its height function approximates
H^{*}
. First we can construct the level line of
H^{*}
as

\displaystyle\gamma(y,t)=\inf\{x:H^{*}(x,t)>y\},\quad 0\leqslant y<\theta.

Since
H^{*}
is
2
-Lipschitz we have
H^{*}(\gamma(y,t),t)=y
for any
0\leqslant y<\theta
.
Since
\nabla H^{*}\in\overline{{\mathcal{T}}}
, for any
s\geqslant t
,

\displaystyle H^{*}(\gamma(y,t),s)\leqslant H^{*}(\gamma(y,t),t)\leqslant H^{*}(\gamma(y,t)+(s-t),s).

We conclude that
\gamma(y,t)\leqslant\gamma(y,s)\leqslant\gamma(y,t)+(s-t)
. It follows that
\gamma(y,t)
is
1
-Lipschitz in
t
, and
\partial_{t}\gamma(y,t)\in[0,1]
.

We take
m=\lceil\varepsilon N/(2\theta)\rceil
, and construct the nonintersecting Bernoulli paths
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
in the following way: the path
\{x_{i}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is obtained from
\{y_{i}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
by truncating from left using
\gamma(\theta(i-m)/N,t)
, and from right using
\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)
. Here we make the convention
\gamma(y,s)=-\infty
if
y<0
, and
\gamma(y,s)=+\infty
if
y>1
. Formally,
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
is given as for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
and
t\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{T}}}]\!]

\displaystyle x_{i}(t)=\argmin\{|x-y_{i}(t)|:x-y_{i}(t)\in N^{-1}\mathbb{Z},\gamma(\theta(i-m)/N,t)\leqslant x\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)\}.

(B.1)

We denote the height function of
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
as
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Next we check that
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
forms a non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk from
{\mathsf{y}}^{(N)}/N
to
{\mathsf{z}}^{(N)}/N
. Under assumption (
1.16
), for
N
large enough

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|{\mathcal{H}}(x,0)-h(x,0)|,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|{\mathcal{H}}(x,T)-h(x,T)|\leqslant\varepsilon/(4\theta).

It follows that for
t\in\{0,T\}
,

\displaystyle h(y_{i}(t),t)\geqslant{\mathcal{H}}(y_{i}(t),t)-\varepsilon/4=\frac{\theta(i-1)}{N}-\varepsilon/4\geqslant h(\gamma(\theta(i-m)/N,t),

\displaystyle h(y_{i}(t),t)\leqslant{\mathcal{H}}(y_{i}(t),t)+\varepsilon/4=\frac{\theta(i-1)}{N}+\varepsilon/4\leqslant h(\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t),

And thus
\gamma(\theta(i-m)/N,t)\leqslant y_{i}(t)=x_{i}(t)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)
for
t\in\{0,T\}
.

We need to show
x_{i}(t+1/N)-x_{i}({\mathsf{t}}/N)\in\{0,1\}
and
x_{i+1}(t)-x_{i}(t)\geqslant\theta/N
for any
t={\mathsf{t}}/N\in N^{-1}[\![{0,{\mathsf{T}}}]\!]
. By symmetry, we assume
y_{i}(t)\geqslant\gamma_{i}(t)
, so it is far from
\gamma_{i-m}(t)
. In fact we have

\displaystyle y_{i}(t),y_{i+1}(t),y_{i}(t+1/N),y_{i+1}(t+1/N)\geqslant\gamma_{i-m}(t)+\varepsilon/2.

(B.2)

Notice that under (
B.2
) we only need to deal with the possible truncation caused by
\gamma_{i+m}(t)
. There are still several cases

1.

If
\gamma(\theta(i-m)/N,t)\leqslant y_{i}(t)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)
, then
x_{i}(t)=y_{i}(t)
. If also
x_{i}(t+1/N)=y_{i}(t+1/N)
, then
x_{i}(t+1/N)-x_{i}(t)=y_{i}(t+1/N)-y_{i}(t)\in\{0,1/N\}
; otherwise
y_{i}(t+1/N)>\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)
. Recall that
\partial_{s}\gamma(y,s)\in[0,1]
, we must have

\displaystyle y_{i}(t+1/N)>\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)\geqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)\geqslant y_{i}(t)\Rightarrow y_{i}(t+1/N)=y_{i}(t)+1/N.

In this case
x_{i}(t+1/N)=y_{i}(t)
, and
x_{i}(t+1/N)-x_{i}(t)=0
.

If
x_{i+1}(t)=y_{i+1}(t)
, then
x_{i+1}(t)-x_{i}(t)=y_{i+1}(t)-y_{i}(t)\geqslant\theta/N
; otherwise
y_{i+1}(t)>\gamma(\theta(i+m+1)/N,t)
. Recall that
\gamma(y+\theta/N,s)-\gamma(y,s)\geqslant\theta/N
, we must have

\displaystyle y_{i+1}(t)>\gamma(\theta(1+i+m)/N,t+1/N)\geqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)+\theta/N\geqslant y_{i}(t)+\theta/N.

In this case
x_{i+1}(t)\geqslant y_{i}(t)+\theta/N=x_{i}(t)+\theta/N
.

2.

If
y_{i}(t)>\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)
, then
x_{i}(t)=y_{i}(t)-\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. For
y_{i}(t+1/N)
there are two cases: If
y_{i}(t+1/N)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)
, recalling that
\partial_{s}\gamma(y,s)\in[0,1]
, then we must have

\displaystyle y_{i}(t+1/N)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)+1/N<y_{i}(t)+1/N.

In this case
y_{i}(t)=y_{i}(t+1/N)=x_{i}(t+1/N)
, and it follows

\displaystyle y_{i}(t)-1/N=y_{i}(t+1/N)-1/N\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)-1/N\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t).

So
x_{i}(t)=y_{i}(t)-1/N
and
x_{i}(t+1/N)-x_{i}(t)=1/N
.

Otherwise if
y_{i}(t+1/N)\geqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)
, recall
\partial_{t}\gamma(y,t)\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle x_{i}(t)\leqslant x_{i}(t+1/N)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t+1/N)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)+1/N<x_{i}(t)+1/N+1/N.

In this case we still have
x_{i}(t+1/N)-x_{i}(t)\in\{0,1/N\}
.

For
y_{i+1}(t)
there are two cases: If
y_{i+1}(t)\leqslant\gamma(\theta(1+i+m)/N,t+1/N)
, then
x_{i+1}(t)=y_{i+1}(t)
and
x_{i+1}(t)-x_{i}(t)\geqslant y_{i+1}(t)-y_{i}(t)\geqslant\theta/N
.
Otherwise if
y_{i+1}(t)>\gamma(\theta(1+i+m)/N,t+1/N)
, recall that
\gamma(y+\theta/N,s)-\gamma(y,s)\geqslant\theta/N
, so

\displaystyle x_{i}(t)+\theta/N\leqslant\gamma(\theta(i+m)/N,t)+\theta/N\leqslant\gamma(\theta(1+i+m)/N,t),

and
x_{i+1}(t)\geqslant x_{i}(t)+\theta/N
.

Finally we check that the height function
{\mathcal{H}}
of
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
as constructed in (
B.1
) satisfies (
2.1
). For any
x_{i}(t)\leqslant x<x_{i+1}(t)
, then
\gamma(\theta(i-m)/N)\leqslant x<\gamma(\theta(1+i+m)/N)

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{H}}(x,t)-\frac{\theta i}{N}\right|\leqslant\frac{\theta}{N}.

(B.3)

and

\displaystyle\max\left\{0,\frac{\theta(i-m)}{N}\right\}\leqslant H^{*}(x,t)\leqslant\min\left\{\theta,\frac{\theta(1+i+m)}{N}\right\},

(B.4)

The claim (
2.1
) follows from (
B.3
) and (
B.4
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.5
.

The statement (
5.40
) follows from (
4.9
) and
\nabla{\mathcal{A}}(x,t)=(\varrho,-\varrho v)\bm{1}(tv\leqslant x\leqslant 1+tv)
.
On the interval
[tv,\ell+tv]
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{t}(x)=\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{\delta{\rm d}y}{(y-x)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\asymp 1,

and

\displaystyle 1-\kappa_{t}(x)=\int_{[tv,\ell+tv]^{\complement}}\frac{\delta{\rm d}y}{(y-x)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\asymp\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}.

Outside the interval
[tv,\ell+tv]
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa_{t}(x)=\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{\delta{\rm d}y}{(y-x)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\delta+\dist(x,[tv,\ell+tv])+\dist(x,[tv,\ell+tv])^{2}/\ell},

and

\displaystyle 1-\kappa_{t}(x)=\int_{[tv,\ell+tv]^{\complement}}\frac{\delta{\rm d}y}{(y-x)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\asymp 1.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.6
.

From the expression (
4.34
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\kappa_{t}(x)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{{\rm d}y}{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-y}&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{{\rm d}y}{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-y}-\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{{\rm d}y}{(x-\mathrm{i}\delta)-y}\right)\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\ln\frac{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}-\ln\frac{(x-\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(x-\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right),\end{split}

(B.5)

where
\ln(\cdot)
is the branch on
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus[-\infty,0]
.
And

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Hib(\kappa_{t})(x)=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{{\rm d}y}{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-y}&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{{\rm d}y}{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-y}+\int_{tv}^{\ell+tv}\frac{{\rm d}y}{(x-\mathrm{i}\delta)-y}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\ln\frac{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(x+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}+\ln\frac{(x-\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(x-\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right).\end{split}

(B.6)

The expression (
B.5
) is well defined for
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|<\delta
. Thus
\kappa_{t}(x)
extends analytically to
\kappa_{t}(z)
for
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|<\delta
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{z}\kappa_{t}(z)&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\partial_{z}\left(\ln\frac{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}-\ln\frac{(z-\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(z-\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right)\\
&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}-\frac{1}{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}-\frac{1}{(z-\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}+\frac{1}{(z-\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right)\\
&=\frac{\delta}{(z-\ell-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}}-\frac{\delta}{(z-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}}.\end{split}

(B.7)

If
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})
, the first statement in the claim (
4.40
) follows from (
B.7
).

For
z=x+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|\leqslant\delta/2

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\delta}{(z-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\right|\leqslant\frac{\delta}{(x-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\delta^{2}+(x-tv)^{2}},

(B.8)

and similarly

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\delta}{(z-\ell-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\right|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\delta^{2}+(x-\ell-tv)^{2}},

(B.9)

We conclude that by plugging (
B.8
) and (
B.9
) into (
B.7
),

\displaystyle|\partial_{z}\kappa_{t}(z)|\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\delta^{2}+\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})^{2}}\lesssim\frac{\min\{\kappa_{t}(x),1-\kappa_{t}(x)\}}{\delta},

(B.10)

where the last inequality is from
Lemma

4.5
. The claim (
4.37
) follows from integrating (
B.10
) from
x
to
x+\mathrm{i}\eta
.

The expression of the Hilbert transform (
B.11
) also extends analytically to
\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z)
for
|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|<\delta
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\ln\frac{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}+\ln\frac{(z-\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(z-\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right).\end{split}

(B.11)

For
z=x+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
|\eta|\leqslant\delta/2
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\ln\frac{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-(\ell+tv)}{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right|\leqslant\ln\left|1+\frac{\ell}{(z+\mathrm{i}\delta)-tv}\right|+\pi\leqslant\ln(\ell/\delta)+\OO(1),

and we have the same estimate for the second term on the righthand side of (
B.11
). This gives the first statement of (
2
). To prove the second statement of (
2
), we can rewrite (
B.11
) as,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\ln((z-\ell-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2})-\ln((z-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2})\right).\end{split}

(B.12)

If
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\gtrsim\ell
, from (
B.11
), we have

\displaystyle\Hib(\kappa_{t}(z)\lesssim\frac{\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})},

this gives the second statement in (
4.40
).

For
z=x+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
|\eta|\leqslant\delta/2
, we have

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln((z-\ell-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2})|=|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln(\mathrm{i}2\eta(x-\ell-tv)+(x-\ell-tv)^{2}+(\delta^{2}-\eta^{2}))|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[\ln(x-\ell-tv)^{2}+(\delta^{2}-\eta^{2})]+\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln\left(1+\frac{\mathrm{i}2\eta(x-\ell-tv)}{(x-\ell-tv)^{2}+(\delta^{2}-\eta^{2})}\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{|\eta|}{\sqrt{\delta^{2}-\eta^{2}}}\lesssim\frac{\eta}{\delta},

where in the last two inequalities we used that
(x-\ell-tv)^{2}+(\delta^{2}-\eta^{2})\geqslant 2|x-\ell-tv|\sqrt{\delta^{2}-\eta^{2}}
. We have the same estimate for the second term on the righthand side of (
B.12
). This gives the second statement of (
2
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.7
.

We recall
\kappa_{t}(x)
from (
4.34
) and its properties from
Lemma

4.5
and
Lemma

4.6
. We will first show the following term (from (
4.41
))

\displaystyle\ln\sin(-\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t))-\ln\sin(\pi\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(x,t)+\pi\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(x,t)),

can be extended to
z\in{\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle\ln\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))-\ln\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))=\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}\,.

(B.13)

Recall that
\kappa_{t}(x)\in[0,1]
(from
Lemma

4.5
) and
\zeta\leqslant\varrho v\leqslant 1-\zeta
, we have that

\displaystyle\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))\asymp\min\{\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x),1-\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x)\}.

Thanks to (
4.37
), we have that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))-\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))|&\lesssim\pi\varrho v|\kappa_{t}(z)-\kappa_{t}(x)|\\
&\lesssim\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|}{\delta}\min\{\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x),\varrho v(1-\kappa_{t}(x))\}\\
&\lesssim\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|}{\delta}\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x)),\end{split}

(B.14)

provided that
\delta\geqslant C\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]
for
C>1
large enough. Similarly, for the second term on the left of (
B.13
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))-\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))|&\lesssim\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|}{\delta}\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))\\
&\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))\,.\end{split}

(B.15)

The two estimates (
B.14
) and (
B.15
) together gives that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}\right|\asymp\left|\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))}\right|.

(B.16)

If
\kappa_{t}(x)\leqslant 1/2
, then

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))}\right|\asymp\frac{\varrho v}{\varrho(1-v)}\in\left[\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta},\frac{1-\zeta}{\zeta}\right].

(B.17)

If
\kappa_{t}(x)\geqslant 1/2
, then using
\zeta\leqslant\varrho v\leqslant 1-\zeta
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\zeta&\lesssim\min\{\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x),1-\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x)\}\lesssim\left|\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))}\right|\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\min\{\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x),1-\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x)\}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\zeta}.\end{split}

(B.18)

We conclude from plugging (
B.16
), (
B.17
) and (
B.18
) into (
B.13
)

\displaystyle|\ln\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))-\ln\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))|\leqslant\ln(1/\zeta)+C.

(B.19)

The above estimate (
B.19
) and the first statement of
Lemma

4.6
together imply that we can extend
f_{t}(z)
to the strip region, and

\displaystyle f_{t}(z)=e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\varrho\kappa_{t}(z)}\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))},\quad|f_{t}(z)|\leqslant\frac{C}{\zeta}.

The estimate (
B.19
) and the second statement of
Lemma

4.6
together imply that we can extend
g_{t}(z)
to the strip region, and

\displaystyle g_{t}(z)=\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}-\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z),\quad|g_{t}(z)|\leqslant\ln(1/\zeta)+\ln(\ell/\delta)+C.

For
\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})\geqslant\ell
, it follows from (
4.37
) and (
4.36
) in
Lemma

4.5

\displaystyle|\kappa_{t}(z)|\lesssim\frac{C\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})+\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})^{2}/\ell}.

Then by Taylor expansion

\displaystyle\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}

\displaystyle=\ln\frac{\sin(v)}{\sin(1-v)}+\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z)}+\ln\frac{\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z)}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}

\displaystyle=\ln\frac{\sin(v)}{\sin(1-v)}+\OO\left(\frac{\delta}{\dist(z,\{tv,\ell+tv\})}\right).

This gives the claim (
4.42
). Next, we estimate
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[g_{t}(z)]
for
z=x+\mathrm{i}\eta
. By taking imaginary part of (
B.13
), and using (
B.14
) and (
B.15
)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))}-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(z))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))}

\displaystyle=\arg\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|}{\delta}\right)\right)-\arg\left(1+\OO\left(\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|}{\delta}\right)\right)=\OO\left(\frac{|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]|}{\delta}\right).

The above estimate together with the second statement (
2
) of
Lemma

4.6
imply that

\displaystyle|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}g_{t}(z)|\leqslant\frac{C\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}[z]}{\delta}.

\Hib(\kappa_{t})(z)
can be computed explicitely, see (
B.12
), and its derivative at
z=x
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}\Hib(\kappa_{t})(x)\right|=\left|\frac{x-\ell-tv}{(x-\ell-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}}-\frac{x-tv}{(x-tv)^{2}+\delta^{2}}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\dist(x,\{tv,\ell+tv\})+\delta}.

(B.20)

Since
g_{t}(z)
is analytic, we have
|\partial_{x}g_{t}(x)|=|\partial_{\eta}g_{t}(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)|_{\eta=0}|
. As a consequence of (
B.14
) and (
B.15
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}\ln\frac{\sin(\pi\varrho v\kappa_{t}(x))}{\sin(\pi\varrho(1-v)\kappa_{t}(x))}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}.

(B.21)

The claim
|\partial_{x}g_{t}(x)|\lesssim 1/\delta
follows from (
B.21
) and (
B.20
).

Recall our construction of
g_{t}
from (
4.41
), since
\widetilde{H}(x-tv,t)
is constant for
0\leqslant t\leqslant\ell
, so is

g_{t}(x-tv)
. By taking derivative with respect to
t
we get

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g_{t}-v\partial_{x}g_{t}=0.

Thus
|\partial_{t}g_{t}|\leqslant v|\partial_{x}g_{t}|\leqslant C/\delta
.
∎

Appendix C
Asymptotics for Macdonald Polynomials

In this section, we explain the correspondence between non-intersecting
\theta
-Bernoulli walk ensembles and certain Macdonald ascending process (
C.4
). This correspondence will be used to derive large deviation asymptotics for (skew) Macdonald polynomials. We collect some basic properties of Macdonald symmetric functions in
Appendix

C
. We recall the Macdonald ascending process in
Section

C.2
. Our main references are
[
47
]
and
[
61
]
. In
Section

C.3
, we prove
Theorem

1.8
.

C.1
Macdonald polynomials and specializations

We use the following notations:

\displaystyle(a;q)_{\infty}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-aq^{i-1}),\quad f(u)=\frac{(tu;q)_{\infty}}{(qu;q)_{\infty}},\quad\Gamma_{q}(x)=(1-q)^{1-x}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{x};q)_{\infty}}.

(C.1)

The next class of Markov chains is built out of the specializations of Macdonald polynomials. We refer to
[
46
, Section VI]
and
[
8
]
for definitions and properties of Macdonald symmetric functions and use them as a black box in this section. Macdonald symmetric functions
P
and
Q
are indexed by
partitions and implicitly depend on two parameters
q,t\in(0,1)
. The coefficients for the symmetric functions are in
\mathbb{Q}[q,t]
. Macdonald symmetric functions
P_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};q,t)
and
Q_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};q,t)
are elements of the algebra
\Lambda
of the symmetric functions in infinitely many variables
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
uniquely determined by the following two properties:

1.

P_{\bm{\lambda}}
,
|\bm{\lambda}|=m
, can be expressed in terms of the monomial symmetric functions via a strictly upper unitriangular transition matrix:

P_{\bm{\lambda}}=m_{\bm{\lambda}}+\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}<{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}_{m}}R_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}P_{{\bm{\mu}}},

where
R_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}
are functions of
q,t
and
{\bm{\mu}}<{\bm{\lambda}}
is comparison in the dominance order on the set
\mathbb{Y}_{m}
of all partitions of
m
(equivalently, Young diagrams with
m
boxes).

2.

They are pairwise orthogonal with respect to the scalar product defined on the power sums via

\langle p_{\bm{\lambda}},p_{\bm{\mu}}\rangle_{q,t}=\delta_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}z_{\bm{\lambda}}(q,t),\qquad p_{\lambda}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}p_{\lambda_{i}},\qquad z_{\bm{\lambda}}(q,t)=\prod_{i\geqslant 1}i^{m_{i}}(m_{i})!\prod_{i=1}^{\ell({\bm{\lambda}})}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}}}{1-t^{\lambda_{i}}},

where
{\bm{\lambda}}=1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\dots
, i.e.
m_{i}
is the multiplicity of
i
in
{\bm{\lambda}}
,
\ell({\bm{\lambda}})
is the number of rows, and
p_{k}=(x_{1})^{k}+(x_{2})^{k}+\dots
,
k\geqslant 1
.

We further define
Q_{\bm{\lambda}}=\frac{P_{\bm{\lambda}}}{\langle P_{\bm{\lambda}},P_{\bm{\lambda}}\rangle_{q,t}}.
Finally, the skew Macdonald polynomials
P_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
and
Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
are defined through the expansions:

\displaystyle P_{\bm{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}}P_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(x_{1},x_{1},\dots;q,t)P_{\bm{\mu}}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t),

\displaystyle Q_{\bm{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(x_{1},x_{1},\dots;q,t)Q_{\bm{\mu}}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t).

C.2
Macdonald ascending processes

Computations in the algebra of symmetric functions
\Lambda
can be converted into numeric identities by means of
specializations
, which are algebra homomorphism from
\Lambda
to the set of complex numbers. Specialization
\rho
is uniquely determined by its values on any set of algebraic generators of
\Lambda
and we use
(p_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
as such generators. The value of
\rho
on a symmetric function
f
is denoted
f(\rho)
.
Given two specializations
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, we define their union
(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
through the formula:

p_{k}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=p_{k}(\rho)+p_{k}(\rho^{\prime}),\quad k\geqslant 1.

A specialization
\rho
is called
Macdonald nonnegative
if its values on all (skew) Macdonald symmetric functions are non-negative, i.e., if for all partitions
\bm{\lambda}
and
\bm{\mu}
,

\displaystyle P_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho;q,t)\geqslant 0,

The description of Macdonald nonnegative specializations was conjectured in
[
44
]
and proven in
[
48
]
:

Theorem C.1

(
[
48
]
)
.

For any fixed
q,t\in(0,1)
, Macdonald nonnegative specializations can be parameterized by triplets
(\bm{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 1},\bm{\beta}=\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 1},\gamma)
of nonnegative numbers satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i})<\infty
. The specialization
\rho
corresponding to a triplet
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\gamma)
is defined by for
k\geqslant 2

p_{1}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}+\frac{1-q}{1-t}\left(\gamma+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\beta_{i}\right),\qquad p_{k}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\alpha_{i})^{k}+(-1)^{k-1}\frac{1-q^{k}}{1-t^{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\beta_{i})^{k}.

Following
[
8
, Section 2.3]
, we create Markov chains out of the Macdonald-positive specializations:

Definition C.2
.

Given two specializations
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
we define the ascending transition through

\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}\mid{\bm{\mu}})=\frac{1}{\Pi(\rho;\rho^{\prime})}\frac{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(\rho;q,t)}{P_{\bm{\mu}}(\rho;q,t)}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho^{\prime};q,t),

(C.2)

where
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
are partitions with
{\bm{\mu}}\subset{\bm{\lambda}}
and
\Pi(\rho;\rho^{\prime})
is the result of applying
\rho
to the
x_{i}
variables and
\rho^{\prime}
to the
y_{j}
variables in the infinite product

\Pi=\prod_{i,j\geqslant 1}\frac{(tx_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}.

To obtain the asymptotics of Skew Macdonald polynomials, we study the following special Macdonald ascending process. We fix
N,{\mathsf{T}}\geqslant 1
, and a sequence of positive numbers
b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1}
. The transition probability at time
0\leqslant t\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}-1
is given in the notations of Theorem
C.1
as

\rho:\,\alpha_{i}=t^{i-1},\,1\leqslant i\leqslant N;\qquad\rho_{\mathsf{t}}:\beta_{1}=b_{\mathsf{t}},

(C.3)

with all other parameters set to
0
. This gives a Markov process on Young diagrams

{\bm{\lambda}}(0),{\bm{\lambda}}(1),\cdots,{\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{T}})

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{t}}+1)={\bm{\lambda}}|{\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{t}})={\bm{\mu}})=\frac{1}{\Pi(\rho,\beta_{1}=b_{t})}\frac{P_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(\rho;q,t)}{P_{{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho;q,t)}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\beta_{1}=b_{t};q,t).

(C.4)

Then the transition probability gives the skew Macdonald polynomials

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}({\mathsf{T}})={\bm{\lambda}}|{\bm{\lambda}}(0)={\bm{\mu}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Pi((\rho,\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1}))}\frac{P_{{\bm{\lambda}}}(\rho;q,t)}{P_{{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho;q,t)}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\bm{\beta}=(b_{0},\cdots,b_{{\mathsf{T}}-1});q,t).

(C.5)

The evaluation of the Macdonald polynomial under the principle specialization
\rho=(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1})
is explicit, see
[
46
, Chapter VI, (6.11)]
:

\displaystyle P_{\bm{\lambda}}(1,t,\dots,t^{N-1};q,t)=t^{\sum_{i=1}^{N}(i-1)\lambda_{i}}\prod_{i<j\leqslant N}\frac{(q^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}}{(q^{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}\frac{(t^{j-i+1};q)_{\infty}}{(t^{j-i};q)_{\infty}},

(C.6)

for
{\bm{\lambda}}=\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\dots\geqslant\lambda_{N}\geqslant 0
;
P_{\bm{\lambda}}(\rho;q,t)=0
if
\lambda_{N+1}>0
. We further use the
w_{u,v}
automorphism of the algebra of symmetric functions
\Lambda
defined on the power sums by:

w_{u,v}(p_{k})=(-1)^{k-1}\frac{1-u^{k}}{1-v^{k}}p_{k}.

As shown in
[
46
, Chapter VI, Section 7]
,
